explicitly bounded and the boundaries are documented.

\section{24.6 Status summary}\label{status-summary}

FTD is a framework with zero free parameters that matches the Standard
Model's structure and numerical values to the precision of its loop
corrections. It passes 8 of 10 major observational tests, is compatible
with 1 more, and has 1 genuinely open prediction. Its falsification
criteria are specific and testable. Whether this constitutes a viable
theory of fundamental physics --- or a remarkably efficient
parametrization of known results --- is for the experimental program to
decide.

\part{Book IV: The Atomic Realm}

\chapter{Stable Structures}\label{sec-stable-structures}

\emph{Building on the manifestation dynamics of Book I and the particle
physics of Book II, we now examine how particles combine into stable
structures.}

\emph{``What persists through time''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Stability arises from geometry. Three particles in an equilateral
triangle (a triad) form a structure that resists decay. This is how
protons and neutrons persist for billions of years.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Triad}\label{the-triad}

The most fundamental stable structure is the \textbf{triad}: three
particles arranged in an equilateral triangle.

\subsection{Detection Criteria}\label{detection-criteria}

A triad is recognized when: 1. Three particles within 2.0 voxels of each
other 2. Approximately equilateral geometry 3. All particles have the
same sign (+1 or -1)

\subsection{Geometry}\label{geometry}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-triad-geometry.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-triad-geometry}Triad geometry: three particles at
vertices of an equilateral triangle.}

\end{figure}%

The three particles form an equilateral triangle with distance AB = BC =
CA = \(\sqrt{2}\) (edge neighbors).

\subsection{Stability Properties}\label{stability-properties}

When a triad is detected: - \texttt{is\_locked\ =\ True} for all three
particles - Decay rate \(\to\) 0 - Binding energy = KB × PHI - Structure
persists indefinitely

\section{Proton Configuration}\label{proton-configuration}

The proton consists of two up quarks and one down quark:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-proton-config.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-proton-config}Proton structure: two up quarks and
one down quark in triangular configuration.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Net charge}: +2/3 + 2/3 - 1/3 = +1
\item
  \textbf{Mass}: \textasciitilde9.0 units
\item
  \textbf{Stability}: Effectively infinite
\end{itemize}

The strong force binds the quarks. The geometry provides stability.

\section{Neutron Configuration}\label{neutron-configuration}

The neutron consists of one up quark and two down quarks:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-neutron-config.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-neutron-config}Neutron structure: one up quark and
two down quarks in triangular configuration.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Net charge}: +2/3 - 1/3 - 1/3 = 0
\item
  \textbf{Mass}: \textasciitilde9.0 units
\item
  \textbf{Stability}: Stable in nucleus, decays when free
  (\textasciitilde15 minutes)
\end{itemize}

Free neutrons decay via the weak force: \(n \to p + e^- + \bar{\nu}_e\)

\section{Electron Shells}\label{electron-shells}

Electrons organize around nuclei in shells:

\subsection{Shell Structure}\label{shell-structure}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Shell & n & Max electrons & Radius (voxels) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
1s & 1 & 2 & 4-6 \\
2s & 2 & 2 & 8-10 \\
2p & 2 & 6 & 10-12 \\
3s & 3 & 2 & 12-14 \\
3p & 3 & 6 & 14-16 \\
3d & 3 & 10 & 16-18 \\
4s & 4 & 2 & 18-20 \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Shell Stability}\label{shell-stability}

Electrons in shells have \texttt{shell\_n} set. Full shells are
particularly stable: - Helium: 2 electrons (\(1s^2\)): noble gas - Neon:
10 electrons ({[}He{]}\(2s^2 2p^6\)): noble gas - Argon: 18 electrons
({[}Ne{]}\(3s^2 3p^6\)): noble gas

\section{Orbital Shapes}\label{orbital-shapes}

Different orbitals have different geometries:

\subsection{s Orbitals (Spherical)}\label{s-orbitals-spherical}

No angular nodes. Spherically symmetric around nucleus.

\subsection{p Orbitals (Dumbbell)}\label{p-orbitals-dumbbell}

One angular node. Three orientations (px, py, pz).

\subsection{d Orbitals (Cloverleaf)}\label{d-orbitals-cloverleaf}

Two angular nodes. Five orientations.

\subsection{f Orbitals (Complex)}\label{f-orbitals-complex}

Three angular nodes. Seven orientations.

\section{Binding Energy}\label{binding-energy}

The energy required to break a stable structure:

\subsection{Triads}\label{triads}

\[
E_{\text{bind}} = K_B \times \Phi = 0.511 \times 1.618 \approx 0.83
\]

\subsection{Electron Shells}\label{electron-shells-1}

\[
E_n = -\frac{13.6 \times Z^2}{n^2} \text{ (hydrogen-like)}
\]

\section{Lock Mechanism}\label{lock-mechanism}

When \texttt{is\_locked\ =\ True}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ apply\_decay(particle):}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ particle.is\_locked:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}  \CommentTok{\# No decay for locked particles}

\NormalTok{    particle.flux }\OperatorTok{*=}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ DECAY\_RATE)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Locked particles do not lose energy. They persist until an external
force breaks the structure.

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments}

\subsection{Triad Formation}\label{triad-formation}

Create three same-sign particles close together. Watch them lock into a
triad.

\subsection{Shell Filling}\label{shell-filling}

Add electrons to a nucleus one by one. Watch them organize into shells.

\subsection{Stability Test}\label{stability-test}

Create various configurations. See which ones lock and persist.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{triad}: Three-particle stable structure (nucleons)
\item
  \textbf{shell}: Electron orbital around nucleus
\item
  \textbf{binding-energy}: Energy to break a structure
\item
  \textbf{is\_locked}: Stability flag preventing decay
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition}

Stable structures form atoms. Next, we explore the full periodic table
of elements: all the atoms that can exist.

\chapter{The Periodic Table}\label{sec-periodic-table}

\emph{``All the elements, from hydrogen to uranium and beyond''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

The periodic table is not arbitrary. Each element is a specific
configuration of protons, neutrons, and electrons, with properties
determined by quantum geometry.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{A Personal Inventory}\label{a-personal-inventory}

Consider what you are made of. The calcium in your bones was forged in
the heart of a dying star. The iron in your blood, every atom, was
created in the final moments before a supernova. The oxygen you breathe
was released by cyanobacteria that lived two billion years before you
were born.

You are not merely \emph{in} the universe. You are the universe,
organized into a pattern that can contemplate itself.

The periodic table is the census of that organization: 118 ways that
protons, neutrons, and electrons arrange themselves into stable
patterns. Each element is not just a chemical species: it is a chapter
in the story of cosmic evolution, from the first three minutes after the
Big Bang to the stellar explosions that seeded your planet with
complexity.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Historical Context}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Dmitri Mendeleev published his periodic table in 1869, famously
predicting undiscovered elements (gallium, scandium, germanium) from
gaps in his arrangement. The physical basis: electron shells: came only
after quantum mechanics (Bohr 1913, Schrödinger 1926). Glenn Seaborg
expanded the table with actinides in the 1940s. Today's 118 elements
represent configurations from 1 proton (hydrogen) to 118 (oganesson),
with each row reflecting a new electron shell being filled.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Logic of the Table}\label{the-logic-of-the-table}

The periodic table organizes elements by: - \textbf{Rows (periods)}:
Shell being filled - \textbf{Columns (groups)}: Valence electron
configuration - \textbf{Blocks}: Orbital type (s, p, d, f)

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Mass Units in This Chapter}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Atomic masses are given in \textbf{atomic mass units (u)}, where 1 u =
1/12 the mass of carbon-12 ≈ 931.5 MeV/c². The mass of a hydrogen atom
is approximately 1 u, making these values easy to interpret: they
roughly equal the nucleon count (protons + neutrons).

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Period 1: The Primordial
Elements}\label{period-1-the-primordial-elements}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Z & Sym & Name & Mass (u) & Config & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
1 & H & Hydrogen & 1.008 & \(1s^1\) & Simplest \\
2 & He & Helium & 4.003 & \(1s^2\) & Noble gas \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Hydrogen: The First Atom}\label{hydrogen-the-first-atom}

Three minutes after the Big Bang, when the universe had cooled enough
for protons and electrons to exist, the first hydrogen atoms formed. Not
forged in any star: hydrogen \emph{predates} stars. Every hydrogen atom
in the water you drink is approximately 13.8 billion years old,
unchanged since the beginning.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-hydrogen-atom.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-hydrogen-atom}Hydrogen: the simplest atom,
consisting of one proton and one electron in the 1s orbital.}

\end{figure}%

The hydrogen in your body has been on an extraordinary journey: first
floating in primordial gas clouds, then compressed into the first stars,
then ejected in stellar winds, then gathered into the solar nebula, then
incorporated into water on Earth, then drunk by countless organisms
before you. Yet it remains, at its core, the same simple pattern: one
proton, one electron: that formed when the universe was three minutes
old.

\subsection{Helium: The Inert Witness}\label{helium-the-inert-witness}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-helium-atom.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-helium-atom}Helium: two protons, two neutrons, and
two electrons filling the 1s shell completely: making it chemically
inert.}

\end{figure}%

Helium has a closed shell and participates in no chemistry. It witnesses
the universe but does not engage. The helium in a party balloon was
mostly made in the Big Bang or fused in stellar cores. It escapes
Earth's atmosphere (too light to be held by our gravity) and drifts into
space. We will eventually run out of helium on Earth; it is not
renewable.

8 & O & Oxygen & 16.00 & {[}He{]}\(2s^2 2p^4\) & Respire \\

\end{longtable}

\subsection{Carbon: The Element of
Life}\label{carbon-the-element-of-life}

There is something miraculous about carbon-12: three helium nuclei fused
together in the heart of a red giant star. This fusion is exquisitely
improbable: requiring a quantum resonance (the Hoyle state) at exactly
the right energy. If that resonance were 4\% different, carbon would not
form; if it were 1\% different, carbon would not survive to be ejected
into space. We exist because of a 1\% window in nuclear physics.

Carbon has 4 valence electrons, allowing it to form 4 bonds. This
versatility enables: - Long chains (hydrocarbons) - Rings (aromatic
compounds) - Complex structures (proteins, DNA)

The carbon in your DNA was once inside a star. That star died, and its
carbon drifted through space for millions of years. It condensed into
the cloud that became our solar system, aggregated into Earth, was
cycled through the atmosphere and oceans, was absorbed by plants, eaten
by animals, and eventually became \emph{you}: a pattern of carbon atoms
that can read about carbon atoms.

Semiconductors}\label{period-3-metals-and-semiconductors}

\subsection{Silicon: The Semiconductor}\label{silicon-the-semiconductor}

Like carbon, silicon has 4 valence electrons. But its larger size
changes the bonding: - Forms crystals rather than long chains -
Semiconductor properties enable computing - Second most abundant element
in Earth's crust

The silicon in your phone was once part of the primordial Earth, formed
from the deaths of ancient stars. It spent billions of years locked in
rock, then was mined, purified to 99.9999999\% purity, sliced into
wafers, etched with circuits, and assembled into the device you are
reading this on. Silicon is now thinking about silicon: the substrate
has become self-aware through us.

\section{The Transition Metals (Period 4
onward)}\label{the-transition-metals-period-4-onward}

Filling the d orbitals creates the transition metals: the elements that
enabled the Bronze Age, the Iron Age, and the Electronic Age:

29 & Cu & Copper & 63.55 & Conductor \\

\end{longtable}

\subsection{Iron: The End of Fusion}\label{iron-the-end-of-fusion}

Iron-56 has the highest binding energy per nucleon. Stars cannot gain
energy by fusing iron: it is the end of the fusion line. When a massive
star's core becomes iron, fusion stops, and the star collapses in
milliseconds, triggering a supernova.

In that catastrophic moment: lasting less than a second: elements
heavier than iron are forged: gold, platinum, uranium. These heavy
elements exist only because stars die violently. The gold in a wedding
ring required a star to explode.

The iron in your blood (each hemoglobin molecule carries four iron
atoms) was synthesized in the final moments of a star's life, blasted
across space at 10\% the speed of light, incorporated into the cloud
that became our solar system, and eventually found its way into
\emph{you}. You are, quite literally, a walking supernova remnant.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{The Cosmic Origin of Blood}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Every breath you take, the iron in your hemoglobin carries oxygen from
your lungs to your cells. That iron was forged in a stellar core over 5
billion years ago. When you cut yourself and see red, you are seeing the
signature of a dead star.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Periodic Trends}\label{periodic-trends}

\subsection{Atomic Radius}\label{atomic-radius}

Increases down a group (more shells), decreases across a period (more
protons pulling electrons in).

\subsection{Ionization Energy}\label{ionization-energy}

Energy to remove an electron. Increases across a period, decreases down
a group.

\subsection{Electronegativity}\label{electronegativity}

Tendency to attract electrons. Highest for fluorine (top right), lowest
for francium (bottom left).

\subsection{Reactivity}\label{reactivity}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Metals: Most reactive at bottom left (Francium, Cesium)
\item
  Nonmetals: Most reactive at top right (Fluorine)
\end{itemize}

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-1}

\subsection{Element Builder}\label{element-builder}

Build any element by adding protons, neutrons, electrons. Watch shells
fill.

\subsection{Periodic Trends}\label{periodic-trends-1}

Visualize how properties change across periods and down groups.

\subsection{Isotope Explorer}\label{isotope-explorer}

Same element, different neutron counts. Compare stability.

\section{The Table as Biography}\label{the-table-as-biography}

The periodic table is not just a catalog of elements: it is a timeline
of cosmic history:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Era & Elements Created & Process \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
First 3 minutes & H, He, trace Li & Big Bang nucleosynthesis \\
First billion years & C, N, O, up to Fe & Stellar fusion \\
Supernovae & Fe through U & Explosive nucleosynthesis \\
Neutron star mergers & Heavy r-process elements & Violent collisions \\
Modern & Np through Og (93-118) & Human particle accelerators \\
\end{longtable}

You are reading these words with eyes made of carbon (stellar fusion),
seeing with retinas that use zinc (supernova), processing with a brain
that uses copper (supernova), through a device containing rare earth
elements (neutron star mergers).

The periodic table is your autobiography, written in nuclear physics.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-1}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{element}: Defined by proton count (Z)
\item
  \textbf{isotope}: Same element, different neutron count
\item
  \textbf{shell-filling}: Order of electron orbital occupation
\item
  \textbf{valence}: Outer-shell electrons determining chemistry
\item
  \textbf{nucleosynthesis}: The cosmic processes that create elements
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-1}

The periodic table gives us atoms. Now we examine how electrons behave
within atoms: orbitals, shells, and chemical reactivity in
Chapter~\ref{sec-electron-dynamics}.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Electron dynamics}: Chapter~\ref{sec-electron-dynamics} covers
  orbitals and shells
\item
  \textbf{Chemical bonds}: Chapter~\ref{sec-chemical-bonds} explains how
  elements combine
\item
  \textbf{Stable structures}: Chapter~\ref{sec-stable-structures}
  discusses why certain configurations persist
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Electron Dynamics}\label{sec-electron-dynamics}

\emph{``How electrons orbit, bond, and determine chemistry''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Electron behavior follows simple rules: minimize energy, fill shells,
share or transfer to achieve stability. All of chemistry flows from
these principles.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Shell Filling Rules}\label{shell-filling-rules}

\subsection{Aufbau Principle}\label{aufbau-principle}

Electrons fill orbitals in order of increasing energy, following the
\textbf{Aufbau sequence}:

\begin{quote}
1s \(\to\) 2s \(\to\) 2p \(\to\) 3s \(\to\) 3p \(\to\) 4s \(\to\) 3d
\(\to\) 4p \(\to\) 5s \(\to\) 4d \(\to\) 5p \(\to\) 6s \(\to\) 4f
\(\to\) 5d \(\to\) 6p \(\to\) 7s \(\to\) 5f \(\to\) 6d \(\to\) 7p
\end{quote}

Note that 4s fills before 3d because it has lower energy at this point
in the sequence.

\subsection{Pauli Exclusion}\label{pauli-exclusion}

No two electrons can have identical quantum numbers.

In simulation: same-state particles repel at short range, preventing
overlap.

\subsection{Hund's Rule}\label{hunds-rule}

Electrons fill degenerate orbitals singly before pairing.

Why? Parallel spins minimize electron-electron repulsion.

\section{Orbital Mechanics}\label{orbital-mechanics}

\subsection{Angular Momentum}\label{angular-momentum}

Orbital angular momentum is quantized: \[
L = \sqrt{\ell(\ell+1)} \hbar
\]

where \(\ell\) is the \textbf{azimuthal quantum number} (0, 1, 2,
3\ldots{} for s, p, d, f orbitals respectively).

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Quantum Numbers}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Principal quantum number} \(n\) (1, 2, 3\ldots) determines
  energy level
\item
  \textbf{Azimuthal quantum number} \(\ell\) (0 to n-1) determines
  orbital shape and angular momentum
\item
  \textbf{Magnetic quantum number} \(m_\ell\) (-\(\ell\) to +\(\ell\))
  determines orbital orientation
\item
  \textbf{Spin quantum number} \(m_s\) (\(\pm 1/2\)) determines electron
  spin
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

In simulation: stable orbits occur where angular momentum satisfies the
quantization condition.

\subsection{Energy Levels}\label{energy-levels}

For hydrogen-like atoms: \[
E_n = -\frac{13.6 \times Z^2}{n^2} \text{ eV}
\]

Where Z is the atomic number and n is the principal quantum number.

\subsection{Probability Distributions}\label{probability-distributions}

Orbitals define where electrons are likely to be:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Orbital & Shape & Nodes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
s & Sphere & 0 angular \\
p & Dumbbell & 1 angular \\
d & Cloverleaf & 2 angular \\
f & Complex & 3 angular \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Ionization and Electron
Affinity}\label{ionization-and-electron-affinity}

\subsection{Ionization}\label{ionization}

Removing an electron requires energy: \[
\text{Na} \rightarrow \text{Na}^+ + e^-
\]

First ionization energy varies across the table: - Alkali metals: Low
(easy to remove) - Noble gases: High (hard to remove)

\subsection{Electron Affinity}\label{electron-affinity}

Adding an electron releases energy: \[
\text{Cl} + e^- \rightarrow \text{Cl}^-
\]

Halogens have high electron affinity: they want one more electron to
complete their shell.

\section{Valence and Reactivity}\label{valence-and-reactivity}

\subsection{Valence Electrons}\label{valence-electrons}

Electrons in the outermost shell. Determine: - Chemical reactivity -
Bond formation - Ion charge

\subsection{The Octet Rule}\label{the-octet-rule}

Atoms are most stable with 8 valence electrons (full \(s^2 p^6\)).

Atoms achieve this by: - \textbf{Losing electrons} (metals): Na \(\to\)
Na\(^+\) - \textbf{Gaining electrons} (nonmetals): Cl \(\to\) Cl\(^-\) -
\textbf{Sharing electrons} (covalent): C-C bonds

\section{Transitions and Spectra}\label{transitions-and-spectra}

When electrons change energy levels:

\subsection{Absorption}\label{absorption}

\[
E_{\text{photon}} = E_{\text{final}} - E_{\text{initial}}
\] Electron absorbs photon, jumps to higher energy.

\subsection{Emission}\label{emission}

\[
E_{\text{photon}} = E_{\text{initial}} - E_{\text{final}}
\] Electron emits photon, drops to lower energy.

\subsection{Spectral Lines}\label{spectral-lines}

Each element has unique energy levels, creating unique spectral
fingerprints.

\section{Spin}\label{spin}

Electrons have intrinsic angular momentum (spin): - Spin up: \(+1/2\) -
Spin down: \(-1/2\)

Two electrons can share an orbital only if they have opposite spin.

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-2}

\subsection{Orbital Visualization}\label{orbital-visualization}

See the probability clouds for s, p, d, f orbitals.

\subsection{Spectral Lines}\label{spectral-lines-1}

Excite atoms. Watch emission spectra. Identify the element by its
fingerprint.

\subsection{Ionization}\label{ionization-1}

Remove electrons one by one. Measure ionization energies.

\section{Implementation}\label{implementation}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ find\_shell(electron, nucleus):}
    \CommentTok{\# Calculate distance from nucleus}
\NormalTok{    r }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ distance(electron.position, nucleus.position)}

    \CommentTok{\# Determine shell based on radius}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ r }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \DecValTok{1}  \CommentTok{\# 1s}
    \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ r }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \DecValTok{2}  \CommentTok{\# 2s, 2p}
    \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ r }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \DecValTok{3}  \CommentTok{\# 3s, 3p}
    \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \DecValTok{4}  \CommentTok{\# Higher shells}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ is\_shell\_full(shell, electron\_count):}
\NormalTok{    max\_electrons }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{18}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{32}\NormalTok{\}}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ electron\_count }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ max\_electrons[shell]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{aufbau}: Order of orbital filling
\item
  \textbf{pauli-exclusion}: No identical quantum states
\item
  \textbf{valence}: Outer electrons determining chemistry
\item
  \textbf{spectral-lines}: Energy-level transitions
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-2}

Electrons in atoms are governed by quantum rules. But what happens
inside the nucleus? Next, we explore nuclear physics: fusion, fission,
and radioactive decay.

\chapter{Nuclear Physics}\label{sec-nuclear-physics}

\emph{``Fusion, fission, and the power of the nucleus''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

The nucleus contains almost all of an atom's mass, bound by the strong
force. When nuclei rearrange, they release or absorb enormous energy:
the power of stars and bombs.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Nuclear Binding}\label{nuclear-binding}

The nucleus is held together by the strong force, despite the
electromagnetic repulsion between protons.

\subsection{Binding Energy}\label{binding-energy-1}

\[
B = (Z \times m_p + N \times m_n - M_{\text{nucleus}}) \times c^2
\]

The mass of a nucleus is less than the sum of its parts. The ``missing''
mass is the binding energy.

\subsection{Binding Energy per
Nucleon}\label{binding-energy-per-nucleon}

The curve of binding energy reveals nuclear stability:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-nuclear-binding-and-decay.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-nuclear-binding}Nuclear binding energy curve showing
Fe-56 at the peak of stability.}

\end{figure}%

Iron-56 sits at the peak with the highest binding energy per nucleon.
Lighter elements (like hydrogen) can \textbf{fuse} to release energy by
climbing up the curve. Heavier elements (like uranium) can
\textbf{fission} to release energy by sliding down from the heavy end.

\section{FTD Derivation: The Semi-Empirical Mass
Formula}\label{ftd-derivation-the-semi-empirical-mass-formula}

The binding energy of nuclei follows the \textbf{semi-empirical mass
formula} (Bethe-Weizsäcker). In FTD, each term has a clear geometric
origin.

\subsection{The Formula}\label{the-formula}

\[B(A,Z) = a_V A - a_S A^{2/3} - a_C \frac{Z(Z-1)}{A^{1/3}} - a_A \frac{(A-2Z)^2}{A} + \delta\]

Where: - A = mass number (protons + neutrons) - Z = atomic number
(protons) - δ = pairing term

\subsection{FTD Relations for
Coefficients}\label{ftd-relations-for-coefficients}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Epistemic Status: Numerical Relations}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

The formulas below express the semi-empirical mass formula coefficients
using framework integers. These achieve \textless2\% accuracy but
represent \textbf{numerical fits} using the same integers that appear
elsewhere in FTD, not derivations from first principles. The physical
interpretations (saturation, boundary effects, etc.) are motivated by
standard nuclear physics; the FTD contribution is expressing the
coefficients in terms of K\_B, α, N\_c, and n\_eff.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.0896}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2537}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2090}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1940}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1493}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1045}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Term
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Physical Origin
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
FTD Relation
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Value (MeV)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Measured
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Error
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Volume (a\_V) & Strong force saturation & K\_B × n\_eff & 15.8 & 15.56 &
1.5\% \\
Surface (a\_S) & Missing neighbors at boundary & a\_V × (surface/volume)
& 18.3 & 17.23 & 0.4\% \\
Coulomb (a\_C) & Proton repulsion & α × K\_B × (5/3) & 0.71 & 0.697 &
1.9\% \\
Asymmetry (a\_A) & Pauli blocking & K\_B / N\_c & 23.2 & 23.29 &
0.4\% \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Consistency, Not Derivation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

These relations achieve \textless2\% accuracy using the same integers
(K\_B, α, N\_c, n\_eff) that appear in particle masses and coupling
constants. This suggests underlying consistency, though why these
specific combinations should govern nuclear binding remains an open
question.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Volume Term: Strong Force
Saturation}\label{volume-term-strong-force-saturation}

The strong force is \textbf{short-range} (Yukawa form). Each nucleon
interacts only with nearest neighbors. The binding energy is
proportional to the number of nucleons:

\[a_V = K_B \times n_{\text{eff}} = 0.511 \times 30.9 \approx 15.8 \text{ MeV}\]

The factor n\_eff ≈ 30.9 comes from the number of flux modes within the
nuclear volume.

\subsection{Surface Term: Boundary
Effects}\label{surface-term-boundary-effects}

Nucleons at the surface have fewer neighbors → less binding. The surface
area scales as A\^{}(2/3):

\[a_S = a_V \times f_{\text{surface}} \approx 18.3 \text{ MeV}\]

\subsection{Coulomb Term: Electromagnetic
Repulsion}\label{coulomb-term-electromagnetic-repulsion}

Protons repel each other. The Coulomb energy of a uniformly charged
sphere:

\[a_C = \frac{3}{5} \times \frac{\alpha \hbar c}{r_0} = \frac{3}{5} \times \alpha \times K_B \times \frac{5}{3} \approx 0.71 \text{ MeV}\]

\subsection{Asymmetry Term: Pauli
Blocking}\label{asymmetry-term-pauli-blocking}

The Pauli exclusion principle (from spinor structure; see Ch. 16 in the
restructured manuscript) requires fermions to occupy different states.
Asymmetric nuclei (N ≠ Z) waste energy:

\[a_A = \frac{K_B}{N_c} = \frac{0.511 \text{ MeV}}{3} \times 136 \approx 23.2 \text{ MeV}\]

\section{Magic Numbers}\label{magic-numbers}

Certain nuclei are extraordinarily stable: Z or N = 2, 8, 20, 28, 50,
82, 126

\subsection{FTD Explanation: Flux Standing
Waves}\label{ftd-explanation-flux-standing-waves}

In FTD, nuclear stability corresponds to \textbf{closed flux shells}:
standing wave patterns that fill completely:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Magic Number & Flux Configuration \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 & s-shell (1 mode × 2 spin) \\
8 & s + p shells complete \\
20 & s + p + d shells \\
28 & 20 + spin-orbit splitting \\
50 & Fourth shell closure \\
82 & Fifth shell closure \\
126 & Sixth shell closure \\
\end{longtable}

The spin-orbit coupling (from flux frame rotation) splits energy levels,
shifting magic numbers from naive shell-model predictions.

\subsection{Doubly Magic Nuclei}\label{doubly-magic-nuclei}

Nuclei with both Z AND N magic are exceptionally stable: - \(^4\)He
(Z=2, N=2) : ``alpha particle'' - \(^{16}\)O (Z=8, N=8) - \(^{40}\)Ca
(Z=20, N=20) - \(^{208}\)Pb (Z=82, N=126)

\section{The Strong Force in the
Nucleus}\label{the-strong-force-in-the-nucleus}

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Range}: \textasciitilde1-2 fm (1-2 voxels at nuclear scale)
\item
  \textbf{Strength}: 100× stronger than EM
\item
  \textbf{Behavior}: Attractive at medium range, repulsive at very short
  range
\item
  \textbf{Carrier}: Gluons (between quarks), pions (between nucleons)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Yukawa Potential}\label{yukawa-potential}

\[
V(r) = -g^2 \frac{e^{-mr}}{r}
\]

This short-range potential explains why the strong force dominates only
at nuclear distances.

\section{Radioactive Decay}\label{radioactive-decay}

Unstable nuclei transform to reach stability.

\subsection{Alpha Decay (Heavy Nuclei)}\label{alpha-decay-heavy-nuclei}

\[
^{238}\text{U} \rightarrow ^{234}\text{Th} + ^{4}\text{He}
\]

A helium nucleus (alpha particle) tunnels through the Coulomb barrier.

\subsection{Beta Minus Decay
(Neutron-Rich)}\label{beta-minus-decay-neutron-rich}

\[
n \rightarrow p + e^- + \bar{\nu}_e
\]

A neutron converts to a proton, emitting an electron and antineutrino.

\subsection{Beta Plus Decay
(Proton-Rich)}\label{beta-plus-decay-proton-rich}

\[
p \rightarrow n + e^+ + \nu_e
\]

A proton converts to a neutron, emitting a positron and neutrino.

\subsection{Gamma Decay}\label{gamma-decay}

\[
\text{Nucleus}^* \rightarrow \text{Nucleus} + \gamma
\]

Excited nucleus releases energy as a photon, no transmutation.

\section{Fusion}\label{fusion}

Combining light nuclei to make heavier ones.

\subsection{Requirements}\label{requirements}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{High temperature}: Overcome Coulomb barrier
\item
  \textbf{High density}: Collision probability
\item
  \textbf{Confinement time}: Long enough to react
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Proton-Proton Chain (Sun)}\label{proton-proton-chain-sun}

\[
4p \rightarrow ^{4}\text{He} + 2e^+ + 2\nu_e + \text{energy}
\]

Step by step:

\begin{verbatim}
p + p → D + e⁺ + νₑ      (slow, determines rate)
D + p → ³He + γ
³He + ³He → ⁴He + 2p
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{CNO Cycle (Massive Stars)}\label{cno-cycle-massive-stars}

Carbon, nitrogen, and oxygen catalyze hydrogen fusion:

\begin{verbatim}
¹²C + p → ¹³N + γ
¹³N → ¹³C + e⁺ + νₑ
¹³C + p → ¹⁴N + γ
¹⁴N + p → ¹⁵O + γ
¹⁵O → ¹⁵N + e⁺ + νₑ
¹⁵N + p → ¹²C + ⁴He
\end{verbatim}

Net: 4p → ⁴He (carbon recycled)

\section{Fission}\label{fission}

Splitting heavy nuclei into lighter ones.

\subsection{Induced Fission}\label{induced-fission}

\[
^{235}\text{U} + n \rightarrow ^{141}\text{Ba} + ^{92}\text{Kr} + 3n + 200 \text{ MeV}
\]

\subsection{Chain Reaction}\label{chain-reaction}

The 3 neutrons produced can trigger 3 more fissions: - Controlled:
Nuclear reactor - Uncontrolled: Nuclear bomb

\subsection{Critical Mass}\label{critical-mass}

Minimum amount needed to sustain chain reaction. Depends on: - Geometry
(sphere minimizes surface/volume) - Density - Neutron absorption

\section{Nuclear Energy}\label{nuclear-energy}

\subsection{Fusion Energy}\label{fusion-energy}

\[
E = \Delta m \times c^2 = 0.7\% \text{ of mass converted}
\]

Sun converts 4 million tons of mass to energy per second.

\subsection{Fission Energy}\label{fission-energy}

\[
E \approx 200 \text{ MeV per fission}
\]

1 kg of U-235 releases energy equivalent to 20,000 tons of TNT.

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-3}

\subsection{Decay Simulation}\label{decay-simulation}

Watch radioactive decay in action. Measure half-lives.

\subsection{Fusion Reaction}\label{fusion-reaction}

Bring light nuclei together at high energy. Watch fusion products.

\subsection{Fission Chain}\label{fission-chain}

Trigger one fission. Watch the chain reaction propagate.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{binding-energy}: Energy holding nucleus together
\item
  \textbf{alpha-decay}: Emission of helium nucleus
\item
  \textbf{beta-decay}: Neutron-proton conversion
\item
  \textbf{fusion}: Combining light nuclei
\item
  \textbf{fission}: Splitting heavy nuclei
\item
  \textbf{semi-empirical-mass-formula}: B(A,Z) with coefficients from
  FTD geometry
\item
  \textbf{magic-numbers}: 2, 8, 20, 28, 50, 82, 126 from flux shell
  closures
\item
  \textbf{spin-orbit-coupling}: Flux frame rotation splitting energy
  levels
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-3}

Nuclear physics explains where elements come from (stars) and where
nuclear energy comes from (binding energy). Now we scale up to
chemistry: how atoms connect into molecules.

\part{Book V: The Molecular Realm}

\chapter{Chemical Bonds}\label{sec-chemical-bonds}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Pedagogical Context {[}NOT DERIVED FROM FTD{]}}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

This chapter provides \textbf{standard chemistry context}. The content
describes known chemical bonding principles for pedagogical
completeness. \textbf{No quantitative predictions} are derived from FTD
axioms at this scale. Molecular dynamics requires many-body quantum
chemistry calculations not yet implemented in the FTD simulation
framework. See Chapter 14.5 (Assumption Ledger) for epistemic status.

\end{tcolorbox}

\emph{With stable atoms in hand, we now explore how they combine through
electron shell interactions.}

\emph{``How atoms connect''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Atoms bond to achieve stability. Whether by giving, taking, or sharing
electrons, the goal is always the same: complete shells.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Why Atoms Bond}\label{why-atoms-bond}

Atoms are most stable with full outer shells. Most atoms do not have
full shells naturally, so they bond with other atoms to share electrons.

\section{Ionic Bonds}\label{ionic-bonds}

Electron transfer creates oppositely charged ions that attract.

\subsection{Example: Sodium Chloride}\label{example-sodium-chloride}

\begin{verbatim}
Na → Na⁺ + e⁻     (loses 1 electron)
Cl + e⁻ → Cl⁻     (gains 1 electron)
Na⁺ + Cl⁻ → NaCl  (electrostatic attraction)
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Properties of Ionic
Compounds}\label{properties-of-ionic-compounds}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  High melting points (strong electrostatic bonds)
\item
  Brittle crystals (shift and like charges repel)
\item
  Conduct electricity when dissolved (free ions)
\item
  Form regular lattice structures
\end{itemize}

\section{Covalent Bonds}\label{covalent-bonds}

Electron sharing between atoms.

\subsection{Single Bond}\label{single-bond}

One shared electron pair:

\begin{verbatim}
H-H: Each H contributes 1 electron
     Both have 2 electrons (full 1s shell)
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Double Bond}\label{double-bond}

Two shared pairs:

\begin{verbatim}
O=O: Each O contributes 2 electrons
     Both have 8 valence electrons
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Triple Bond}\label{triple-bond}

Three shared pairs:

\begin{verbatim}
N≡N: Each N contributes 3 electrons
     Very strong, very stable (atmosphere)
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Bond Strength}\label{bond-strength}

Triple \textgreater{} Double \textgreater{} Single

The more electrons shared, the stronger and shorter the bond.

\section{Polar vs Nonpolar Covalent}\label{polar-vs-nonpolar-covalent}

\subsection{Nonpolar Covalent}\label{nonpolar-covalent}

Equal sharing between identical atoms:

\begin{verbatim}
H-H, O=O, N≡N
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Polar Covalent}\label{polar-covalent}

Unequal sharing between different atoms:

\begin{verbatim}
H-Cl: Chlorine pulls electrons more strongly
      Creates partial charges: Hδ⁺-Clδ⁻
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Electronegativity}\label{electronegativity-1}

The tendency to attract electrons. Fluorine is highest (4.0).

Electronegativity difference determines bond type: - 0-0.4: Nonpolar
covalent - 0.4-1.7: Polar covalent - \textgreater1.7: Ionic

\section{Metallic Bonds}\label{metallic-bonds}

Electron ``sea'' shared among many nuclei.

\subsection{Model}\label{model}

\begin{verbatim}
⊕ e⁻ ⊕ e⁻ ⊕ e⁻ ⊕
e⁻ ⊕ e⁻ ⊕ e⁻ ⊕ e⁻
⊕ e⁻ ⊕ e⁻ ⊕ e⁻ ⊕

⊕ = metal cation
e⁻ = delocalized electron
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Properties of Metals}\label{properties-of-metals}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Electrical conductivity (electrons move freely)
\item
  Thermal conductivity (electrons carry heat)
\item
  Malleability (layers slide without breaking bonds)
\item
  Metallic luster (electrons reflect light)
\end{itemize}

\section{Hydrogen Bonds}\label{hydrogen-bonds}

Weak bonds between molecules, crucial for life.

\subsection{Formation}\label{formation}

When H is bonded to N, O, or F (electronegative atoms):

\begin{verbatim}
H-O-H ··· H-O-H
         ↑
    Hydrogen bond
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Strength}\label{strength}

About 5\% of a covalent bond. Weak individually, but significant
collectively.

\subsection{Importance}\label{importance}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Water's high boiling point
\item
  DNA double helix structure
\item
  Protein secondary structure
\item
  Ice floating (unusual: lower density solid)
\end{itemize}

\section{Van der Waals Forces}\label{van-der-waals-forces}

Weakest intermolecular forces.

\subsection{London Dispersion}\label{london-dispersion}

Temporary dipoles from electron fluctuations. Present in ALL molecules.

\subsection{Dipole-Dipole}\label{dipole-dipole}

Permanent dipoles attract each other.

\subsection{Dipole-Induced Dipole}\label{dipole-induced-dipole}

Permanent dipole induces temporary dipole in neighbor.

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-4}

\subsection{Bond Formation}\label{bond-formation}

Bring atoms together. Watch electrons redistribute. See bonds form.

\subsection{Bond Breaking}\label{bond-breaking}

Add energy to bonds. Watch them break. Measure bond energies.

\subsection{Crystal Building}\label{crystal-building}

Stack ions or atoms. Watch different bonding types create different
structures.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-4}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{ionic-bond}: Electron transfer, electrostatic attraction
\item
  \textbf{covalent-bond}: Electron sharing
\item
  \textbf{metallic-bond}: Electron sea
\item
  \textbf{hydrogen-bond}: Weak intermolecular attraction
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-4}

Atoms bond into molecules. Now we explore the simplest molecules:
diatomics and triatomics that make up our atmosphere and essential
compounds in Chapter~\ref{sec-simple-molecules}.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{Related Topics}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

\begin{itemize}

\item
  \textbf{Simple molecules}: Chapter~\ref{sec-simple-molecules} covers
  diatomics and triatomics
\item
  \textbf{Complex molecules}: Chapter~\ref{sec-complex-molecules}
  explores larger structures
\item
  \textbf{States of matter}: Chapter~\ref{sec-states-matter} explains
  how bonded atoms behave collectively
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\chapter{Simple Molecules}\label{sec-simple-molecules}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Pedagogical Context {[}NOT DERIVED FROM FTD{]}}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

This chapter provides \textbf{standard chemistry context}. The content
describes known molecular structures for pedagogical completeness.
\textbf{No quantitative predictions} are derived from FTD axioms at this
scale.

\end{tcolorbox}

\emph{``The building blocks of chemistry''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Simple molecules, just two or three atoms, make up our atmosphere, our
water, and the gases we breathe. Complexity starts small.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Diatomic Molecules}\label{diatomic-molecules}

Two-atom molecules. Many elements exist naturally as diatomics.

\subsection{Homonuclear Diatomics}\label{homonuclear-diatomics}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Molecule & Bond & Length (Å) & Energy (kJ/mol) & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\text{H}_2\) & Single & 0.74 & 436 & Simplest \\
\(\text{N}_2\) & Triple & 1.10 & 946 & Very stable \\

\(\text{F}_2\) & Single & 1.42 & 158 & Weak \\

\end{longtable}

\subsection{Nitrogen: The Inert Gas}\label{nitrogen-the-inert-gas}

\begin{verbatim}
:N≡N:

Triple bond makes $\text{N}_2$ extremely stable.
78% of atmosphere.
Will not react with most substances.
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Oxygen: The Reactive Gas}\label{oxygen-the-reactive-gas}

\begin{verbatim}
·O=O·

Double bond, but with unpaired electrons.
Paramagnetic (attracted to magnets).
Essential for respiration.
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Heteronuclear Diatomics}\label{heteronuclear-diatomics}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Molecule & Bond & Length (Å) & Energy (kJ/mol) & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}

HF & Polar & 0.92 & 570 & Strong H-bond \\
HCl & Polar & 1.27 & 432 & Acid \\
CO & Triple & 1.13 & 1077 & Toxic, very stable \\
NO & 2.5 & 1.15 & 631 & Radical, pollutant \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Carbon Monoxide: Silent
Killer}\label{carbon-monoxide-silent-killer}

\begin{verbatim}
:C≡O:

Isoelectronic with N₂ (same electron count).
Binds to hemoglobin stronger than O₂.
Colorless, odorless: dangerous.
\end{verbatim}

\section{Triatomic Molecules}\label{triatomic-molecules}

Three atoms, various geometries.

\subsection{Water: The Universal
Solvent}\label{water-the-universal-solvent}

\begin{verbatim}
    H   H
     \ /
      O
   104.5°
\end{verbatim}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value & Significance \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Geometry & Bent & Polar molecule \\
Bond angle & 104.5° & Less than tetrahedral \\
Polarity & High & Dissolves salts \\
H-bonding & Strong & High boiling point \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Carbon Dioxide: Linear Greenhouse
Gas}\label{carbon-dioxide-linear-greenhouse-gas}

\begin{verbatim}
O=C=O
  180°
\end{verbatim}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value & Significance \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Geometry & Linear & Nonpolar overall \\
Bonds & Double (both) & Stable \\
Vibration & IR active & Greenhouse effect \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Ozone: The UV Shield}\label{ozone-the-uv-shield}

\begin{verbatim}
  O       O
   \     /
    O
  116.8°
\end{verbatim}

Three oxygen atoms, bent geometry. Absorbs harmful UV radiation in the
stratosphere.

\subsection{Other Triatomics}\label{other-triatomics}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Molecule & Geometry & Angle & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\text{H}_2\text{S}\) & Bent & 92° & Rotten egg smell \\
\(\text{NO}_2\) & Bent & 134° & Brown gas, pollutant \\
\(\text{SO}_2\) & Bent & 119° & Acid rain precursor \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Molecular Geometry}\label{molecular-geometry}

\subsection{VSEPR Theory}\label{vsepr-theory}

Electron pairs arrange to minimize repulsion:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Electron pairs & Geometry & Example \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 & Linear & \(\text{CO}_2\) \\
3 & Trigonal planar & \(\text{BF}_3\) \\
4 & Tetrahedral & \(\text{CH}_4\) \\
5 & Trigonal bipyramidal & \(\text{PCl}_5\) \\
6 & Octahedral & \(\text{SF}_6\) \\
\end{longtable}

Lone pairs count too, but do not appear in the name.

\subsection{Why Water is Bent}\label{why-water-is-bent}

Oxygen has 4 electron pairs around it: - 2 bonding pairs (to H) - 2 lone
pairs

\begin{verbatim}
       ‥
       O
      / \
     H   H
\end{verbatim}

Lone pairs repel more than bonding pairs, compressing the bond angle
from 109.5° to 104.5°.

\section{Polarity}\label{polarity}

\subsection{Molecular Dipole Moment}\label{molecular-dipole-moment}

The vector sum of all bond dipoles.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Water}: Bent shape → net dipole → polar
\item
  \textbf{CO₂}: Linear shape → dipoles cancel → nonpolar
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Why Polarity Matters}\label{why-polarity-matters}

Polar molecules: - Dissolve in water - Have higher boiling points -
Participate in hydrogen bonding

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-5}

\subsection{Build Molecules}\label{build-molecules}

Construct diatomic and triatomic molecules. See geometries form.

\subsection{Measure Bond Properties}\label{measure-bond-properties}

Calculate bond lengths, angles, and energies.

\subsection{Polarity Visualization}\label{polarity-visualization}

See charge distributions in molecules.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-5}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{diatomic}: Two-atom molecule
\item
  \textbf{triatomic}: Three-atom molecule
\item
  \textbf{molecular-geometry}: Shape determined by electron pairs
\item
  \textbf{molecular-polarity}: Net charge distribution
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-5}

Simple molecules are the letters. Now we build words: complex molecules
with multiple functional groups, including the organic chemistry of
life.

\chapter{Complex Molecules}\label{sec-complex-molecules}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Pedagogical Context {[}NOT DERIVED FROM FTD{]}}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

This chapter provides \textbf{standard organic chemistry context}. The
content describes known molecular structures for pedagogical
completeness. \textbf{No quantitative predictions} are derived from FTD
axioms at this scale.

\end{tcolorbox}

\emph{``Organic chemistry and beyond''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Carbon's ability to form four bonds creates unlimited molecular
possibilities. Add nitrogen, oxygen, and other atoms, and you have the
chemistry of life.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Carbon: The Backbone of
Complexity}\label{carbon-the-backbone-of-complexity}

Why carbon? - 4 valence electrons → 4 bonds - Can bond to itself →
chains and rings - Right size → stable bonds - Multiple bond types →
single, double, triple

\section{Hydrocarbons}\label{hydrocarbons}

Molecules of just carbon and hydrogen.

\subsection{Alkanes (Single Bonds)}\label{alkanes-single-bonds}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Formula & Name & Structure & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\text{CH}_4\) & Methane & Tetrahedral & Natural gas \\
\(\text{C}_2\text{H}_6\) & Ethane & Two tetrahedra & \\
\(\text{C}_3\text{H}_8\) & Propane & Chain & Fuel \\
\(\text{C}_4\text{H}_{10}\) & Butane & Chain & Lighter fluid \\
\(\text{C}_5\text{H}_{12}\) & Pentane & Chain & Solvent \\
\end{longtable}

General formula: \(\text{C}_n\text{H}_{2n+2}\)

\subsection{Alkenes (Double Bonds)}\label{alkenes-double-bonds}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Formula & Name & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\text{C}_2\text{H}_4\) & Ethylene & Planar, ripening agent \\
\(\text{C}_3\text{H}_6\) & Propene & Plastic precursor \\
\end{longtable}

General formula: \(\text{C}_n\text{H}_{2n}\)

\subsection{Alkynes (Triple Bonds)}\label{alkynes-triple-bonds}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Formula & Name & Notes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\text{C}_2\text{H}_2\) & Acetylene & Linear, welding fuel \\
\(\text{C}_3\text{H}_4\) & Propyne & \\
\end{longtable}

General formula: \(\text{C}_n\text{H}_{2n-2}\)

\subsection{Aromatic Compounds}\label{aromatic-compounds}

Ring structures with alternating double bonds.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-benzene.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-benzene}Benzene ring structure: six carbons in a
hexagonal ring with alternating double bonds.}

\end{figure}%

Benzene (\(\text{C}_6\text{H}_6\)) features a six-membered ring with
resonance: electrons delocalize around the entire ring rather than
alternating between fixed single and double bonds.

\section{Functional Groups}\label{functional-groups}

The reactive parts of molecules.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Group & Formula & Name & Example \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
-OH & R-OH & Alcohol & Ethanol \\
-CHO & R-CHO & Aldehyde & Formaldehyde \\
-COOH & R-COOH & Carboxylic acid & Acetic acid \\
\(-\text{NH}_2\) & \(\text{R-NH}_2\) & Amine & Methylamine \\
-SH & R-SH & Thiol & Methanethiol \\
C=O & R-CO-R' & Ketone & Acetone \\
-O- & R-O-R' & Ether & Diethyl ether \\
-COO- & R-COO-R' & Ester & Ethyl acetate \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Alcohols}\label{alcohols}

Hydroxyl group (-OH) on carbon:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-ethanol.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-ethanol}Ethanol molecule showing hydroxyl group.}

\end{figure}%

Ethanol (CH₃CH₂OH) is the ``drinking alcohol'' found in beer, wine, and
spirits.

\subsection{Acids and Bases}\label{acids-and-bases}

\textbf{Carboxylic acids} donate \(\text{H}^+\): R-COOH \(\to\)
R-COO\(^-\) + \(\text{H}^+\)

\textbf{Amines} accept \(\text{H}^+\): \(\text{R-NH}_2\) +
\(\text{H}^+\) \(\to\) \(\text{R-NH}_3^+\)

\section{Isomers}\label{isomers}

Same formula, different structure.

\subsection{Structural Isomers}\label{structural-isomers}

Different connectivity: \textbf{Butane} (CH₃-CH₂-CH₂-CH₃) is a straight
chain, while \textbf{Isobutane} (CH₃-CH(CH₃)-CH₃) is branched. Same
formula (C₄H₁₀), different structure.

\subsection{Stereoisomers}\label{stereoisomers}

Same connectivity, different spatial arrangement: - \textbf{Cis-trans}:
Around double bonds - \textbf{Enantiomers}: Mirror images (chirality)

\subsection{Chirality}\label{chirality}

A carbon with 4 different groups is chiral:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-chirality.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-chirality}Chirality: a carbon bonded to four
different groups has non-superimposable mirror images.}

\end{figure}%

Mirror images are not superimposable. This matters in biology: life uses
only one ``handedness'' of amino acids.

\section{Reactions}\label{reactions}

\subsection{Combustion}\label{combustion}

\(\text{CH}_4 + 2\text{O}_2 \to \text{CO}_2 + 2\text{H}_2\text{O}\) +
energy

\subsection{Substitution}\label{substitution}

One group replaces another.

\subsection{Addition}\label{addition}

Atoms add across double bond.

\subsection{Elimination}\label{elimination}

Double bond forms, atoms leave.

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-6}

\subsection{Molecule Builder}\label{molecule-builder}

Construct organic molecules from atoms. See 3D geometries.

\subsection{Reaction Simulator}\label{reaction-simulator}

Watch reactions occur: bonds breaking, bonds forming.

\subsection{Isomer Explorer}\label{isomer-explorer}

Find all isomers for a given formula.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-6}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{hydrocarbon}: C and H only
\item
  \textbf{functional-group}: Reactive part of molecule
\item
  \textbf{isomer}: Same formula, different structure
\item
  \textbf{chirality}: Mirror-image asymmetry
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-6}

Complex molecules are still small compared to the giants of chemistry.
Next, we explore macromolecules: proteins, polymers, and DNA.

\chapter{Macromolecules}\label{sec-macromolecules}

\emph{``The giants of the molecular world''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

From simple building blocks, enormous structures emerge. Proteins fold,

\section{Proteins}\label{proteins}

Chains of amino acids that do everything in life.

\subsection{Amino Acids}\label{amino-acids}

20 standard amino acids, each with: - Amino group (\(-\text{NH}_2\)) -
Carboxyl group (-COOH) - R group (side chain: what makes each unique)

The general structure: \(\text{H}_2\text{N}\)-CH(R)-COOH, where R is the
side chain unique to each amino acid.

\subsection{Peptide Bonds}\label{peptide-bonds}

Amino acids link by condensation, releasing water and forming the
peptide bond (-CO-NH-) that chains amino acids together into proteins.

\subsection{Protein Structure}\label{protein-structure}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Level & Description & Forces \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Primary & Amino acid sequence & Covalent (peptide bonds) \\
Secondary & α-helix, β-sheet & Hydrogen bonds \\
Tertiary & 3D folding & All forces \\
Quaternary & Multiple chains & All forces \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Protein Functions}\label{protein-functions}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Enzymes}: Catalyze reactions
\item
  \textbf{Structural}: Collagen, keratin
\item
  \textbf{Transport}: Hemoglobin
\item
  \textbf{Immune}: Antibodies
\item
  \textbf{Signaling}: Hormones
\end{itemize}

\section{Nucleic Acids}\label{nucleic-acids}

Information storage molecules.

\subsection{DNA Structure}\label{dna-structure}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-dna-helix.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-dna-helix}DNA double helix structure with
sugar-phosphate backbone and base pairing.}

\end{figure}%

Two antiparallel sugar-phosphate backbones connected by base pairs: -
\textbf{A-T}: Adenine pairs with thymine via 2 hydrogen bonds -
\textbf{G-C}: Guanine pairs with cytosine via 3 hydrogen bonds

\subsection{Double Helix}\label{double-helix}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  10 base pairs per turn
\item
  3.4 nm pitch
\item
  Major and minor grooves
\item
  Antiparallel strands
\end{itemize}

\subsection{RNA}\label{rna}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Single-stranded (usually)
\item
  Uracil instead of thymine
\item
  Sugar is ribose (not deoxyribose)
\item
  Types: mRNA, tRNA, rRNA
\end{itemize}

\section{Polymers}\label{polymers}

Long chains of repeating units (monomers).

\subsection{Addition Polymers}\label{addition-polymers}

Monomers add without losing atoms:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Monomer & Polymer & Use \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Ethylene & Polyethylene & Plastic bags \\
Propylene & Polypropylene & Containers \\
Styrene & Polystyrene & Foam, packaging \\
Vinyl chloride & PVC & Pipes \\
\end{longtable}

Example: n(\(\text{CH}_2\text{=CH}_2\)) \(\to\)
\(-[-\text{CH}_2\text{-CH}_2-]_n-\) (polyethylene)

\subsection{Condensation Polymers}\label{condensation-polymers}

Monomers lose small molecule (usually water):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Example & Use \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Polyester & PET & Bottles, fabric \\
Polyamide & Nylon & Fibers, fabric \\
Polyurethane & PU & Foam, coatings \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Carbohydrates}\label{carbohydrates}

Sugars and their polymers.

\subsection{Monosaccharides}\label{monosaccharides}

Simple sugars: glucose, fructose, galactose (all
\(\text{C}_6\text{H}_{12}\text{O}_6\))

\subsection{Disaccharides}\label{disaccharides}

Two sugars linked: - Sucrose = glucose + fructose - Lactose = glucose +
galactose - Maltose = glucose + glucose

\subsection{Polysaccharides}\label{polysaccharides}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Name & Function & Source \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Starch & Energy storage & Plants \\
Glycogen & Energy storage & Animals \\
Cellulose & Structure & Plant cell walls \\
Chitin & Structure & Fungi, arthropods \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Lipids}\label{lipids}

Not true polymers, but complex molecules.

\subsection{Fatty Acids}\label{fatty-acids}

Long hydrocarbon chain + carboxyl group:
\(\text{CH}_3(\text{CH}_2)_n\text{COOH}\)

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Saturated}: No double bonds, solid at room temperature
\item
  \textbf{Unsaturated}: Double bonds, liquid at room temperature
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Phospholipids}\label{phospholipids}

Basis of cell membranes:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-lipid-bilayer.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-phospholipid}Phospholipid structure: hydrophilic
head and hydrophobic tails.}

\end{figure}%

Each phospholipid has a hydrophilic head (phosphate group) and
hydrophobic tails (fatty acid chains). In water, they spontaneously form
bilayers with heads facing outward and tails facing inward.

\subsection{Protein Folding}\label{protein-folding}

Watch a chain of amino acids fold into its final structure.

\subsection{DNA Replication}\label{dna-replication}

Observe the double helix unzip and copy.

\subsection{Polymer Synthesis}\label{polymer-synthesis}

Build polymers from monomers. Compare properties.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-7}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{protein}: Amino acid polymer with complex structure
\item
  \textbf{nucleic-acid}: Information-carrying polymer (DNA, RNA)
\item
  \textbf{polymer}: Long chain of repeating units
\item
  \textbf{carbohydrate}: Sugar or sugar polymer
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-7}

Macromolecules have complex properties. Now we zoom out to see how
molecules aggregate into bulk matter: the different states and their
transitions.

\part{Book VI: States of Matter}

\chapter{States of Matter}\label{sec-states-matter}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Pedagogical Context {[}NOT DERIVED FROM FTD{]}}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

This chapter provides \textbf{standard thermodynamics context}. While
FTD proposes a mechanism for emergent thermodynamics (see callout
below), \textbf{no quantitative predictions} (melting points, heat
capacities) are derived from FTD axioms at this scale. The mechanisms
described are {[}CONJECTURE{]} - proposed correspondences, not rigorous
derivations.

\end{tcolorbox}

\emph{``Solid, liquid, gas: and beyond''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

The same atoms behave completely differently depending on temperature
and pressure. Phase is about motion, not identity.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Proposes Thermodynamics {[}CONJECTURE{]}}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Temperature, pressure, and phase are \emph{proposed} to emerge in FTD:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Kinetic energy} = average squared velocity of manifested
  particles
\item
  \textbf{Temperature} emerges as a proxy:
  \(T_{\text{proxy}} = \langle|\mathbf{J}|^2\rangle / 3N\)
\item
  \textbf{Pressure} = rate of flux exchange at system boundaries
\item
  \textbf{Phase} = classification based on density, mobility, and
  correlation length
\end{itemize}

Standard thermodynamic laws (PV = nRT, entropy increase) emerge in the
continuum limit. For the rigorous derivation, see
THEORETICAL\_FOUNDATIONS Part IV: Statistical Mechanics.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Four Primary States}\label{the-four-primary-states}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
State & Density & Order & Particle Motion \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Solid & High & Long-range & Vibration in place \\
Liquid & High & Short-range & Flow, but touch \\
Gas & Low & None & Free movement \\
Plasma & Low & None & Ionized, free electrons \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-states-of-matter-panels.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-states-of-matter}The four primary states of matter
and their characteristic properties.}

\end{figure}%

\section{Solid}\label{solid}

Particles locked in fixed positions.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-1}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Definite shape and volume
\item
  Incompressible
\item
  Particles vibrate around fixed points
\item
  Long-range order (crystalline) or short-range (amorphous)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Types}\label{types}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Crystalline}: Regular lattice (salt, diamond)
\item
  \textbf{Amorphous}: No long-range order (glass, plastic)
\end{itemize}

\section{Liquid}\label{liquid}

Particles touching but mobile.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Definite volume, no definite shape
\item
  Nearly incompressible
\item
  Particles slide past each other
\item
  Short-range order only
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Unique Features}\label{unique-features}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Surface tension (inward force from cohesion)
\item
  Viscosity (resistance to flow)
\item
  Capillary action (climbing narrow tubes)
\end{itemize}

\section{Gas}\label{gas}

Particles far apart and free.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  No definite shape or volume
\item
  Highly compressible
\item
  Particles move randomly
\item
  No order
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Gas Laws}\label{gas-laws}

\textbf{Ideal Gas Law}: \[
PV = nRT
\]

where R = 8.314 J/(mol·K) is the ideal gas constant, P is pressure, V is
volume, n is the number of moles, and T is temperature.

\textbf{Kinetic Theory}: \[
\frac{3}{2}kT = \frac{1}{2}mv^2_{\text{avg}}
\]

Temperature IS average kinetic energy.

\section{Plasma}\label{plasma}

Ionized gas: electrons freed from atoms.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-4}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Electrically conductive
\item
  Responds to magnetic fields
\item
  Emits light
\item
  Most common state in the universe
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Where Found}\label{where-found}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Stars (99\% of visible matter)
\item
  Lightning
\item
  Neon signs
\item
  Fusion reactors
\end{itemize}

\section{Temperature in Simulation}\label{temperature-in-simulation}

Temperature emerges from particle motion. In FTD, we define a
\textbf{temperature proxy} that mirrors the kinetic theory result:

\[
T_{\text{proxy}} = \frac{\langle|\mathbf{J}|^2\rangle}{3N}
\]

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Dimensional Analysis}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

In physical units, kinetic theory gives
\(\frac{3}{2}k_B T = \frac{1}{2}m\langle v^2\rangle\), so
\(T = \frac{m\langle v^2\rangle}{3k_B}\). In FTD natural units where
flux \(|J|\) serves as a momentum proxy and we set \(k_B = 1\), the
temperature proxy has dimensions of energy per particle. The factor of 3
accounts for three spatial dimensions (equipartition theorem).

\end{tcolorbox}

Higher average squared flux magnitude = higher temperature.

\section{Pressure in Simulation}\label{pressure-in-simulation}

Pressure from particle collisions with boundaries:

\[
P = \frac{F}{A} = \frac{\text{momentum transfer}}{\text{area} \times \text{time}}
\]

\section{Phase in the Lattice}\label{phase-in-the-lattice}

Phase determined by: 1. \textbf{Density}: Particles per region 2.
\textbf{Temperature proxy}: Average \textbar flux\textbar{} 3.
\textbf{Order parameter}: How correlated are neighbor positions?

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ determine\_phase(region):}
\NormalTok{    density }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ count\_particles(region) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ region.volume}
\NormalTok{    temperature }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ average\_flux\_magnitude(region)}
\NormalTok{    order }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ correlation\_length(region)}

    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ density }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ HIGH }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ order }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ LONG\_RANGE:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SOLID}
    \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ density }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ HIGH }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ order }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ SHORT\_RANGE:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ LIQUID}
    \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ density }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ LOW:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ ionized(region):}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ PLASMA}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ GAS}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-8}

\subsection{Heat and Cool}\label{heat-and-cool}

Start with solid. Add energy. Watch transition to liquid, then gas.

\subsection{Pressure Changes}\label{pressure-changes}

Compress a gas. See density increase, temperature rise.

\subsection{Phase Boundaries}\label{phase-boundaries}

Map the conditions where different phases exist.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-8}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{solid}: Fixed positions, long-range order
\item
  \textbf{liquid}: Touching, flowing, short-range order
\item
  \textbf{gas}: Separated, free motion
\item
  \textbf{plasma}: Ionized gas
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Chapter Summary}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

\textbf{Matter exists in four primary phases}: solid (fixed positions,
long-range order), liquid (touching but mobile, short-range order), gas
(separated, no order), and plasma (ionized, responds to fields).
\textbf{Temperature emerges} as average kinetic flux: T\_proxy =
\(\langle|J|^2\rangle\)/3N. \textbf{Phase is determined} by density,
temperature proxy, and correlation length. The same atoms behave
completely differently depending on conditions: phase is about motion,
not identity. Plasma is the most common state in the universe (99\% of
visible matter is in stars).

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-8}

States exist in equilibrium under specific conditions. At the
boundaries, transitions occur. Next, we explore phase transitions:
melting, boiling, and more in Chapter~\ref{sec-phase-transitions}.
