\section{Transition}\label{transition-17}

Solid ground and gaseous atmosphere need protection. Next, we explore
magnetospheres: the invisible shields that protect worlds from space.

\chapter{Magnetospheres}\label{sec-magnetospheres}

\emph{``Invisible shields''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

A planet's magnetic field is its shield against the solar wind. Without
one, atmospheres erode and surfaces are irradiated. Earth's
magnetosphere makes life possible.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Planetary Magnetic Fields}\label{planetary-magnetic-fields}

\subsection{The Dynamo Mechanism}\label{the-dynamo-mechanism}

Magnetic fields arise from: 1. \textbf{Conducting fluid} (liquid metal
core) 2. \textbf{Convection} (heat-driven motion) 3. \textbf{Rotation}
(Coriolis effect organizes flow)

\[
\frac{\partial \mathbf{B}}{\partial t} = \nabla \times (\mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{B}) + \eta \nabla^2 \mathbf{B}
\]

\subsection{Magnetic Field Comparison}\label{magnetic-field-comparison}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Planet & Field Strength & Dynamo Active? \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Mercury & 1\% Earth & Weak \\
Venus & None & No (slow rotation) \\
Earth & 25-65 \(\mu\)T & Yes \\
Mars & Crustal only & Extinct \\
Jupiter & 20× Earth & Very active \\
Saturn & 0.5× Earth & Active \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Magnetosphere Structure}\label{magnetosphere-structure}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-magnetosphere-structure.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-magnetosphere}The structure of Earth's
magnetosphere: solar wind compressed on the dayside creates the bow
shock and magnetopause, while the magnetotail extends away from the
Sun.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Key Regions}\label{key-regions}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Region & Description \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Bow shock & Where solar wind slows (supersonic \(\to\) subsonic) \\
Magnetopause & Boundary of magnetic dominance \\
Plasmasphere & Inner region of trapped plasma \\
Radiation belts & High-energy particle zones \\
Magnetotail & Extended tail (sunward side) \\
\end{longtable}

\section{The Solar Wind}\label{the-solar-wind}

Stream of charged particles from the Sun:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Speed}: 300-800 km/s
\item
  \textbf{Density}: \textasciitilde5 particles/cm³
\item
  \textbf{Composition}: Protons, electrons, some He
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Interaction with
Magnetosphere}\label{interaction-with-magnetosphere}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Solar wind approaches
\item
  Bow shock slows particles
\item
  Flow diverted around magnetopause
\item
  Some particles enter via cusps
\item
  Tail stretches in antisunward direction
\end{enumerate}

\section{Van Allen Belts}\label{van-allen-belts}

Trapped charged particle zones:

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Inner Belt (1-2
\(R_\oplus\))}{Inner Belt (1-2 R\_\textbackslash oplus)}}\label{inner-belt-1-2-r_oplus}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Primarily protons
\item
  Energies: 10-100 MeV
\item
  Source: Cosmic rays, solar protons
\end{itemize}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Outer Belt (3-6
\(R_\oplus\))}{Outer Belt (3-6 R\_\textbackslash oplus)}}\label{outer-belt-3-6-r_oplus}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Primarily electrons
\item
  Energies: 0.1-10 MeV
\item
  Source: Solar wind injection
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Hazards}\label{hazards}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Damage to electronics
\item
  Radiation exposure for astronauts
\item
  Satellites must navigate carefully
\end{itemize}

\section{Aurora}\label{aurora}

When solar particles enter the atmosphere, they follow magnetic field
lines toward the poles. Upon colliding with atmospheric gases, they
excite atoms which then emit light, creating the aurora.

\subsection{Colors}\label{colors}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Color & Cause \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Green & Oxygen (557.7 nm) \\
Red & Oxygen (630.0 nm) \\
Blue/Purple & Nitrogen \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Locations}\label{locations}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Auroral ovals around magnetic poles
\item
  Expand during magnetic storms
\item
  Visible from 60-70° latitude typically
\end{itemize}

\section{Magnetic Reversals}\label{magnetic-reversals}

Earth's field flips polarity:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Average interval: \textasciitilde300,000 years
\item
  Last reversal: 780,000 years ago
\item
  Transition takes: \textasciitilde1,000-10,000 years
\item
  Field weakens during reversal
\end{itemize}

\section{Without a Magnetosphere}\label{without-a-magnetosphere}

\subsection{Mars}\label{mars-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Lost its magnetic field \textasciitilde4 billion years ago
\item
  Solar wind strips atmosphere
\item
  Surface now irradiated
\item
  May have prevented life
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Implications}\label{implications}

Magnetospheres are crucial for: - Atmospheric retention - Surface
habitability - Protection from cosmic rays

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-10}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Magnetosphere:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, planet):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.planet }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ planet}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.field\_strength }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ dynamo\_strength(planet.core)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ shape(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, solar\_wind):}
        \CommentTok{\# Pressure balance determines magnetopause}
\NormalTok{        r\_magnetopause }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.standoff\_distance(solar\_wind.pressure)}

        \CommentTok{\# Tail extends antisunward}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.tail\_length }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{100} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ r\_magnetopause}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ deflect(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, particle):}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ particle.charge }\OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{:}
            \CommentTok{\# Lorentz force}
\NormalTok{            F }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ particle.charge }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ cross(particle.velocity, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.B\_field)}
\NormalTok{            particle.apply\_force(F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-18}

We have explored planets in detail. Now we scale up to the engines that
power solar systems: stars themselves.

\part{Book IX: The Stellar Realm}

\chapter{Stellar Formation}\label{sec-stellar-formation}

\emph{``From nebula to star''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Stars are born when gravity defeats pressure in molecular clouds. The
same process that forms planets (accretion) operates at vastly larger
scales to ignite fusion.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Produces Stellar Formation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Stellar formation in FTD follows from flux dynamics at large scales:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Gravitational instability} = regions where flux density
  exceeds local pressure support
\item
  \textbf{Jeans mass} emerges from the balance of gravitational flux
  gradients vs kinetic flux
\item
  \textbf{Fragmentation} occurs when cooling (flux dissipation) reduces
  Jeans mass
\item
  \textbf{Fusion ignition} happens when core flux density reaches
  nuclear threshold
\end{itemize}

The hierarchical structure of star-forming regions reflects the
scale-free nature of gravitational flux collapse.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Molecular Cloud Collapse}\label{molecular-cloud-collapse}

\subsection{Initial Conditions}\label{initial-conditions}

Stars form in giant molecular clouds: - Size: 10-100 parsecs - Mass:
\(10^4\)-\(10^6\) \(M_\odot\) - Temperature: 10-20 K - Composition:
\(\text{H}_2\), He, dust

\subsection{Trigger Mechanisms}\label{trigger-mechanisms}

Something must start the collapse: - \textbf{Supernova shockwave}:
Compression - \textbf{Cloud collision}: Density increase -
\textbf{Spiral arm compression}: Density wave - \textbf{Turbulence
decay}: Support removal

\subsection{Jeans Criterion}\label{jeans-criterion}

Collapse occurs when gravity overcomes thermal pressure:

\[
M > M_J = \left(\frac{5k_BT}{G\mu m_H}\right)^{3/2} \left(\frac{3}{4\pi\rho}\right)^{1/2}
\]

For typical molecular cloud: \(M_J \approx 10-100 \, M_\odot\)

\section{Fragmentation}\label{fragmentation}

Large clouds break into multiple protostars through hierarchical
fragmentation: the initial cloud subdivides into clumps, each of which
continues collapsing independently.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-star-formation.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-star-formation}Star formation from molecular cloud
collapse through fragmentation, accretion, and protostellar evolution to
main sequence.}

\end{figure}%

\section{Protostellar Evolution}\label{protostellar-evolution}

\subsection{Class 0 (0-10,000 years)}\label{class-0-0-10000-years}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Deeply embedded in cloud
\item
  Invisible at optical wavelengths
\item
  Accretion onto central object
\item
  Bipolar outflows begin
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Class I (10,000-100,000
years)}\label{class-i-10000-100000-years}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Envelope still infalling
\item
  Infrared bright
\item
  Disk forming
\item
  Outflows strengthen
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Class II (100,000-1 million
years)}\label{class-ii-100000-1-million-years}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  T Tauri star
\item
  Visible optically
\item
  Disk prominent
\item
  Planet formation begins
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Class III (\textgreater1 million
years)}\label{class-iii-1-million-years}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Disk dispersing
\item
  Pre-main sequence
\item
  Approaching hydrogen fusion
\item
  ``Weak-line'' T Tauri
\end{itemize}

\section{The Birth Line}\label{the-birth-line}

Stars become visible when they emerge from their natal cloud. On the
Hertzsprung-Russell diagram, protostars first appear along the birth
line, then contract along pre-main sequence tracks until they reach the
main sequence where hydrogen fusion begins.

\section{Star Formation Efficiency}\label{star-formation-efficiency}

Only 1-10\% of cloud mass becomes stars: - Stellar winds disperse gas -
Radiation pressure pushes dust - Supernovae clear remaining material

\section{Initial Mass Function}\label{initial-mass-function}

The distribution of stellar masses at birth:

\[
\frac{dN}{dM} \propto M^{-2.35}
\]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Many more low-mass stars than high-mass
\item
  Few stars \textgreater{} 50 \(M_\odot\)
\item
  Peak around 0.1-0.5 \(M_\odot\)
\end{itemize}

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-11}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ form\_star(molecular\_cloud):}
    \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ cloud.mass }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ jeans\_mass(cloud):}
        \CommentTok{\# Gravitational collapse}
\NormalTok{        cloud.contract()}

        \CommentTok{\# Temperature rises from compression}
\NormalTok{        cloud.temperature }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ compression\_heating()}

        \CommentTok{\# Check for ignition}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ cloud.core\_temp }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ FUSION\_THRESHOLD:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ MainSequenceStar(cloud)}

        \CommentTok{\# Fragment if unstable}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ should\_fragment(cloud):}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ [form\_star(fragment) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ fragment }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ split(cloud)]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-19}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{jeans-mass}: Minimum mass for collapse
\item
  \textbf{fragmentation}: Cloud breaking into multiple stars
\item
  \textbf{protostar}: Star before hydrogen fusion begins
\item
  \textbf{t-tauri}: Young pre-main-sequence star
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-19}

Protostars become real stars when fusion ignites. Next
(Chapter~\ref{sec-main-sequence}), we explore the main sequence: where
stars spend most of their lives, steadily fusing hydrogen into helium
for billions of years.

The journey from molecular cloud to main-sequence star takes millions of
years: a geological instant on cosmic timescales, yet long enough for
complex processes of fragmentation, accretion, and disk formation that
determine not just stellar properties but also the planets that may form
alongside.

\item
  \textbf{Main sequence}: Chapter~\ref{sec-main-sequence} describes
  stellar hydrogen burning
\item
  \textbf{Stellar nucleosynthesis}:
  Chapter~\ref{sec-stellar-nucleosynthesis} covers element formation in
  stars
\item
  \textbf{Stellar death}: Chapter~\ref{sec-stellar-death} explores how
  stars end their lives
\item
  \textbf{Gravity wells}: Chapter~\ref{sec-gravity-wells} explains the
  gravitational dynamics underlying collapse
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Main Sequence}\label{sec-main-sequence}

\emph{``Where stars spend their lives''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

A main sequence star is a self-regulating fusion reactor: gravity
squeezes, fusion pushes back. This equilibrium can last billions of
years.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Hydrostatic Equilibrium}\label{hydrostatic-equilibrium}

The balance that defines a star:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-hydrostatic-equilibrium.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-hydrostatic}Hydrostatic equilibrium in a star:
gravity pulls inward while pressure from fusion pushes outward, creating
a stable balance.}

\end{figure}%

At every point in a stable star, the inward gravitational force is
exactly balanced by the outward pressure from fusion reactions in the
core.

\[
\frac{dP}{dr} = -\frac{Gm(r)\rho}{r^2}
\]

At every point, pressure exactly balances gravity.

\section{Energy Generation}\label{energy-generation}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Proton-Proton Chain (M \textless{} 1.5
\(M_\odot\))}{Proton-Proton Chain (M \textless{} 1.5 M\_\textbackslash odot)}}\label{proton-proton-chain-m-1.5-m_odot}

Dominant in Sun-like stars:

\[
4p \rightarrow {^4\text{He}} + 2e^+ + 2\nu_e + 26.7 \text{ MeV}
\]

Step by step:

\begin{verbatim}
p + p → D + e⁺ + νₑ     (slow: rate limiting)
D + p → ³He + γ
³He + ³He → ⁴He + 2p
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{CNO Cycle (M \textgreater{} 1.5
\(M_\odot\))}{CNO Cycle (M \textgreater{} 1.5 M\_\textbackslash odot)}}\label{cno-cycle-m-1.5-m_odot}

Carbon, nitrogen, oxygen catalyze fusion:

\begin{verbatim}
¹²C + p → ¹³N + γ
¹³N → ¹³C + e⁺ + νₑ
¹³C + p → ¹⁴N + γ
¹⁴N + p → ¹⁵O + γ     (slow: rate limiting)
¹⁵O → ¹⁵N + e⁺ + νₑ
¹⁵N + p → ¹²C + ⁴He
\end{verbatim}

Net: 4p → ⁴He (carbon recycled)

\section{The Mass-Luminosity
Relation}\label{the-mass-luminosity-relation}

\[
L \propto M^{3.5}
\]

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Mass & Luminosity & Temp & Lifetime \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
0.1 \(M_\odot\) & 0.001 \(L_\odot\) & 3000 K & 10 trillion yr \\
0.5 \(M_\odot\) & 0.03 \(L_\odot\) & 4000 K & 50 billion yr \\
1 \(M_\odot\) & 1 \(L_\odot\) & 5800 K & 10 billion yr \\
2 \(M_\odot\) & 16 \(L_\odot\) & 8000 K & 1 billion yr \\
10 \(M_\odot\) & 3000 \(L_\odot\) & 20000 K & 30 million yr \\
50 \(M_\odot\) & 500000 \(L_\odot\) & 40000 K & 4 million yr \\
\end{longtable}

More massive stars exhibit significantly shorter main-sequence lifetimes
due to their elevated core temperatures and correspondingly higher
fusion rates.

\section{Spectral Classification}\label{spectral-classification}

Stars are classified by temperature:

\begin{verbatim}
O B A F G K M (L T Y for brown dwarfs)

"(Traditional mnemonic: O, B, A, F, G, K, M, L, T, Y, ordered by decreasing surface temperature)"
\end{verbatim}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Temperature & Color & Example \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
O & \textgreater30,000 K & Blue & ζ Ophiuchi \\
B & 10-30,000 K & Blue-white & Rigel \\
A & 7,500-10,000 K & White & Sirius \\
F & 6,000-7,500 K & Yellow-white & Procyon \\
G & 5,200-6,000 K & Yellow & Sun \\
K & 3,700-5,200 K & Orange & Arcturus \\
M & 2,400-3,700 K & Red & Betelgeuse \\
\end{longtable}

\section{The HR Diagram}\label{the-hr-diagram}

The fundamental tool of stellar astronomy:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-hr-diagram.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-hr-diagram}The Hertzsprung-Russell diagram:
luminosity versus temperature for stars. The main sequence runs
diagonally from hot, luminous O stars to cool, dim M stars.}

\end{figure}%

The main sequence is a diagonal band where luminosity correlates with
temperature. Stars spend most of their lives on this band, burning
hydrogen in their cores.

\section{Stellar Structure}\label{stellar-structure}

\subsection{Core}\label{core}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Fusion zone
\item
  15 million K (Sun)
\item
  25\% of radius
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Radiative Zone}\label{radiative-zone}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Energy transport by photons
\item
  Photons scatter, random walk
\item
  25-70\% of radius (depends on mass)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Convective Zone}\label{convective-zone}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Energy transport by bulk motion
\item
  Hot material rises, cool sinks
\item
  Outer 30\% (Sun)
\end{itemize}

\section{Self-Regulation}\label{self-regulation}

The stellar thermostat:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Fusion rate drops → core contracts
\item
  Contraction heats core
\item
  Higher temperature → faster fusion
\item
  More pressure → expansion
\item
  Expansion cools core
\item
  Back to equilibrium
\end{enumerate}

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-12}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ MainSequenceStar:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, mass):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mass }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mass}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.luminosity }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mass}\OperatorTok{**}\FloatTok{3.5}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.temperature }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{5800} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ mass}\OperatorTok{**}\FloatTok{0.5}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.lifetime }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{10e9} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ mass}\OperatorTok{**}\FloatTok{2.5}  \CommentTok{\# years}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ evolve(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, dt):}
        \CommentTok{\# Burn hydrogen}
\NormalTok{        H\_burned }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.luminosity }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ dt }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ FUSION\_EFFICIENCY}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.core\_hydrogen }\OperatorTok{{-}=}\NormalTok{ H\_burned}

        \CommentTok{\# Adjust structure}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.maintain\_equilibrium()}

        \CommentTok{\# Check for end of main sequence}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.core\_hydrogen }\OperatorTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.evolve\_off\_main\_sequence()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-20}

Stars do not just burn hydrogen. They forge heavier elements in their
cores. Next, we explore stellar nucleosynthesis: the origin of the
periodic table.

\chapter{Stellar Nucleosynthesis}\label{sec-stellar-nucleosynthesis}

\emph{``Forging the elements''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Every atom heavier than hydrogen was made inside a star. The carbon in
your body, the iron in your blood: all forged in stellar cores and
scattered by explosions.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Produces Nucleosynthesis}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Element formation follows from flux dynamics at nuclear densities:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Fusion} = flux-mediated tunneling through Coulomb barriers
  when core flux density is sufficient
\item
  \textbf{Binding energy} = net flux reduction when nucleons form stable
  configurations (triads)
\item
  \textbf{Iron peak} = maximum binding energy per nucleon (flux
  minimum); no energy release beyond
\item
  \textbf{r-process/s-process} = neutron flux capture rates in different
  stellar environments
\end{itemize}

The periodic table emerges from the stability landscape of nuclear flux
configurations.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Origin of Elements}\label{origin-of-elements}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Element & Origin \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
H, He (most) & Big Bang \\
He (some), C, O, N & Stellar fusion \\
Elements up to Fe & Massive star cores \\
Elements beyond Fe & Supernovae, mergers \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Hydrogen Burning}\label{hydrogen-burning}

Where it all starts (main sequence):

\[
4{^1\text{H}} \rightarrow {^4\text{He}} + 2e^+ + 2\nu_e + 26.7 \text{ MeV}
\]

This is the longest phase: billions of years for Sun-like stars.

\section{Helium Burning}\label{helium-burning}

\subsection{The Triple-Alpha Process}\label{the-triple-alpha-process}

When hydrogen is exhausted:

\[
{^4\text{He}} + {^4\text{He}} \rightarrow {^8\text{Be}} \quad (\text{unstable, } \tau = 10^{-16} \text{ s})
\] \[
{^8\text{Be}} + {^4\text{He}} \rightarrow {^{12}\text{C}} + \gamma
\]

Requires T \textgreater{} 100 million K.

\subsection{Carbon Production}\label{carbon-production}

The Hoyle resonance makes this possible: - \(^{12}\)C has an excited
state at exactly the right energy - Without it, almost no carbon would
form - Fine-tuning: Move the level by 1\%, no carbon

\subsection{Further Burning}\label{further-burning}

\[
{^{12}\text{C}} + {^4\text{He}} \rightarrow {^{16}\text{O}} + \gamma
\]

\section{Advanced Burning Stages}\label{advanced-burning-stages}

Only in massive stars (\textgreater{} 8 \(M_\odot\)):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Stage & Fuel & Products & T (K) & Duration \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
H & H & He & \(1.5 \times 10^7\) & 10 Myr \\
He & He & C, O & \(1 \times 10^8\) & 1 Myr \\
C & C & Ne, Mg & \(6 \times 10^8\) & 1000 yr \\
Ne & Ne & O, Mg & \(1.2 \times 10^9\) & 1 yr \\
O & O & Si, S & \(1.5 \times 10^9\) & months \\
Si & Si & Fe & \(3 \times 10^9\) & days \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The Onion Structure}\label{the-onion-structure}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-nucleosynthesis-onion.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-onion-structure}The onion-shell structure of a
massive star before supernova: concentric burning layers from hydrogen
on the outside to an inert iron core at the center.}

\end{figure}%

Each shell burns a different fuel, with progressively heavier elements
toward the center. The inert iron core grows until it exceeds the
Chandrasekhar limit and collapses.

\section{Iron: The End of the Line}\label{iron-the-end-of-the-line}

Iron-56 has the highest binding energy per nucleon.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Fusing iron \emph{absorbs} energy
\item
  No energy to maintain pressure
\item
  Core collapses → supernova
\end{itemize}

\section{Beyond Iron}\label{beyond-iron}

\subsection{The s-process (Slow Neutron
Capture)}\label{the-s-process-slow-neutron-capture}

In AGB stars: \[
{^A\text{X}} + n \rightarrow {^{A+1}\text{X}}
\]

If unstable, beta decay before next capture: \[
{^{A+1}\text{X}} \rightarrow {^{A+1}\text{Y}} + e^- + \bar{\nu}_e
\]

Creates elements up to bismuth.

\subsection{The r-process (Rapid Neutron
Capture)}\label{the-r-process-rapid-neutron-capture}

In supernovae and neutron star mergers: - Many neutrons captured before
decay - Creates heaviest elements (gold, uranium) - Requires extreme
neutron flux

\section{Yield}\label{yield}

A 25 \(M_\odot\) star produces (approximately):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Element & Mass (\(M_\odot\)) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
O & 3.0 \\
C & 0.4 \\
Ne & 0.3 \\
Mg & 0.1 \\
Si & 0.15 \\
S & 0.05 \\
Fe & 0.1 \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Cosmic Abundances}\label{cosmic-abundances}

Result of billions of years of nucleosynthesis:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Element & Abundance (relative to Si = \(10^6\)) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
H & \(2.8 \times 10^{10}\) \\
He & \(2.7 \times 10^9\) \\
O & \(2.4 \times 10^7\) \\
C & \(1.0 \times 10^7\) \\
Ne & \(3.4 \times 10^6\) \\
Fe & \(9.0 \times 10^5\) \\
N & \(3.1 \times 10^6\) \\
Si & \(1.0 \times 10^6\) \\
\end{longtable}

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-13}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ nucleosynthesis(star, stage):}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ stage }\OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}hydrogen\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        rate }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pp\_chain\_rate(star.core\_temp)}
\NormalTok{        star.helium }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ rate }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ dt}
\NormalTok{        star.hydrogen }\OperatorTok{{-}=} \DecValTok{4} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ rate }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ dt}

    \ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ stage }\OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}helium\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        rate }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ triple\_alpha\_rate(star.core\_temp)}
\NormalTok{        star.carbon }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ rate }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ dt}
\NormalTok{        star.helium }\OperatorTok{{-}=} \DecValTok{3} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ rate }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ dt}

    \CommentTok{\# ... continue for heavier elements}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-21}

\subsection{Fusion Chain}\label{fusion-chain}

Watch hydrogen become helium, helium become carbon.

\subsection{Element Factory}\label{element-factory}

Run massive star through all burning stages.

\subsection{Abundance Calculator}\label{abundance-calculator}

Sum contributions from many generations of stars.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-21}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{triple-alpha}: Helium to carbon fusion
\item
  \textbf{s-process}: Slow neutron capture
\item
  \textbf{r-process}: Rapid neutron capture
\item
  \textbf{iron-peak}: Fusion endpoint
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-21}

Stars do not last forever. When fusion ends, stars die: sometimes
quietly, sometimes explosively. Next, we explore stellar death.

\chapter{Stellar Death}\label{sec-stellar-death}

\emph{``How stars end their lives''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

A star's mass determines its fate. Small stars fade quietly into white
dwarfs. Massive stars explode as supernovae, seeding the universe with
heavy elements.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Produces Stellar Death}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Stellar endpoints follow from the limits of flux support:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{White dwarf} = electron degeneracy pressure (Pauli exclusion
  on flux states) halts collapse
\item
  \textbf{Neutron star} = neutron degeneracy pressure after electrons
  merge with protons
\item
  \textbf{Black hole} = no flux mechanism can support the core; collapse
  to singularity
\item
  \textbf{Supernova} = gravitational potential energy released when core
  collapses faster than flux can respond
\end{itemize}

The Chandrasekhar limit (\textasciitilde1.4 \(M_\odot\)) emerges from
the maximum electron degeneracy pressure.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Low Mass Stars (\textless{} 8
\(M_\odot\))}{Low Mass Stars (\textless{} 8 M\_\textbackslash odot)}}\label{low-mass-stars-8-m_odot}

The path of Sun-like stars.

\subsection{Red Giant Phase}\label{red-giant-phase}

When core hydrogen exhausted: 1. Core contracts, heats 2. Shell burning
begins 3. Envelope expands 4. Surface cools → red 5. Star swells to
\textasciitilde100× original size

\subsection{Helium Flash}\label{helium-flash}

In stars \textless{} 2 \(M_\odot\): - Degenerate core ignites helium -
Runaway fusion (seconds) - Core expands, stabilizes

\subsection{Asymptotic Giant Branch
(AGB)}\label{asymptotic-giant-branch-agb}

After helium exhausted: - Double shell burning (H and He) - Thermal
pulses - Strong mass loss - Planetary nebula forms

\subsection{Planetary Nebula}\label{planetary-nebula}

The expelled envelope forms a planetary nebula: an expanding shell of
gas illuminated by ultraviolet radiation from the hot central star.
These beautiful objects persist for tens of thousands of years before
dispersing into the interstellar medium.

\subsection{White Dwarf}\label{white-dwarf}

What remains: - Carbon-oxygen core - \textasciitilde0.6 \(M_\odot\)
typically - Earth-sized - Electron degeneracy pressure - Slowly cools
forever

\section{\texorpdfstring{High Mass Stars (\textgreater{} 8
\(M_\odot\))}{High Mass Stars (\textgreater{} 8 M\_\textbackslash odot)}}\label{high-mass-stars-8-m_odot}

The path to supernova.

\subsection{Pre-Supernova}\label{pre-supernova}

Multiple burning stages create onion structure: - Each stage shorter
than the last - Iron core grows - Silicon burning lasts only days

\subsection{Core Collapse}\label{core-collapse}

When iron core exceeds Chandrasekhar mass (\textasciitilde1.4
\(M_\odot\)): 1. Electron degeneracy fails 2. Core collapses in
milliseconds 3. Neutronization: p + e\(^-\) \(\to\) n + \(\nu_e\) 4.
Core bounces at nuclear density 5. Shock wave launches

\subsection{The Explosion}\label{the-explosion}

Energy release: approximately \(10^{46}\) J, with 99\% carried away by
neutrinos. The remaining 1\% drives the explosion, launching the stellar
envelope outward at speeds up to 10,000 km/s.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-stellar-fates.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-stellar-fates}The pathways of stellar death:
low-mass stars become white dwarfs via red giant and planetary nebula
phases, while massive stars explode as supernovae leaving neutron stars
or black holes.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Supernova Types}\label{supernova-types}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Mechanism & Spectrum \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Ia & White dwarf thermonuclear & No H, strong Si \\
Ib & Core collapse, no H envelope & No H \\
Ic & Core collapse, no H or He & No H or He \\
II & Core collapse with H & H present \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Remnant}\label{remnant}

What is left: - Neutron star (8-25 \(M_\odot\) progenitor) - Black hole
(\textgreater25 \(M_\odot\) progenitor)

\section{Type Ia Supernovae}\label{type-ia-supernovae}

Special case: exploding white dwarf

\subsection{Mechanism}\label{mechanism-1}

White dwarf in binary system: 1. Accretes matter from companion 2.
Approaches 1.4 \(M_\odot\) 3. Carbon ignites throughout 4. Entire star
explodes

\subsection{Standard Candles}\label{standard-candles}

All Type Ia have similar peak luminosity: - Used to measure cosmic
distances - Led to discovery of dark energy

\section{Supernova Remnants}\label{supernova-remnants}

The debris expands for millennia:

\subsection{Stages}\label{stages}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Free expansion} (few hundred years)
\item
  \textbf{Sedov-Taylor} (thousands of years)
\item
  \textbf{Radiative} (tens of thousands of years)
\item
  \textbf{Merger} with ISM
\end{enumerate}

Famous remnants: Crab Nebula, Cassiopeia A, Vela

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-14}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ stellar\_death(star):}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ star.mass }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{\# Low mass path}
\NormalTok{        star.become\_red\_giant()}
\NormalTok{        star.eject\_planetary\_nebula()}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ WhiteDwarf(star.core\_mass)}

    \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{\# High mass path}
\NormalTok{        star.burn\_all\_stages()}
\NormalTok{        core }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ star.build\_iron\_core()}

        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ core.mass }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ CHANDRASEKHAR:}
\NormalTok{            energy }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ core.collapse()}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Supernova(star, energy)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Red Giant Evolution}\label{red-giant-evolution}

Watch Sun-like star expand into red giant.

\subsection{Supernova Explosion}\label{supernova-explosion}

Watch shock wave propagate through star.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-22}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{red-giant}: Expanded star with shell burning
\item
  \textbf{planetary-nebula}: Expelled envelope of dying star
\item
  \textbf{white-dwarf}: Electron-degenerate remnant
\item
  \textbf{supernova}: Explosive stellar death
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-22}

When massive stars die, they leave behind exotic objects: white dwarfs,
neutron stars, and black holes. Next, we explore these compact objects.

\chapter{Compact Objects}\label{sec-compact-objects}

\emph{``The endpoints of stellar evolution''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Gravity never gives up. White dwarfs, neutron stars, and black holes
represent gravity's victory at progressively higher densities:
electrons, neutrons, and finally nothing can resist.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{White Dwarfs}\label{white-dwarfs}

The end state of Sun-like stars.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-11}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Mass & 0.5-1.4 \(M_\odot\) \\
Radius & \textasciitilde1 \(R_\oplus\) \\
Density & \textasciitilde{}\(10^6\) g/cm\(^3\) \\
Temperature & 10,000-100,000 K (initially) \\
Composition & C, O (mostly) \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Electron Degeneracy}\label{electron-degeneracy-1}

Pauli exclusion: no two electrons in same state - Electrons fill up to
Fermi energy - Pressure from exclusion principle - Independent of
temperature

\subsection{Chandrasekhar Limit}\label{chandrasekhar-limit}

\[
M_{\text{Ch}} = 1.44 \, M_\odot
\]

Above this mass, electron degeneracy fails.

\subsection{Cooling}\label{cooling}

White dwarfs are born hot and cool over billions of years: - No energy
generation - Radiate stored thermal energy - Eventually become black
dwarfs (not yet: universe too young)

\section{Neutron Stars}\label{neutron-stars}

When electron degeneracy fails.

\subsection{Formation}\label{formation-2}

In core collapse: \[
p + e^- \rightarrow n + \nu_e
\]

Protons and electrons combine into neutrons.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-12}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Mass & 1.4-2.5 \(M_\odot\) \\
Radius & \textasciitilde10 km \\
Density & \textasciitilde{}\(10^{14}\) g/cm\(^3\) \\
Surface gravity & \(10^{11}\) g \\
Magnetic field & \(10^8\)-\(10^{15}\) G \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Structure}\label{structure-1}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-compact-objects.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-compact-objects}The internal structure of compact
objects: white dwarfs supported by electron degeneracy, neutron stars
with their exotic layered interiors, and black holes defined by their
event horizons.}

\end{figure}%

A neutron star has distinct layers: an outer crust of iron lattice and
electrons, an inner crust of neutron-rich nuclei with free neutrons
dripping out, an outer core of superfluid neutrons and superconducting
protons, and a mysterious inner core that may contain exotic quark
matter.

\subsection{Pulsars}\label{pulsars}

Rotating neutron stars with beamed radiation emit like cosmic
lighthouses. Their magnetic axes are typically misaligned with their
rotation axes, causing the beams to sweep across space. When Earth lies
in the beam's path, we detect regular pulses.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Periods: milliseconds to seconds
\item
  Extremely regular (some rival atomic clocks)
\item
  Slow down gradually as rotational energy is radiated away
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Magnetars}\label{magnetars}

Neutron stars with extreme magnetic fields: - B \textasciitilde{}
\(10^{14}\)-\(10^{15}\) G - Occasional giant flares - Most magnetized
objects known

\section{Black Holes}\label{black-holes}

When nothing can resist gravity.

\subsection{Formation}\label{formation-3}

From core collapse of very massive stars (\textgreater25 \(M_\odot\)).

\subsection{Event Horizon}\label{event-horizon}

Schwarzschild radius: \[
r_s = \frac{2GM}{c^2} \approx 3 \text{ km} \times \frac{M}{M_\odot}
\]

Nothing, not even light, escapes from within.

\subsection{Types}\label{types-1}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Mass & Formation \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Stellar & 3-100 \(M_\odot\) & Core collapse \\
Intermediate & 100-\(10^5\) \(M_\odot\) & Unknown \\
Supermassive & \(10^6\)-\(10^{10}\) \(M_\odot\) & Galaxy centers \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Properties (Kerr Black
Hole)}\label{properties-kerr-black-hole}

Rotating black holes have: - Event horizon (smaller than non-rotating) -
Ergosphere (region where space is dragged) - Ring singularity (not a
point)

\subsection{Information Paradox}\label{information-paradox}

Quantum mechanics says information cannot be destroyed. Black holes seem
to destroy information. Resolution: Hawking radiation? Holography?

\section{Hawking Radiation}\label{hawking-radiation}

Black holes emit thermal radiation:

\[
T_H = \frac{\hbar c^3}{8\pi G M k_B} \approx 6 \times 10^{-8} \text{ K} \times \frac{M_\odot}{M}
\]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Virtual pairs at horizon: one escapes, one falls in
\item
  Black holes slowly evaporate
\item
  For stellar mass: timescale \textgreater\textgreater{} age of universe
\end{itemize}

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-15}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ CompactObject:}
    \ControlFlowTok{pass}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ WhiteDwarf(CompactObject):}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, mass):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mass }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{min}\NormalTok{(mass, }\FloatTok{1.4}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{\# Chandrasekhar limit}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.radius }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ EARTH\_RADIUS }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1.4} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ mass)}\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.support }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}electron\_degeneracy\textquotesingle{}}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ NeutronStar(CompactObject):}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, mass):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mass }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mass}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.radius }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{10}  \CommentTok{\# km, approximately}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.B\_field }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{10}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{12}  \CommentTok{\# Gauss}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.period }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ initial\_period(mass)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.support }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}neutron\_degeneracy\textquotesingle{}}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ BlackHole(CompactObject):}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, mass):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.mass }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mass}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.r\_s }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ G }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ mass }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ c}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.support }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{None}  \CommentTok{\# Nothing resists}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-23}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{electron-degeneracy}: Quantum pressure from electrons
\item
  \textbf{neutron-degeneracy}: Quantum pressure from neutrons
\item
  \textbf{event-horizon}: Point of no return
\item
  \textbf{hawking-radiation}: Black hole evaporation
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-23}

Stars are the building blocks of galaxies. Now we scale up to explore
how galaxies form and evolve.

\part{Book X: The Galactic Realm}

\chapter{Galaxy Formation}\label{sec-galaxy-formation}

\emph{``From seeds to spirals''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Galaxies form from tiny density fluctuations in the early universe,
amplified by gravity over billions of years. Dark matter provides the
scaffolding.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Produces Galaxy Formation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Galaxy formation emerges from the flux field's response to primordial
perturbations:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Dark matter halos} = unmanifested flux concentrations (s = 0)
  that source gravity
\item
  \textbf{Density fluctuations} = inherited from quantum fluctuations in
  the early flux field
\item
  \textbf{Gravitational instability} = flux gradients amplifying initial
  perturbations
\item
  \textbf{Baryon infall} = manifested matter (s ≠ 0) following the
  gravitational flux wells
\end{itemize}

The cosmic web structure reflects the topology of flux concentrations on
cosmological scales.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Initial Conditions}\label{initial-conditions-1}

\subsection{Density Fluctuations}\label{density-fluctuations}

The early universe was almost, but not quite, uniform: - Quantum
fluctuations during inflation - Stretched to cosmic scales - Density
variations: \(\delta\rho/\rho\) \textasciitilde{} \(10^{-5}\)

\subsection{Seeds}\label{seeds}

These tiny variations are the seeds of all cosmic structure: - Slightly
overdense regions attract more matter - Grow through gravitational
instability - Eventually collapse into galaxies

\section{The Role of Dark Matter}\label{the-role-of-dark-matter}

Dark matter dominates structure formation: - Does not interact with
light (no pressure) - Collapses first - Creates gravitational wells -
Baryons fall in later

\subsection{The Cosmic Web}\label{the-cosmic-web}

Dark matter forms a web-like structure: dense nodes (galaxy clusters)
connected by filaments, with vast voids in between. Galaxies form at the
intersection points where dark matter halos concentrate.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-cosmic-web.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-cosmic-web}The cosmic web structure: dark matter
halos at nodes and along filaments, with galaxies forming in the densest
regions.}

\end{figure}%

\section{Formation Pathways}\label{formation-pathways}

\subsection{Hierarchical Merging (Modern
View)}\label{hierarchical-merging-modern-view}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-galaxy-formation-pathways.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-galaxy-pathways}Galaxy formation pathways: from
small dark matter halos in the early universe through hierarchical
merging to present-day galaxies.}

\end{figure}%

In this bottom-up process, small halos form first and progressively
merge into larger structures over cosmic time.

\subsection{Bottom-Up Process}\label{bottom-up-process}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Dark matter halos form} (z \textasciitilde{} 20-30)
\item
  \textbf{Gas falls in} (cools, condenses)
\item
  \textbf{First stars form} (z \textasciitilde{} 20)
\item
  \textbf{Small galaxies merge} (z \textasciitilde{} 10-2)
\item
  \textbf{Continued growth} (ongoing)
\end{enumerate}

\section{Gas Cooling and Star
Formation}\label{gas-cooling-and-star-formation}

\subsection{Cooling Time}\label{cooling-time}

Gas must cool to collapse: \[
t_{\text{cool}} = \frac{3k_BT n}{2 \Lambda(T) n^2}
\]

Where Λ(T) is the cooling function.

If \(t_{\text{cool}} < t_{\text{Hubble}}\): gas can cool, collapse, form
stars.

\subsection{The Cooling Curve}\label{the-cooling-curve}

Gas cools most efficiently at temperatures between \(10^4\) and \(10^6\)
K, where atomic line cooling dominates. Above this range, bremsstrahlung
(free-free emission) takes over but is less efficient. Below it, the gas
becomes neutral and cooling rates drop sharply.

\section{Angular Momentum}\label{angular-momentum-1}

Galaxies spin because: - Tidal torques from neighbors - Random motions
during collapse - Conservation of angular momentum

\subsection{Disk Formation}\label{disk-formation}

Gas with angular momentum settles into disk: - Falls to center - Spins
up (conservation) - Flattens perpendicular to rotation axis

\section{Feedback}\label{feedback}

Star formation regulates itself:

\subsection{Stellar Feedback}\label{stellar-feedback}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Supernovae heat and expel gas
\item
  Stellar winds inject energy
\item
  UV radiation ionizes gas
\end{itemize}

\subsection{AGN Feedback}\label{agn-feedback}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Central black hole accretes
\item
  Jets and outflows
\item
  Can quench star formation
\end{itemize}

\section{Galaxy Assembly}\label{galaxy-assembly}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ galaxy\_formation(dark\_matter\_halo):}
    \CommentTok{\# Baryons fall into dark matter well}
\NormalTok{    gas }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ accrete\_baryons(halo)}

    \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ universe.age }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{13.8}\ErrorTok{\_billion\_years}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{\# Gas cools}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ cooling\_time(gas) }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ hubble\_time():}
\NormalTok{            gas.cool()}
\NormalTok{            gas.condense()}

        \CommentTok{\# Stars form from dense gas}
\NormalTok{        stars }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ form\_stars(dense\_gas)}

        \CommentTok{\# Feedback regulates}
\NormalTok{        feedback(stars, gas)}
\NormalTok{        feedback(central\_black\_hole, gas)}

        \CommentTok{\# Mergers}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ merger\_event():}
\NormalTok{            merge\_with(other\_galaxy)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Halo Collapse}\label{halo-collapse}

Watch dark matter halo form from initial fluctuations.

\subsection{Disk Formation}\label{disk-formation-1}

See gas settle into rotating disk.

\subsection{Merger Simulation}\label{merger-simulation}

Collide two galaxies. Watch the result.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-24}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{hierarchical-merging}: Small objects combine into larger
\item
  \textbf{dark-matter-halo}: Invisible gravitational scaffold
\item
  \textbf{cooling-flow}: Gas radiating energy and condensing
\item
  \textbf{feedback}: Self-regulation of star formation
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-24}

Galaxies form from the same processes at every mass scale. Now we
explore the variety of galaxies that result: spirals, ellipticals, and
more.

\chapter{Galaxy Types}\label{sec-galaxy-types}

\emph{``The cosmic menagerie''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Galaxies come in distinct types, but these are not fixed categories:
they represent different evolutionary states, shaped by mergers,
environment, and star formation history.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Hubble Classification}\label{the-hubble-classification}

\subsection{Hubble's Tuning Fork}\label{hubbles-tuning-fork}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-hubble-fork.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-hubble-fork}The Hubble tuning fork classification:
ellipticals on the left, lenticulars at the junction, and spirals
branching into normal and barred types on the right.}

\end{figure}%

This is NOT an evolutionary sequence, but rather a classification scheme
based on morphology.

\section{Elliptical Galaxies (E)}\label{elliptical-galaxies-e}

Smooth, featureless spheroids.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-13}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Description \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Shape & Spheroidal to elongated \\
Stars & Old, red \\
Gas & Very little \\
Star formation & None (dead) \\
Population & \textasciitilde15\% of galaxies \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Classification}\label{classification}

E0 to E7 based on apparent ellipticity: - E0: Circular - E7: Most
elongated

\subsection{Examples}\label{examples-1}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  M87 (giant elliptical in Virgo)
\item
  NGC 1132 (fossil group)
\end{itemize}

\section{Spiral Galaxies (S)}\label{spiral-galaxies-s}

Disk galaxies with spiral arms.

\subsection{Structure}\label{structure-2}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-spiral-structure.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-spiral-structure}Spiral galaxy structure showing the
disk with spiral arms, central bulge, and surrounding dark matter halo.}

\end{figure}%

Spiral galaxies consist of a thin rotating disk containing spiral arms,
a central bulge of older stars, and an extended spherical halo
containing old stars, globular clusters, and dark matter.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-14}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Description \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Structure & Disk + bulge + halo \\
Stars & Mix of old and young \\
Gas & Significant in disk \\
Star formation & In spiral arms \\
Population & \textasciitilde70\% of bright galaxies \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Subtypes}\label{subtypes}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Bulge & Arms & Gas \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Sa & Large & Tight & Less \\
Sb & Medium & Moderate & Medium \\
Sc & Small & Open & More \\
Sd & Tiny & Very open & Most \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Barred Spirals (SB)}\label{barred-spirals-sb}

Central bar connects to spiral arms: - Possibly a phase, not permanent -
Affects gas flow - Milky Way is SBbc

\section{Lenticular Galaxies (S0)}\label{lenticular-galaxies-s0}

Intermediate between elliptical and spiral.

\subsection{Properties}\label{properties-15}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Disk but no arms
\item
  Little gas or star formation
\item
  Old stellar population
\item
  Possibly ``faded'' spirals
\end{itemize}

\section{Irregular Galaxies (Irr)}\label{irregular-galaxies-irr}

No regular structure.

\subsection{Types}\label{types-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Irr I}: Some structure visible
\item
  \textbf{Irr II}: Chaotic, often interacting
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Examples}\label{examples-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Large/Small Magellanic Clouds
\item
  Many dwarf galaxies
\end{itemize}

\section{Galaxy Properties}\label{galaxy-properties}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Mass & Diameter & Example \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Giant elliptical & \(10^{13}\) \(M_\odot\) & 300 kpc & M87 \\
Large spiral & \(10^{11}\) \(M_\odot\) & 30 kpc & Milky Way \\
Dwarf elliptical & \(10^7\) \(M_\odot\) & 1 kpc & Many \\
Dwarf irregular & \(10^8\) \(M_\odot\) & 3 kpc & SMC \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Why Different Types?}\label{why-different-types}

\subsection{Formation History}\label{formation-history}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Gas-rich mergers → spirals
\item
  Gas-poor mergers → ellipticals
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Environment}\label{environment}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Clusters → more ellipticals
\item
  Field → more spirals
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Transformation}\label{transformation}

Spirals can become ellipticals through: - Major mergers - Ram pressure
stripping - Strangulation (cut off from gas)

\section{The Morphology-Density
Relation}\label{the-morphology-density-relation}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-morphology-density-relation.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-morph-density}The morphology-density relation:
elliptical galaxies dominate in dense cluster environments, while
spirals are more common in the field.}

\end{figure}%

Dense environments such as galaxy cluster centers are dominated by
elliptical and lenticular galaxies, while spiral and irregular galaxies
are more common in lower-density field environments.

\subsection{Galaxy Zoo}\label{galaxy-zoo}

Classify galaxy images by type.

\subsection{Merger Product}\label{merger-product}

Predict outcome of spiral + spiral collision.

\subsection{Environment Effect}\label{environment-effect}

Place galaxy in cluster. Watch transformation.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-25}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{elliptical}: Smooth, spheroidal, old stars
\item
  \textbf{spiral}: Disk with arms, ongoing star formation
\item
  \textbf{lenticular}: Disk without arms
\item
  \textbf{irregular}: No clear structure
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-25}

The Milky Way is our home galaxy: a barred spiral. We now explore our
cosmic neighborhood in detail.

\chapter{The Milky Way}\label{sec-milky-way}

\emph{``Our cosmic home''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

We live inside a galaxy: a barred spiral of several hundred billion
stars, with a supermassive black hole at its center. The Milky Way is
our address in the universe.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{How FTD Produces Galactic Structure}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

The Milky Way's structure emerges from flux dynamics at galactic scales:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Spiral arms} = density waves in the stellar flux, triggering
  star formation as material passes through
\item
  \textbf{Dark matter halo} = unmanifested flux (s = 0) providing the
  gravitational scaffold
\item
  \textbf{Central black hole} = extreme flux concentration where nothing
  escapes
\item
  \textbf{Rotation curve} = flat because dark matter flux dominates
  beyond the visible disk
\end{itemize}

The barred spiral morphology reflects the angular momentum distribution
of the primordial flux cloud.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Basic Properties}\label{basic-properties}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Property & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Type & SBbc (barred spiral) \\
Diameter & \textasciitilde100,000 light-years \\
Thickness & \textasciitilde1,000 ly (disk) \\
Mass & \textasciitilde1.5 trillion \(M_\odot\) (with dark matter) \\
Stars & 100-400 billion \\
Age & \textasciitilde13.6 billion years \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Structure}\label{structure-3}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-milky-way-structure.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-milky-way-structure}The structure of the Milky Way:
edge-on and face-on views showing the thin disk, thick disk, central
bulge with bar, spiral arms, and surrounding halo.}

\end{figure}%

The Milky Way has a complex multi-component structure: a thin disk where
most star formation occurs, a thicker disk of older stars, a central
bulge with a bar, and an extended spherical halo containing old stars
and dark matter.

\section{Components}\label{components}

\subsection{Thin Disk}\label{thin-disk}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Contains most visible stars
\item
  Young to intermediate age
\item
  Star formation ongoing
\item
  Circular orbits
\item
  Scale height: \textasciitilde300 pc
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Thick Disk}\label{thick-disk}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Older stars
\item
  More vertical extent
\item
  Scale height: \textasciitilde1000 pc
\item
  Formed early or from mergers
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Bulge/Bar}\label{bulgebar}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Central concentration
\item
  Bar \textasciitilde10,000 ly long
\item
  Mix of old and young stars
\item
  Contains central black hole
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Halo}\label{halo}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Spheroidal distribution
\item
  Old, metal-poor stars
\item
  Globular clusters (\textasciitilde150)
\item
  Extends to \textasciitilde300,000 ly
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Dark Matter Halo}\label{dark-matter-halo}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Dominates mass
\item
  Extends to \textasciitilde600,000 ly
\item
  \textasciitilde85\% of total mass
\item
  Inferred from rotation curve
\end{itemize}

\section{Spiral Arms}\label{spiral-arms}

\subsection{Major Arms}\label{major-arms}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Arm & Distance from Sun \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Perseus & 6,000 ly \\
Sagittarius & 8,000 ly \\
Scutum-Centaurus & 12,000 ly \\
Norma & 15,000 ly \\
\end{longtable}

Our Sun is in the Orion Spur, between Perseus and Sagittarius.

\subsection{Why Arms?}\label{why-arms}

Density wave theory: - Arms are waves, not material structures - Stars
and gas enter and exit - Compression triggers star formation - Young,
bright stars make arms visible

\section{The Galactic Center}\label{the-galactic-center}

\subsection{Sagittarius A*}\label{sagittarius-a}

Supermassive black hole: - Mass: 4.1 million \(M_\odot\) - Distance:
26,000 ly - Schwarzschild radius: \textasciitilde12 million km -
Currently quiescent

\subsection{Central Parsec}\label{central-parsec}

Extreme environment: - High stellar density - Young massive stars
(unexpected!) - Streamers of hot gas - Strong magnetic fields

\section{Solar Neighborhood}\label{solar-neighborhood}

\subsection{The Sun's Location}\label{the-suns-location}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  26,000 ly from center
\item
  In thin disk
\item
  Orion Spur
\item
  Moving at 220 km/s around center
\item
  Orbital period: \textasciitilde230 million years
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Nearby Stars}\label{nearby-stars}

Within 20 light-years: - \textasciitilde100 known stellar systems -
Proxima Centauri: 4.24 ly - Barnard's Star: 5.96 ly - Sirius: 8.6 ly

\section{Satellite Galaxies}\label{satellite-galaxies}

The Milky Way has \textasciitilde60 known satellites:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Galaxy & Distance & Mass \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Large Magellanic Cloud & 160,000 ly & \(10^{10}\) \(M_\odot\) \\
Small Magellanic Cloud & 200,000 ly & \(7 \times 10^9\) \(M_\odot\) \\
Sagittarius Dwarf & 70,000 ly & \(4 \times 10^8\) \(M_\odot\) \\
Ursa Minor Dwarf & 200,000 ly & \(2 \times 10^7\) \(M_\odot\) \\
\end{longtable}

Sagittarius is currently being cannibalized.

\section{Future}\label{future}

\subsection{Andromeda Collision}\label{andromeda-collision}

In \textasciitilde4.5 billion years: - Milky Way and Andromeda merge -
Will form elliptical galaxy (``Milkomeda'') - Sun may be flung to
outskirts - No direct stellar collisions expected

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-16}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ MilkyWay:}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.disk }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ThinDisk(radius}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{50000}\NormalTok{, mass}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{6e10}\NormalTok{)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bulge }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Bulge(radius}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{3000}\NormalTok{, mass}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e10}\NormalTok{)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.halo }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ DarkMatterHalo(radius}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{300000}\NormalTok{, mass}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e12}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{        v\_circular }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{220}  \CommentTok{\# km/s}

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-26}

\subsection{Galactic Tour}\label{galactic-tour}

Fly through the Milky Way. Visit center, arms, halo.

\subsection{Rotation Curve}\label{rotation-curve}

Measure velocities at different radii. See flat curve (dark matter
evidence).

\subsection{Future Merger}\label{future-merger}

Watch Milky Way and Andromeda collide.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-26}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{spiral-arms}: Density wave star formation regions
\item
  \textbf{galactic-center}: Location of supermassive black hole
\item
  \textbf{rotation-curve}: Velocity vs radius (flat = dark matter)
\item
  \textbf{satellite-galaxies}: Small galaxies orbiting larger
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-26}

Our galaxy does not exist in isolation. Galaxies interact, merge, and
transform. Next, we explore these cosmic collisions.

\chapter{Galaxy Interactions}\label{sec-galaxy-interactions}

\emph{``Cosmic collisions''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Galaxies do not just drift through space: they collide, merge, and
transform each other. These interactions drive galaxy evolution and
trigger dramatic starbursts.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Types of Interactions}\label{types-of-interactions}

\subsection{Flyby}\label{flyby}

Close passage without merger: - Tidal distortions - Triggered star
formation - Can repeat (bound orbit)

\subsection{Minor Merger}\label{minor-merger}

Small galaxy absorbed by large: - Small galaxy disrupted - Large galaxy
barely affected - Adds stars to halo - Common occurrence

\subsection{Major Merger}\label{major-merger}

Two comparable galaxies combine: - Both heavily disrupted - Dramatic
morphological change - Usually produces elliptical - Rare but
transformative

\section{Tidal Effects}\label{tidal-effects}

Gravity from passing galaxy distorts structure, stretching material
outward on both the near and far sides to form tidal tails.

\subsection{Tidal Tails}\label{tidal-tails}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Stretched material on two sides
\item
  Can extend hundreds of thousands of light-years
\item
  Stars, gas, and dark matter
\item
  Examples: Mice Galaxies, Antennae
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Tidal Dwarf Galaxies}\label{tidal-dwarf-galaxies}

Dense clumps in tidal tails can become small galaxies: - Formed from
debris - Lack dark matter - Short-lived?

\section{Merger Sequence}\label{merger-sequence}

\subsection{Stage 1: Approach}\label{stage-1-approach}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Galaxies detect each other gravitationally
\item
  Orbits modified
\item
  No visible interaction yet
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Stage 2: First Passage}\label{stage-2-first-passage}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Tidal tails form
\item
  Bars may be triggered
\item
  Some gas driven to centers
\item
  First starburst
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Stage 3: Separation}\label{stage-3-separation}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Galaxies move apart
\item
  Tidal features stretch
\item
  Dynamic friction slows them
\item
  Will return
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Stage 4: Final Coalescence}\label{stage-4-final-coalescence}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Galaxies merge centers
\item
  Violent relaxation
\item
  Black holes merge
\item
  Major starburst
\item
  AGN possibly triggered
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Stage 5: Relaxation}\label{stage-5-relaxation}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Merged remnant settles
\item
  Usually elliptical shape
\item
  Star formation fades
\item
  ``Post-starburst'' phase
\end{itemize}

\section{Starbursts}\label{starbursts}

Mergers trigger extreme star formation:

\subsection{Mechanism}\label{mechanism-2}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Gas loses angular momentum
\item
  Flows to galaxy centers
\item
  High density → rapid star formation
\item
  Rates 10-100× normal
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Ultra-Luminous Infrared Galaxies
(ULIRGs)}\label{ultra-luminous-infrared-galaxies-ulirgs}

Most extreme starbursts: - \(L > 10^{12} L_\odot\) - Heavily
dust-obscured - Shine in infrared - Often merging systems

\section{The Fate of Supermassive Black
Holes}\label{the-fate-of-supermassive-black-holes}

When galaxies merge, so do their SMBHs:

\subsection{Process}\label{process}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Black holes sink to merger center (dynamical friction)
\item
  Form binary (separation \textasciitilde parsec)
\item
  Binary hardens (ejects stars)
\item
  ``Final parsec problem'': how do they get closer?
\item
  Eventually merge → gravitational waves
\end{enumerate}

\section{Environmental Effects}\label{environmental-effects}

\subsection{Ram Pressure Stripping}\label{ram-pressure-stripping}

In galaxy clusters, galaxies moving through the hot intracluster medium
experience ram pressure that strips gas from their disks, like wind
blowing through a dandelion. This can completely remove a galaxy's gas
reservoir, quenching star formation.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-galaxy-merger-sequence.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-merger-sequence}Galaxy merger sequence: from initial
approach through tidal distortion, starburst, and final coalescence into
an elliptical remnant.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Harassment}\label{harassment}

Repeated high-speed encounters in clusters: - Heats disk stars -
Thickens disk - Eventually transforms spiral → S0

\subsection{Strangulation}\label{strangulation}

Cut off from fresh gas supply: - Existing gas consumed - No new star
formation - Slow fading to red

\section{Famous Interactions}\label{famous-interactions}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
System & Type & Distance \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Antennae & Major merger & 45 Mly \\
Mice & Early merger & 290 Mly \\
Whirlpool (M51) & Interaction & 23 Mly \\
Cartwheel & Ring galaxy & 500 Mly \\
\end{longtable}

\section{In Simulation}\label{in-simulation-17}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ galaxy\_merger(gal1, gal2):}
    \CommentTok{\# Initial approach}
    \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ separation(gal1, gal2) }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ tidal\_radius:}
\NormalTok{        evolve\_orbits(gal1, gal2)}

    \CommentTok{\# Interaction phase}
    \ControlFlowTok{while} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ merged(gal1, gal2):}
        \CommentTok{\# Tidal forces}
\NormalTok{        apply\_tides(gal1, gal2)}

        \CommentTok{\# Gas dynamics}
\NormalTok{        drive\_gas\_inflow(gal1)}
\NormalTok{        drive\_gas\_inflow(gal2)}

        \CommentTok{\# Star formation}
\NormalTok{        starburst(gal1.center)}
\NormalTok{        starburst(gal2.center)}

        \CommentTok{\# Dynamical friction}
\NormalTok{        slow\_relative\_motion(gal1, gal2)}

    \CommentTok{\# Coalescence}
\NormalTok{    remnant }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ combine(gal1, gal2)}
\NormalTok{    merge\_black\_holes(gal1.smbh, gal2.smbh)}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ remnant}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Experiments}\label{experiments-27}

\subsection{Collision Simulator}\label{collision-simulator-1}

Set up two galaxies. Watch them merge over billions of years.

\subsection{Tidal Tail Formation}\label{tidal-tail-formation}

Observe how tidal tails develop during close passage.

\subsection{Starburst Trigger}\label{starburst-trigger}

Measure star formation rate through merger stages.

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-27}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{tidal-interaction}: Gravitational distortion
\item
  \textbf{major-merger}: Comparable mass combination
\item
  \textbf{starburst}: Intense star formation episode
\item
  \textbf{ram-pressure-stripping}: Gas removal by hot ICM
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-27}

Galaxies are embedded in larger structures. Now we scale up to the
cosmic realm: the large-scale structure of the universe itself.

\part{Book XI: The Cosmic Realm}

\chapter{Large-Scale Structure}\label{sec-large-scale-structure}

\emph{``The cosmic web''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Galaxies are not randomly scattered: they form a vast web of filaments,
walls, and voids. This pattern is the largest structure in the universe,
written in dark matter.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Cosmic Web}\label{the-cosmic-web-1}

At the largest scales, the universe has structure:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-cosmic-web-survey-slice.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-cosmic-web-large-scale}The cosmic web: nodes (galaxy
clusters) connected by filaments (galaxy chains) surrounding vast empty
voids.}

\end{figure}%

Nodes are where filaments intersect: these are the galaxy clusters.
Filaments are chains of galaxies connecting the nodes. Voids are the
nearly empty regions between.

\section{Components}\label{components-1}

\subsection{Nodes (Clusters)}\label{nodes-clusters}

Where filaments intersect: - Galaxy clusters - Highest density regions -
100-1000 galaxies - Mass: \(10^{14}\)-\(10^{15}\) \(M_\odot\) - Size:
2-10 Mpc

\section{Evidence for Dark Matter}\label{evidence-for-dark-matter}

\subsection{Galaxy Rotation Curves}\label{galaxy-rotation-curves}

Expected (Keplerian): \[
v(r) \propto r^{-1/2}
\]

Observed: \[
v(r) \approx \text{constant at large } r
\]

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/../../media/images/fig-dark-matter-evidence.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-dm-evidence}Evidence for dark matter: rotation
curves show flat velocities at large radii instead of the expected
Keplerian decline.}

\end{figure}%

This discrepancy between expected and observed rotation curves requires
additional unseen mass.

\subsection{Galaxy Cluster Masses}\label{galaxy-cluster-masses}

Multiple methods give consistent results: - Virial theorem:
\(M = \frac{3\sigma^2 R}{G}\) - X-ray gas: Hydrostatic equilibrium -
Gravitational lensing: Light bending

All give: \(M_{\text{total}} >> M_{\text{visible}}\)

\subsection{Gravitational Lensing}\label{gravitational-lensing}

Mass bends light: - Strong lensing: Multiple images, arcs - Weak
lensing: Statistical distortions - Maps invisible mass directly

\subsection{The Bullet Cluster}\label{the-bullet-cluster}

Two clusters collided, providing smoking-gun evidence for dark matter.
The galaxies passed through each other largely unaffected. The hot gas
collided and was left behind at the collision point. But gravitational
lensing reveals that most of the mass (dark matter) passed through like
the galaxies, now separated from the gas. This proves dark matter is a
distinct component from ordinary matter.

\subsection{CMB Anisotropies}\label{cmb-anisotropies}

Pattern of fluctuations requires dark matter: - Baryons alone do not
match - Need \textasciitilde5× more non-baryonic matter - Confirmed by
Planck satellite

\section{The FTD Interpretation}\label{the-ftd-interpretation}

In FTD, dark matter is not a new particle species: it is a property of
the \textbf{self-field halo} surrounding every manifested particle.
