\section{A}\label{a}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Accretion}]
The accumulation of matter by gravitational attraction. In FTD,
particles clump when density gradients exert net force toward centers.
\item[\textbf{Annihilation}]
The collision of matter (+1) and antimatter (-1) particles, both
returning to void (0) and releasing their energy as flux waves.
\item[\textbf{Anthropic Principle}]
The observation that the universe's constants must permit observers,
since we exist to observe them.
\item[\textbf{Antimatter}]
State -1 particles. Equivalent to matter in all ways except opposite
charge/polarity. Annihilates with matter upon contact.
\item[\textbf{Alpha Power Ladder}]
The hierarchical sequence of α powers encoding fundamental mass and
energy scales: α⁸ (Higgs VEV), α¹¹ (electron mass), α¹⁶ (vacuum energy),
α²⁰ (gravitational coupling). The gaps \{3, 5, 4\} between exponents
encode framework integers.
\item[\textbf{Alpha Precision Formula}]
The correction formula achieving \textbf{0.21 ppt} accuracy for
\(\alpha\):
\(1/\alpha = x_+ - (9/47)|\varepsilon| + (5/64)|\varepsilon|^2\) where
\(\varepsilon = e^\pi - \pi - 20 \approx -9 \times 10^{-4}\). The
correction term 9/47 combines framework integers (47 = N\_c × N\_base² -
1 = 3×16-1), and remarkably \textbar ε\textbar{} ≈ 1/1111 where 1111 =
(b₃+N\_base)(8N\_eff-N\_c) = 11×101. The constant 20 = b₃ + N\_eff = 7 +
13 = 1/c\_fermion connects to the Weyl anomaly coefficient from
conformal field theory. This 6,000× improvement over the base 1.26 ppm
derivation represents the most precise theoretical prediction of α ever
achieved.
\end{description}

\section{B}\label{b}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{b₃ (QCD Beta Coefficient)}]
Framework integer = 7. The first coefficient in the QCD beta function
for SU(3). Appears in electron mass (70 = \(b_3(b_3+N_c)\)) and
gravitational coupling (\(1/(b_3+N_c)^2\)).
\item[\textbf{Born Rule (as Translation Protocol)}]
The interpretation of the Born rule P = \textbar ψ\textbar² not as a
mysterious postulate but as the translation protocol between Domain A
(configurations) and Domain B (probabilities). The squared amplitude is
what remains invariant under domain crossing.
\item[\textbf{Bridge Operator (𝒯)}]
The mathematical structure that translates between Domain A
(ontic/classical) and Domain B (epistemic/probabilistic). In quantum
mechanics, the Born rule P = \textbar ψ\textbar² serves as the bridge
operator. See also: Domain A, Domain B.
\item[\textbf{Baryon}]
A composite particle made of three quarks (triad configuration).
Includes protons and neutrons.
\item[\textbf{Bell Test}]
Experimental verification of quantum correlations exceeding classical
bounds. Measures S parameter (classical ≤2, quantum ≤2√2).
\item[\textbf{Big Bang}]
The initial genesis event from which the universe emerged. In FTD,
initial flux creates conditions for widespread particle genesis.
\item[\textbf{Binding}]
The locking of particles into stable structures (triads, shells). Bound
particles resist decay.
\item[\textbf{Bounded Orbit (Mandelbrot)}]
An orbit in the Mandelbrot iteration that remains finite forever. Points
with bounded orbits lie inside the set. In FTD, analogous to conscious
processes that sustain themselves indefinitely - the iteration keeps
going.
\item[\textbf{Black Hole}]
A region where density is so high that the escape velocity exceeds C.
Particles entering cannot leave.
\end{description}

\section{C}\label{c}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{C (Speed of Causality)}]
Fundamental constant = 1.0 voxel/tick. The maximum speed at which any
influence can propagate. Nothing exceeds C.
\item[\textbf{Casimir Effect}]
Attractive force between close surfaces due to excluded vacuum
fluctuation modes. Demonstrates reality of zero-point energy.
\item[\textbf{Ce₂Zr₂O₇ (Cerium Zirconium Oxide)}]
Pyrochlore material exhibiting quantum spin ice behavior. In 2025,
confirmed as the first material showing emergent photon-like excitations
from discrete lattice dynamics, validating FTD's core mechanism of gauge
boson emergence from constraint structure.
\item[\textbf{CP Violation (in Baryons)}]
Asymmetry between matter and antimatter decay rates in three-quark
systems. First observed in baryons by LHCb in 2025 (Λb⁰ → pK⁻π⁺π⁻,
5.2σ). FTD derives CP violation from integer ratios: δ\_CP = arctan(7/3)
≈ 66.8°.
\item[\textbf{Calorimeter}]
Detector measuring particle energy by total absorption. Used in particle
physics experiments.
\item[\textbf{Cantor Dust (Julia Set)}]
The Julia set for parameters c outside the Mandelbrot set. A totally
disconnected set of points: the ``dust'' that remains when bounded
orbits become infinitely rare. In FTD, the consciousness regime (k =
0.5, c ≈ 0.676) produces Cantor dust Julia sets, representing the open,
indeterminate nature of conscious dynamics.
\item[\textbf{Consciousness Quadratic}]
The master quadratic evaluated at k = 0.5 (consciousness regime): y² -
(G\emph{)²y/2 + (G})³/2 = 0. Yields \textbf{complex roots} y = 2.19 ±
2.86i, placing consciousness outside the Mandelbrot set in a
fundamentally different ontological regime from physics (which has real
roots).
\item[\textbf{Consciousness Regime}]
The parameter region k = 0.5 in the TRD-Mandelbrot framework.
Characterized by: Mandelbrot parameter c ≈ 0.676 (outside the set),
complex quadratic roots, escaping orbits, Cantor dust Julia sets, and
open/indeterminate dynamics. Contrasts with the physics regime (k = 16,
inside the set, real roots, bounded orbits).
\item[\textbf{Critical Coefficient (k\_c)}]
The value k\_c = 4/G* ≈ 1.352 at which the TRD-Mandelbrot transformation
maps to the cardioid cusp c = 1/4. The boundary between bounded and
unbounded regimes. Satisfies the exact identity k\_c × c\_cusp × G* = 1.
\item[\textbf{Chain of Necessity}]
The dependency chain used in this book to organize which results rely on
which inputs. Where the text asserts ``uniqueness,'' it is always meant
relative to an explicitly stated constraint class or search space (see
the Assumption Ledger).
\item[\textbf{Charge}]
A property that determines electromagnetic interaction. In FTD, derived
from quark composition (±1/3, ±2/3).
\item[\textbf{Complexity}]
Properties of wholes that emerge from but are not present in parts.
Arises from interactions between many components.
\item[\textbf{Conformal Anomaly (c\_fermion)}]
The trace anomaly coefficient in conformal field theory for a Weyl
fermion: c\_fermion = 1/20. In FTD, the reciprocal 20 = b₃ + N\_eff = 7
+ 13 appears in the alpha precision formula as the constant in ε =
e\^{}π - π - 20. This remarkable connection between framework integers
and CFT provides independent theoretical support for the precision
formula.
\item[\textbf{Curl}]
Vector differential operator measuring rotation in a field. Used for
magnetic force calculations.
\end{description}

\section{D}\label{d}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Distinction}]
The primitive epistemic act of marking one thing as different from
another. In EPL-ST, distinction is the absolute foundation from which
all else follows, prior to logic, truth, or existence. The ability to
distinguish ``this'' from ``that'' is the most basic operation of any
epistemic agent. From distinction alone, the ternary valuation \{+1, −1,
0\} follows as a theorem. See \textbf{?@sec-formal-logic}.
\item[\textbf{Dark Energy}]
The component driving accelerating expansion. In FTD, emergent from
dynamic void flux: ρ\_DE = ½⟨J²⟩\_vacuum. DESI 2025 data (4.2σ) shows w
evolves over cosmic time, consistent with FTD prediction that void flux
is dynamic, not constant.
\item[\textbf{Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument (DESI)}]
Spectroscopic survey instrument at Kitt Peak National Observatory. DR2
(2025) provided 4.2σ evidence for evolving dark energy (w₀ ≈ −0.7, wₐ ≈
−1), challenging the cosmological constant and supporting FTD's dynamic
void interpretation.
\item[\textbf{Dispositional Ontology}]
The philosophical position that reality is fundamentally composed of
dispositions (tendencies, potentials) rather than categorical
properties. In FTD, the flux field J represents dispositional properties
of the void substrate.
\item[\textbf{Domain A (𝒜)}]
The ontic/classical domain of configurations. Contains definite states,
actual arrangements, and physical structures. Distinguished from Domain
B by containing no probabilities, only actualities. The substrate of
what \emph{is}.
\item[\textbf{Domain B (ℬ)}]
The epistemic/probabilistic domain. Contains probability distributions,
knowledge states, and information about systems. Distinguished from
Domain A by containing no definite configurations, only distributions
over possibilities. The domain of what is \emph{known} or
\emph{knowable}.
\item[\textbf{Domain Confusion}]
The philosophical error of projecting properties appropriate to one
domain onto another. Examples include treating probabilities as physical
substances (B→A error) or treating configurations as merely epistemic
(A→B error).
\item[\textbf{Domain Interpenetration}]
The property that Domain A and Domain B exist within each other:
physical structures in A can encode information (B content), while
probability distributions in B describe configurations in A. Neither
domain is more fundamental.
\item[\textbf{Dark Matter}]
Non-luminous matter detected only by gravitational effects. In FTD,
potentially particles that interact gravitationally but not
electromagnetically.
\item[\textbf{Decay}]
The gradual reduction of flux magnitude. Controlled by DECAY\_RATE.
Bound particles have suppressed decay.
\item[\textbf{Density}]
The magnitude of the flux vector: ρ = \textbar J\textbar. Determines
genesis probability when above KB.
\item[\textbf{Divergence}]
Scalar field measuring flux source/sink at a point. Positive divergence
→ matter genesis; negative → antimatter.
\end{description}

\section{E}\label{e}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Electromagnetism}]
Force between charged particles. Arises from charge gradients in FTD.
\item[\textbf{EPL-ST (Epistemic-Physical Logic with Sorts and Times)}]
The formal logical framework underlying FTD. Establishes six primitive
sorts (Observers, Epistemic States, Propositions, Distinctions, Events,
Worlds), proves ternary valuation is necessary, and derives the
requirement for 3D space from stable embedding conditions. See
\textbf{?@sec-formal-logic}.
\item[\textbf{Epistemic Extension}]
A mapping φ: E → E′ where new distinctions may be added while preserving
all prior distinctions consistently. The requirement that epistemic
extension be stable leads to the theorem that d ≥ 3 dimensions are
necessary.
\item[\textbf{Electron Electric Dipole Moment (eEDM)}]
A measure of the asymmetric charge distribution along the electron's
spin axis. A non-zero eEDM would indicate CP violation beyond the
Standard Model. The 2025 JILA measurement set the world's best limit:
\textbar d\_e\textbar{} \textless{} 4.1 × 10⁻³⁰ e·cm. FTD predicts d\_e
≈ 0 for fundamental manifestations, consistent with observation.
\item[\textbf{Encoding Relation}]
The mathematical map ε: A → B that extracts epistemic content
(information, meaning) from physical structure. A book encodes
information; a brain encodes beliefs. The encoding relation is how
Domain A structures can ``contain'' Domain B content.
\item[\textbf{Epistemic Inertia}]
Resistance to belief change proportional to the ``mass'' of evidence
supporting current beliefs. Analogous to physical inertia but in Domain
B. See also: Noetic Mass.
\item[\textbf{Epistemic Physical Object}]
A configuration in Domain A that encodes content from Domain B.
Examples: books (encode information), brains (encode beliefs),
measurement devices (encode measurement results). The mechanism by which
domains interpenetrate.
\item[\textbf{Edge of Chaos}]
The critical boundary between ordered (predictable) and chaotic (random)
regimes where maximum computational capability exists. Visualized
perfectly by the Mandelbrot set boundary: inside is order, outside is
chaos, the boundary itself is where all complexity lives. In FTD, the
locus of consciousness.
\item[\textbf{Eight Thousand One Hundred Twenty-Eight (8128 = T(127))}]
The 127th triangular number. Remarkably, 8128 is the \textbf{fourth
perfect number} (equals the sum of its proper divisors:
1+2+4+\ldots+4064 = 8128). Appears in bottom quark mass: m\_b/m\_e =
T(127) + 42 = 8128 + 42 = 8170.
\item[\textbf{Eight Thousand One Hundred Seventy (8170)}]
Bottom/electron mass ratio = T(127) + 42 = 8128 + 42 = 8170. Combines
the perfect number 8128 with the bridge constant 42. Error: 0.12\%.
\item[\textbf{Eleven Hundred Eleven (1111)}]
The quantum correction denominator in the alpha precision formula: 1111
= 11 × 101 = (b₃ + N\_base)(8N\_eff - N\_c) = (7+4)(8×13-3) = 11 × 101.
Remarkably, \textbar ε\textbar{} = \textbar e\^{}π - π - 20\textbar{} ≈
1/1111 to high precision. All four framework integers \{3, 4, 7, 13\}
participate in this single number, suggesting it represents a
fundamental coupling constant. The factorization into twin primes 11 and
101 may have deeper significance.
\item[\textbf{Emergence}]
The appearance of novel properties at higher levels of organization.
Atoms have wetness; electrons do not.
\item[\textbf{Emergent Gauge Bosons}]
Gauge field quanta (photon-like excitations) that arise from collective
dynamics of an underlying discrete system rather than being fundamental.
Confirmed in quantum spin ice (Ce₂Zr₂O₇, 2025), validating FTD's core
mechanism where photons emerge from flux field dynamics on a lattice.
\item[\textbf{Entanglement}]
Correlation between particles created together (pair production). Shared
UUID indicates common origin.
\item[\textbf{Entropy}]
Measure of disorder or uncertainty. Always increases in isolated systems
(Second Law).
\item[\textbf{Eight (8 = 2\^{}N\_c)}]
Derived integer = 2³. Appears as the denominator in the W boson mass
formula (8α²) where it encodes SU(2) weak isospin structure. Also equals
Fibonacci F₆.
\item[\textbf{Escaping Orbit (Mandelbrot)}]
An orbit in the Mandelbrot iteration that diverges to infinity. Points
with escaping orbits lie outside the set. In FTD, analogous to processes
that cannot sustain themselves - the iteration stops, returning to void.
\item[\textbf{Evaporation}]
Transition from manifested state (±1) to void (0) when density drops
below KB.
\item[\textbf{Event Horizon}]
Boundary around a black hole beyond which nothing can escape.
\end{description}

\section{F}\label{f}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Fibonacci Skeleton}]
The underlying structure of FTD's framework integers as Fibonacci
numbers. F₄=3=N\_c, F₆=8=2\^{}N\_c, F₇=13=N\_eff, F₁₀=55=T(10). The
effective dimension equals the Fibonacci of the loop length: N\_eff =
F(b₃).
\item[\textbf{Fifty-Five (55 = T(10))}]
The tenth triangular number = 10 × 11/2 = 55. Also Fibonacci F₁₀.
Appears in the proton mass formula: m\_p/m\_e = N\_eff/α + T(b₃ + N\_c)
= 13 × 137 + 55 ≈ 1836.
\item[\textbf{Fine Structure Constant (α)}]
Dimensionless constant = 1/137.036 (0.007297). \textbf{DERIVED} from the
master quadratic as the larger root. Controls electromagnetic strength.
Base accuracy: 1.26 ppm; \textbf{Precision formula: 0.21 ppt} (6,000×
better) via 1/α = x₊ - (9/47)\textbar ε\textbar{} +
(5/64)\textbar ε\textbar² where ε = e\^{}π - π - 20 ≈ -9×10⁻⁴. See also:
Alpha Precision Formula.
\item[\textbf{Flux}]
3D vector field J = {[}Jx, Jy, Jz{]} representing momentum/energy. The
substrate of potential.
\item[\textbf{Four Framework Integers}]
The minimal inputs \{b₃=7, N\_c=3, N\_eff=13, N\_base=4\} from which all
constants are derived. See individual entries.
\item[\textbf{Force}]
Influence that changes particle momentum. Four forces: gravity,
electromagnetic, strong, weak.
\item[\textbf{Frequency}]
Rate of oscillation. Related to energy by E = hf.
\item[\textbf{Forty-One (41 = N\_eff × N\_c + 2)}]
Derived integer = 13 × 3 + 2 = 41. The ratio between top and bottom
quark masses: m\_t/m\_b = 41. Notably, 41 = 42 - 1, connecting the top
quark to the bridge constant minus unity. A prime number.
\item[\textbf{Forty-Two (42 = 2 × N\_c × b₃)}]
The \textbf{bridge constant} = 2 × 3 × 7 = 42. Equals the product of the
first four Heegner numbers (1 × 2 × 3 × 7). Appears as the additive
correction in tau mass (3477 = 17 × 207 - 42) and bottom quark mass
(8170 = T(127) + 42). The prime counting function π(42) = 13 = N\_eff,
creating a remarkable number-theoretic closure.
\item[\textbf{Forty-Seven (47 = N\_c × N\_base² - 1)}]
Derived integer = 3 × 16 - 1 = 47. Appears as the denominator in the
alpha precision formula: the correction term 9/47 provides the
first-order improvement from 1.26 ppm to sub-ppm accuracy. A prime
number.
\end{description}

\section{G}\label{g}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Genesis}]
Transition from void (0) to manifested state (±1). Occurs
probabilistically when density exceeds KB.
\item[\textbf{Graded Monism}]
The ontological position that reality consists of one fundamental
substance (the void) with multiple modes or grades of being.
Distinguished from dualism (two substances) and eliminative monism (only
one mode). In FTD: void (substrate) → flux (disposition) → manifestation
(actuality).
\item[\textbf{Gradient}]
Vector pointing in direction of maximum increase. Gradients drive
forces.
\item[\textbf{Global(t)}]
Everything at one tick. The complete state of the universe at timestamp
\(t\). Contains all entities, observers, and configurations.
\item[\textbf{Gravity}]
Force proportional to density gradient. Always attractive. Weakest force
but infinite range.
\item[\textbf{Gravitational Wave Detector}]
Instrument (e.g., LIGO, Virgo) detecting spacetime ripples from massive
object mergers via laser interferometry.
\item[\textbf{GZK Cutoff}]
Maximum energy for cosmic rays, limited by interaction with CMB photons.
\end{description}

\section{H}\label{h}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Hadron Collider}]
Accelerator colliding protons or heavy ions at high energies. LHC at
CERN reaches 13 TeV center-of-mass energy.
\item[\textbf{H (Planck Resolution)}]
Fundamental constant = 1.0. The quantum of action. Corresponds to ℏ.
\item[\textbf{H(Ω,t) (Horizon)}]
What observer Ω can perceive at tick \(t\). A subset of Global(t).
Mediated by six senses: sight, sound, touch, taste, smell, cognition.
\item[\textbf{Hadron}]
Particle made of quarks. Baryons (3 quarks) or mesons (quark-antiquark
pair).
\item[\textbf{Hawking Radiation}]
Thermal radiation from black holes due to virtual pair production at
horizon.
\item[\textbf{Heat Death}]
Final state of universe with maximum entropy. All gradients eliminated.
\end{description}

\section{I}\label{i}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Interference}]
Combination of flux waves. Constructive when aligned; destructive when
opposed.
\item[\textbf{Ionization}]
Removal of electron from atom. Creates charged ion.
\item[\textbf{is\_locked}]
Boolean flag indicating particle is part of stable structure. Locked
particles resist decay.
\end{description}

\section{J}\label{j}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{j-Invariant (1728 = 12³)}]
The modular j-invariant = 12³ = (N\_base × N\_c)³ = (4 × 3)³ = 1728.
This value is \textbf{derived} from framework integers, not selected.
The j-invariant determines the complex multiplication structure of
elliptic curves; j = 1728 corresponds to the curve y² = x³ - x with
complex multiplication by i. This special curve underlies the master
quadratic derivation of α.
\item[\textbf{Jeans Mass}]
Minimum mass for gravitational collapse to overcome pressure.
\end{description}

\section{K}\label{k}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{KB (Existence Threshold)}]
Fundamental constant = 0.511. Minimum density for manifestation.
Corresponds to electron mass-energy.
\item[\textbf{Kerr Black Hole}]
Rotating black hole with ergosphere and ring singularity.
\end{description}

\section{L}\label{l}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Laplacian}]
Second-order differential operator. Measures deviation from neighborhood
average.
\item[\textbf{Lemniscate-Alpha Curve}]
The self-referential harmonic curve used to construct a geometric route
to the master quadratic. Its arc length reproduces α = 1/137.036 with
1.26 ppm accuracy under the stated construction. Claims of
``uniqueness'' are to be read as uniqueness within the stated constraint
set.
\item[**Lemniscatic Constant (G*)**]
The lemniscatic constant used in this book, \(G^*\approx 2.9587\) (not
to be confused with Gauss's classical constant \(\varpi\approx 2.622\)).
Appears in the master quadratic relating the electromagnetic root and
the color root. Additional algebraic identities (e.g., in terms of the
quadratic roots) are recorded where used.
\item[\textbf{Lepton}]
Fundamental particle (electron, muon, tau, neutrinos). Not made of
quarks.
\item[\textbf{Loop Self-Enumeration}]
The property that b₃ = N\_base + N\_c = 4 + 3 = 7. The FTD loop length
equals the sum of its structural parameters: the framework counts
itself.
\item[\textbf{Lorentz Force}]
Magnetic force on moving charges. Perpendicular to both velocity and
field.
\end{description}

\section{M}\label{m}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Mandelbrot Set}]
The set of complex numbers c for which the iteration z\_\{n+1\} =
z\_n\^{}2 + c remains bounded. A mathematical archetype of
self-reference where output becomes input. In FTD, serves as a visual
and conceptual analog for the sLoop: bounded orbits correspond to
persistent consciousness, escaping orbits correspond to return to void,
and the infinitely complex boundary represents the edge of consciousness
where all complexity lives.
\item[\textbf{Mandelbrot Boundary}]
The infinitely detailed edge between the Mandelbrot set's interior
(bounded orbits) and exterior (escaping orbits). This boundary, where
the iteration is perpetually ``deciding'' whether to persist or
dissolve, serves as the mathematical portrait of the edge of chaos and
the locus of consciousness in FTD.
\item[\textbf{Mandelbrot-Lemniscate Bridge}]
The exact mathematical identity k\_c × c\_cusp × G* = 1 connecting TRD
parameter space to the Mandelbrot set. The transformation c = 1/(k × G*)
maps TRD coefficient k to Mandelbrot parameter c.~Physics (k=16) maps
inside the set; consciousness (k=0.5) maps outside.
\item[\textbf{Minimum Distance (d\_min)}]
The irreducible gap d\_min = (G*)²/32 ≈ 0.2736 that the Lemniscate-Alpha
curve maintains from the origin. Represents the threshold requirement
for manifestation: physics cannot emerge directly from pure void. The
divisor 32 = k\_physics/k\_consciousness = 16/0.5 is the regime ratio.
\item[\textbf{Manifestation}]
The realized state (±1). Distinguished from potential (flux in void).
\item[\textbf{Matter}]
State +1 particles. Conventional matter as opposed to antimatter.
\item[\textbf{Master Quadratic}]
The equation x² - 16(G\emph{)²x + 16(G})³ = 0 whose roots give α
(larger) and N\_c (smaller). The central derivation mechanism.
\item[\textbf{Mass Spectrometer}]
Instrument measuring particle mass-to-charge ratio. Used for identifying
isotopes and molecules.
\item[\textbf{Meson}]
Particle made of quark-antiquark pair. Examples: pion, kaon.
\item[\textbf{Meta-sLoop}]
An sLoop that includes a model of the sLoop itself. The defining
structure of consciousness. A self-model φ(Ω) that contains φ²(φ(Ω)).
\item[\textbf{Moore Neighborhood}]
26 adjacent cells in 3D (all cells sharing at least one corner with
center).
\item[\textbf{MUSE (MUon proton Scattering Experiment)}]
Experiment at PSI designed to resolve the proton radius puzzle by
measuring electron and muon scattering simultaneously under identical
kinematic conditions. Uses mixed lepton beams, GEM detectors, wire
chambers, and scintillators to compare cross sections and form factors
directly.
\end{description}

\section{N}\label{n}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{N\_eff (Effective Dimension)}]
Framework integer = 13 (Fibonacci F₇). Appears in mass formulas and the
Higgs derivation.
\item[\textbf{Noetic Mass (M\_Ω)}]
The ``epistemic inertia'' of an observer: resistance to belief change
based on accumulated evidence. Defined as M\_Ω = ∫ρ\_Ω(x)dx where ρ\_Ω
is the observer's belief density. Heavier noetic mass means more
evidence required to shift beliefs. See also: Epistemic Inertia.
\item[\textbf{N\_base (Base Modes)}]
Framework integer = 4. Number of base harmonic modes. Golden ratio φ =
(1+√(N\_base+1))/2.
\item[\textbf{N\_c (Color Charges)}]
Framework integer ≈ 3.024 (derived as smaller root of master quadratic).
floor(N\_c) = 3 determines number of generations.
\item[\textbf{Nome (q = e\^{}(-π))}]
The modular nome parameter from lemniscate geometry at j = 1728. The
nome q = e\^{}(-π) ≈ 0.0432 appears naturally in elliptic function
theory for the lemniscatic curve. Its appearance in the alpha precision
formula correction terms (via e\^{}π) connects FTD to classical modular
forms and Ramanujan's work on elliptic integrals.
\item[\textbf{Neutrino}]
Nearly massless, uncharged lepton. Barely interacts. State 0 but
distinct from void.
\item[\textbf{Neutron}]
Neutral baryon (udd). Stable in nucleus; decays freely in
\textasciitilde15 minutes.
\item[\textbf{Neutron Lifetime Puzzle}]
Unexplained \textasciitilde10 second discrepancy between bottle method
(τ ≈ 877.8 s) and beam method (τ ≈ 888.1 s) measurements of free neutron
lifetime. FTD interpretation: measurement-context-dependent sLoop
coupling may create different effective decay rates. Status: under
active investigation at PSI, J-PARC, LANL, NIST.
\item[\textbf{Nucleosynthesis}]
Formation of atomic nuclei. Occurs in Big Bang (light elements) and
stars (heavy elements).
\item[\textbf{Nineteen (19 = 2(b₃ + N\_c) - 1)}]
Derived integer = 2 × 10 - 1 = 19. Appears in charm quark mass:
m\_c/m\_e = 13 × 10 × 19 + 15 = 2485. A prime number.
\end{description}

\section{O}\label{o}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{One Hundred Twenty-Seven (127 = N\_eff(b₃ + N\_c) -
N\_c)}]
Derived integer = 13 × 10 - 3 = 127. The index for the triangular number
in bottom quark mass: m\_b/m\_e = T(127) + 42. A \textbf{Mersenne prime}
(2⁷ - 1), the fourth Mersenne prime in the sequence.
\item[\textbf{One Hundred Thirty-Seven (137 ≈ 1/α)}]
The inverse fine structure constant = x₊ from master quadratic ≈
137.036. A prime number. Often called ``the most mysterious number in
physics.'' In FTD, 137 is \textbf{derived} from the lemniscatic constant
G* via the master quadratic, accurate to 1.26 ppm.
\item[\textbf{One Hundred Eighty-Three (183 = N\_eff(N\_eff + 1) + 1)}]
Derived integer = 13 × 14 + 1 = 183. The strange quark to electron mass
ratio: m\_s/m\_e = 183.
\item[\textbf{Observer (Ω)}]
In FTD, any manifested structure (s ≠ 0) capable of coupling to the flux
field. Not privileged by consciousness: a rock can trigger collapse as
well as a brain. The observer is defined by its configuration Ω(t), its
horizon H(Ω,t), and its noetic mass M\_Ω.
\item[\textbf{Orbit}]
Bound trajectory around massive object. Stable when forces balance.
\item[\textbf{Orbital}]
Spatial probability distribution for electron around nucleus. s, p, d, f
shapes.
\item[\textbf{Ω(t) (Omega)}]
Complete configuration of an observer at tick \(t\). Includes every
atom, neuron, memory, and state of the conscious system. The ``soul'' at
one moment.
\end{description}

\section{P}\label{p}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Pair Production}]
Creation of matter-antimatter pair from sufficient energy. Creates
entangled partners.
\item[\textbf{Phase Accumulator (τ)}]
Counter tracking relativistic time. Particle updates when τ ≥ 1.
\item[\textbf{Path Identity (Quantum Indistinguishability)}]
Mechanism by which quantum correlations arise from indistinguishable
particle paths rather than entanglement. Demonstrated in 2025 Bell
violation experiment (S = 2.275 ± 0.057, \textgreater4σ) without
entangled particles, directly supporting FTD's sLoop interpretation that
correlations arise from shared substrate access.
\item[\textbf{Photon}]
Massless boson carrying electromagnetic force. In FTD, flux waves in
void.
\item[\textbf{Planck Scale}]
Smallest meaningful scale. In FTD, 1 voxel = Planck length; 1 tick =
Planck time.
\item[\textbf{Potential}]
Unrealized possibility. Carried by flux field. Precedes manifestation.
\item[\textbf{Potential vs Probability}]
The crucial distinction between Domain A and Domain B. Potential (flux)
is ontic: it exists in physical space as a real field with causal power.
Probability is epistemic: it exists in the space of knowledge as a
distribution over possibilities. Confusing these leads to domain
confusion.
\item[\textbf{Proton}]
Positive baryon (uud). Stable. Nucleus of hydrogen.
\item[\textbf{Proton Mass (π Formula)}]
The remarkably simple relation m\_p/m\_e = 6π⁵ = 1836.118, accurate to
0.002\%. Uses only the integer 6 and π, with no framework integers or
G\emph{. Suggests QCD binding energy emerges from pure circular
geometry. Connected to the framework via 6π⁵ ≈ G}⁷ × (1 - 1/13).
\item[\textbf{Proton Radius Puzzle}]
Discrepancy between proton charge radius measured via muonic hydrogen
(\textasciitilde0.84 fm) and electronic hydrogen (\textasciitilde0.88
fm). FTD interpretation: the proton ``radius'' is intrinsically
probe-dependent because different probes couple to different scales of
the triad flux distribution. MUSE experiment at PSI testing this with
simultaneous electron/muon scattering measurements.
\end{description}

\section{Q}\label{q}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Quark}]
Fundamental constituent of hadrons. Six flavors: up, down, charm,
strange, top, bottom.
\item[\textbf{Quantum}]
Discrete unit. All physical quantities in FTD are quantized.
\item[\textbf{Quantum Spin Ice (QSI)}]
Exotic quantum state on pyrochlore lattice where magnetic moments
exhibit liquid-like correlations and U(1) gauge structure. Distinguished
from classical spin ice by emergent photon-like excitations near zero
energy. Ce₂Zr₂O₇ confirmed as QSI in 2025, providing the first
experimental validation of FTD's mechanism for gauge boson emergence
from discrete lattice dynamics.
\end{description}

\section{R}\label{r}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Regime Ratio (32)}]
The ratio k\_physics/k\_consciousness = 16/0.5 = 32 between the physics
and consciousness parameter regimes. Appears as the denominator in
d\_min = (G*)²/32 and equals 2⁵ (one beyond highest Lemniscate-Alpha
frequency 2⁴ = 16).
\item[\textbf{Relativity}]
Time dilation based on local energy/frequency. High-energy particles
update less frequently.
\item[\textbf{Retarded Position}]
Source position at time of influence emission. Enforces causality.
\end{description}

\section{S}\label{s}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Sixteen (16 = N\_base²)}]
The master quadratic coefficient = 4² = 16. The equation x² -
16(G\emph{)²x + 16(G})³ = 0 produces α and N\_c.~The value 16 is
\textbf{derived} from the complementation principle: in a 2×2×2 minimal
lattice, 24 links minus 7 constraints minus 1 normalization = 16
physical degrees of freedom. Also equals k\_physics in the Mandelbrot
mapping.
\item[\textbf{Seventeen (17 = N\_eff + N\_base)}]
Derived integer = 13 + 4 = 17. The multiplier in tau mass: m\_τ/m\_e =
17 × 207 - 42 = 3477. A prime number.
\item[\textbf{Sixty-Seven (67 = b₃(b₃ + N\_c) - N\_c)}]
Derived integer = 7 × 10 - 3 = 67. The numerator in the W boson mass
formula: m\_W/m\_e = 67/(8α²). Equals 70 - 3, embedding both framework
structure (70) and color charge (3). A prime number.
\item[\textbf{Seventy (70 = b₃(b₃ + N\_c))}]
Derived integer = 7 × 10 = 70. A fundamental structure factor appearing
in the electron mass derivation and the Heegner decomposition of the
master quadratic (70 ± 67 structure). Related to 67 by 70 - 3 = 67.
\item[\textbf{Scattering Experiment}]
Technique probing structure by measuring deflection of incident
particles. Rutherford scattering revealed atomic nucleus.
\item[\textbf{Schwarzschild Radius}]
Size of event horizon for non-rotating black hole. rs = 2GM/c².
\item[\textbf{Shell}]
Electron orbital layer. Numbered 1, 2, 3\ldots{} with increasing radius.
\item[\textbf{Six Senses}]
The channels through which an observer perceives its horizon H(Ω,t):
sight (EM flux), sound (pressure waves), touch (mechanical contact),
taste (chemical close-range), smell (chemical distance), cognition
(internal state, memory, inference).
\item[\textbf{Self-referential Iteration}]
Any process where output becomes input for the next cycle. The
Mandelbrot iteration z\_\{n+1\} = z\_n\^{}2 + c is the purest
mathematical example. In FTD, the sLoop phi -\textgreater{} phi(phi) +
input has identical structure: the self-model feeds back as input to the
next state.
\item[\textbf{sLoop (Self-referential Loop)}]
A closed causal structure where an observing system is part of the
system being observed. The defining structure of FTD's approach to
measurement and consciousness. In an sLoop, the observer and observed
share a common flux substrate, creating correlations that exceed
classical bounds. Key to Bell violations (S ≈ 2√2). Distinguished from
external observation by ontological continuity between observer and
system. See also: Meta-sLoop.
\item[\textbf{Singularity}]
Point of infinite density. Classical concept; may be resolved by quantum
gravity.
\item[\textbf{State}]
The fundamental property of a voxel. Values: -1 (antimatter), 0 (void),
+1 (matter).
\item[\textbf{Spectroscopy}]
Technique analyzing matter by measuring emitted or absorbed
electromagnetic radiation. Reveals atomic structure and composition.
\item[**Strong Coupling (G*/π Formula)**]
The remarkably simple relation α\_s = G\emph{/(8π) = 0.1177, accurate to
0.15\%. The factor 8π ≈ 25.13 explains why cruder approximations (G}/25)
also work. The ratio α\_s/α ≈ 16 equals the physics coefficient k from
the master quadratic.
\item[\textbf{Strong Force}]
Force binding quarks into hadrons. Yukawa-type with short range.
\end{description}

\section{T}\label{t}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Three Regimes (TRD-Mandelbrot)}]
The three parameter regions connecting TRD to the Mandelbrot set via c =
1/(k × G\emph{): (1) Physics regime: k=16, c=0.021, inside M, real
roots; (2) Critical regime: k=k\_c≈1.352, c=0.25, cardioid cusp, double
root; (3) Consciousness regime: k=0.5, c=0.676, outside M, complex
roots. The transformation is exact: k\_c × c\_cusp × G} = 1.
\item[\textbf{Telescope}]
Instrument collecting electromagnetic radiation from distant objects.
Types: optical, radio, X-ray, gamma-ray.
\item[\textbf{Tick}]
Discrete unit of time. One iteration of the causal loop.
\item[\textbf{Time Dilation}]
Slowing of updates for high-energy particles. Relativistic effect.
\item[\textbf{Triad}]
Stable three-particle configuration (equilateral). Forms nucleons.
\item[\textbf{Translation Protocol}]
The mathematical procedure for converting quantities between Domain A
and Domain B. The Born rule P = \textbar ψ\textbar² is the canonical
translation protocol in quantum mechanics. See also: Bridge Operator.
\item[\textbf{Triangular Number T(n)}]
Sum of integers 1+2+\ldots+n = n(n+1)/2. T(10)=55 appears in proton
mass; T(13)=91 encodes SM structure.
\item[\textbf{Thirty-Four Hundred Seventy-Seven (3477)}]
Tau/electron mass ratio = (N\_eff + N\_base) × 207 - 42 = 17 × 207 - 42
= 3477. Combines the muon ratio (207), the tau multiplier (17), and the
bridge constant (42).
\item[\textbf{Two Hundred Seven (207 = 3 × b₃ × (b₃ + N\_c) - N\_c)}]
The muon/electron mass ratio = 3 × 7 × 10 - 3 = 207. One of the most
famous mass ratios in particle physics, here derived purely from
framework integers.
\item[\textbf{Two Thousand Four Hundred Eighty-Five (2485)}]
Charm/electron mass ratio = N\_eff × (b₃ + N\_c) × (2(b₃ + N\_c) - 1) +
N\_eff + 2 = 13 × 10 × 19 + 15 = 2485.
\item[\textbf{Three Hundred Thirty-Four Thousand Nine Hundred Seventy
(334970)}]
Top/electron mass ratio = 8170 × 41 = 334970. The product of the bottom
quark ratio (8170) and the top/bottom multiplier (41 = N\_eff × N\_c +
2). The heaviest fermion mass, derived from four integers. Error:
0.92\%.
\item[\textbf{Ten (10 = b₃ + N\_c)}]
The universal sum = 7 + 3 = 10. Appears in virtually every mass formula:
proton (T(10)=55), tau (17 × 207 - 42), charm (13 × 10 × 19), bottom
(127 = 13 × 10 - 3). The fact that b₃ + N\_c is so ubiquitous suggests
deep structural significance.
\item[\textbf{Tunneling}]
Quantum phenomenon allowing particles to pass through classically
forbidden barriers.
\item[\textbf{Ternary Valuation Theorem}]
The theorem (proved in \textbf{?@sec-formal-logic}) that exactly three
valuation states \{+1, −1, 0\} are both necessary and sufficient for any
system admitting distinguishability. This establishes that FTD's ternary
state space is not an arbitrary design choice but a logical necessity.
\item[\textbf{Two-Domain Framework}]
The FTD philosophical framework distinguishing Domain A (ontic/classical
configurations) from Domain B (epistemic/probabilistic distributions).
Neither domain is reducible to the other; both are necessary for
complete description. The framework resolves measurement paradoxes by
tracking which domain each quantity belongs to.
\end{description}

\section{U}\label{u}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Uniqueness Theorem}]
The proof that \{7, 3, 13, 4\} is the unique non-trivial solution to the
Fibonacci skeleton constraints with multiple colors. Only three
solutions exist: empty \{1,1,1,0\}, minimal \{5,1,5,4\}, and Standard
Model \{7,3,13,4\}.
\item[\textbf{UUID}]
Unique identifier assigned to each manifested particle. Tracks identity
through time.
\item[\textbf{𝕌 (Universe)}]
The set of all Global(t) across all time: \{ Global(t) : t ∈ ℤ \}.
Everything that has existed, exists, or will exist.
\end{description}

\section{V}\label{v}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Vacuum}]
State 0 (void). Not empty: carries flux. Zero-point fluctuations always
present.
\item[\textbf{Void}]
State 0. The ground state of existence. Substrate of all potential.
\item[\textbf{Voxel}]
Fundamental 3D lattice cell. Smallest spatial unit. Contains state,
flux, and properties.
\item[\textbf{Valuation Function (val)}]
The function val: E × Prop → V that assigns epistemic status to
propositions, where V = \{−1, 0, +1\}. Value +1 means ``affirmed''
(proposition distinguished as holding), −1 means ``negated''
(distinguished as not holding), and 0 means ``undetermined'' (not yet
distinguished). The foundation of EPL-ST. See
\textbf{?@sec-formal-logic}.
\end{description}

\section{W}\label{w}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Wave}]
Propagating flux disturbance. Travels at maximum speed C through void.
\item[\textbf{Weak Force}]
Force causing flavor changes (transmutation). Responsible for beta
decay.
\item[\textbf{Winding Number}]
A topological invariant counting how many times a curve wraps around a
point. The Lemniscate-Alpha curve in the consciousness regime (k = 0.5)
has winding number = -2 around the origin, indicating irreducible
topological structure that cannot be continuously deformed away.
\item[\textbf{Weinberg Angle (θ\_W)}]
Electroweak mixing angle. \textbf{DERIVED} as sin²θ\_W = N\_c/N\_eff =
3/13 = 0.2308 (0.19\% accuracy).
\end{description}

\section{Y}\label{y}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{xyztgψ(Δt)}]
Complete specification of one entity at one tick. Includes position
(xyz), time (t), geometric constraint (g), modal constraint (ψ), and
dynamics (Δt). The fundamental ontological unit.
\item[\textbf{Yukawa Potential}]
Form of strong force: proportional to exp(-mr)/r. Short range.
\end{description}

\section{Z}\label{z}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[\textbf{Zero-Point Energy}]
Minimum energy of vacuum. Even at ``empty'' void, fluctuations persist.
\end{description}

\end{longtable}

\chapter{Particle Catalog}\label{sec-particle-catalog}

\emph{``The complete bestiary: mass relations and numerical fits within
the framework''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Major Update: Masses Are Derived}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

This catalog compiles the mass relations used in the Lemniscate-Alpha
program and companion material, using four integers: \(b_3=7\),
\(N_c=3\), \(N_{\text{eff}}=13\), \(N_{\text{base}}=4\). Masses are
shown as ratios to electron mass \(m_e\) = 0.511 MeV/\(c^2\).

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Fundamental Fermions}\label{fundamental-fermions}

\subsection{Quarks (State: +1)}\label{quarks-state-1}

Quarks are the fundamental constituents of hadrons. The catalog presents
the framework's proposed mass relations in a uniform format.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Epistemic Status: Numerical Fits}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Unlike lepton masses (which follow pure integer arithmetic: muon = 3×70
- 3, tau = 17×207 - 42) and the proton mass (which has two independent
derivation paths converging via G*), the quark mass formulas below are
\textbf{numerical fits}: combinations of framework integers that match
experimental data to \textless1\% accuracy but lack an underlying
connecting principle.

An underlying principle connecting these formulas remains an
\textbf{open research question}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsubsection{Quark Mass Relations}\label{quark-mass-relations}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Quark & Formula & m/m\_e & MeV/c² & Error \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Up (u) & N\_base + sin²θ\_W & 4.231 & 2.16 & 0.09\% \\
Down (d) & 2N\_base + 1 + αN\_eff & 9.095 & 4.67 & 0.48\% \\
Strange (s) & N\_eff(N\_eff+1) + 1 & 183 & 93.5 & 0.12\% \\
Charm (c) & \(N_{\text{eff}}(b_3+N_c)(19) + 15\) & 2485 & 1270 &
0.01\% \\
Bottom (b) & \(10^3 \times 8 + 169\) & 8169 & 4180 & 0.14\% \\
Top (t) & (φ² - 64α) × m\_W & 338,400 & 173,000 & 0.12\% \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Composite Particles:
Baryons}\label{composite-particles-baryons}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Epistemic Status: Composite Particles}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

\textbf{Nucleons (proton, neutron)} have framework derivations with
sub-percent accuracy.

\textbf{All other composite particles} (strange baryons, mesons, heavy
hadrons) use \textbf{{[}DATA{]}} from PDG 2024. Framework derivations
for these masses remain an \textbf{open research question}. Values are
included for reference completeness.

\end{tcolorbox}

Where T(10) = 55 is the 10th triangular number, and
\((n-p)/m_e = \varphi^2 - 12\alpha = 2.53\).

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Two Paths to the Proton Mass}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

The proton mass has two independent formulas: the pure geometry path
(\(6\pi^5 = 1836.118\), 0.002\% error) and the framework integer path
(\(N_{\text{eff}}/\alpha + T(10) = 1836.47\), 0.017\% error). Both
converge via \(G^{*7} \times (12/13)\). See
Chapter~\ref{sec-constants-reference} for the full derivation.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Particle Creation
Template}\label{particle-creation-template}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{PARTICLE\_CATALOG }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \CommentTok{\# Quarks}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}up\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.6}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{\},}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}down\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.6}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{\},}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charm\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{25.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{\},}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}strange\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{2.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{\},}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}top\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{350.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{\},}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}bottom\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{80.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{\},}

    \CommentTok{\# Leptons}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}electron\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.511}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{\},}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}muon\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{2.1}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{\},}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}tau\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{35.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{\},}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}neutrino\_e\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.001}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{\},}

    \CommentTok{\# Composite}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}proton\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{9.0}\NormalTok{,}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}composition\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: [}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}up\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}up\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}down\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{],}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}geometry\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}triad\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{    \},}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}neutron\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{9.0}\NormalTok{,}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}composition\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: [}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}up\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}down\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}down\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{],}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}geometry\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}triad\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{    \},}
\NormalTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ create\_particle(particle\_type, position):}
\NormalTok{    template }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ PARTICLE\_CATALOG[particle\_type]}
\NormalTok{    particle }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Voxel(position)}
\NormalTok{    particle.state }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ template[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    particle.mass }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ template[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mass\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    particle.charge }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ template[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}charge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    particle.uuid }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ generate\_uuid()}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ particle}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Composite Particle
Factory}\label{composite-particle-factory}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ create\_proton(center\_position):}
    \CommentTok{"""Create a proton triad at given position."""}
    \CommentTok{\# Equilateral triangle in xy{-}plane}
\NormalTok{    offset }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.707}  \CommentTok{\# sqrt(2)/2 for unit separation}
\NormalTok{    positions }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}
\NormalTok{        center\_position }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ [}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, offset, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{],      }\CommentTok{\# up}
\NormalTok{        center\_position }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ [}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{offset, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{offset}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{],  }\CommentTok{\# up}
\NormalTok{        center\_position }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ [offset, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{offset}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{],   }\CommentTok{\# down}
\NormalTok{    ]}

\NormalTok{    quarks }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}
\NormalTok{        create\_particle(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}up\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, positions[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]),}
\NormalTok{        create\_particle(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}up\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, positions[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]),}
\NormalTok{        create\_particle(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}down\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, positions[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]),}
\NormalTok{    ]}

    \CommentTok{\# Lock the triad}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ q }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ quarks:}
\NormalTok{        q.is\_locked }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{True}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Proton(quarks)}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ create\_hydrogen(center\_position):}
    \CommentTok{"""Create hydrogen atom: proton + electron."""}
\NormalTok{    proton }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ create\_proton(center\_position)}

    \CommentTok{\# Electron at shell radius}
\NormalTok{    electron\_pos }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ center\_position }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ [}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]  }\CommentTok{\# n=1 shell}
\NormalTok{    electron }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ create\_particle(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}electron\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, electron\_pos)}
\NormalTok{    electron.shell\_n }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ HydrogenAtom(proton, electron)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-45}

This catalog compiles the particle spectrum: fundamental fermions with
derived masses, gauge bosons with framework formulas, and composite
hadrons with PDG data. The assumption ledger
(Chapter~\ref{sec-assumption-ledger}) tracks the epistemic status of
each derivation.

\chapter{Assumption Ledger}\label{sec-assumption-ledger}

\emph{``Intellectual honesty requires knowing what we assume, derive,
and conjecture.''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Purpose of This Chapter}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

This chapter categorizes every major claim in FTD by its epistemic
status. Before accepting any claim, check here to see whether it is an
axiom (assumed), theorem (derived), emergence (simulated), or conjecture
(proposed).

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}

\midrule\noalign{}

1 & Space is a discrete 3D lattice & \hyperref[t]{T} \textbf{THEOREM} &
Derived from stable relational embedding (see
\textbf{?@sec-formal-logic}) \\
2 & Time advances in discrete ticks & \hyperref[a]{A} AXIOM &
Instantiates epistemic time \\
3 & Each cell has state ∈ \{−1, 0, +1\} & \hyperref[t]{T}
\textbf{THEOREM} & Derived from EPL-ST necessity \\
4 & Updates are local (26-neighbor) & \hyperref[a]{A} AXIOM & Moore
neighborhood \\
5 & Max speed = 1 cell/tick & \hyperref[a]{A} AXIOM & Causality
preservation \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Ternary States: Theorem, Not Axiom}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

The ternary state space \{−1, 0, +1\} was historically listed as an
axiom, but is now understood as a \textbf{theorem} of Epistemic-Physical
Logic (EPL-ST). The proof in \textbf{?@sec-formal-logic} establishes
that ternary valuation is the \emph{necessary and sufficient} structure
for any system admitting distinguishability. See the Ternary Valuation
Theorem.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Detailed Resolution
Evidence}\label{detailed-resolution-evidence}

\textbf{C1: Renormalization Framework}

The master quadratic derivation (Ch. 8 in v2; formerly Chapter 1.10b)
establishes a renormalization framework through:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  One-loop effective action
  \(\Gamma_{\text{1-loop}}(x) = \frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\ln K(x)\)
\item
  Vacuum polarization correction \(\Pi(x) = 16(G^*)^3/x\)
\item
  Dyson self-consistency: \(x = 16(G^*)^2 - \Pi(x)\)
\end{enumerate}

This yields the master quadratic as the self-consistent solution, with
running coupling structure implicit in the polarization term.

\subsection{The Fibonacci Skeleton
Resolution}\label{the-fibonacci-skeleton-resolution}

The four framework integers \{7, 3, 13, 4\} are not arbitrary. They
satisfy:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Loop self-enumeration}:
  \(b_3 = N_{\text{base}} + N_c = 4 + 3 = 7\)
\item
  \textbf{Fibonacci embedding}: \(n_{\text{eff}} = F_7 = F_{b_3} = 13\)
\item
  \textbf{Power-of-2 closure}: \(2^{N_c} = 2^3 = 8 = F_6\) (only
  Fibonacci power of 2 \textgreater{} 2)
\item
  \textbf{Fixed point}: \(T(b_3 + N_c) = T(10) = 55 = F_{10}\)
  (triangular = Fibonacci)
\item
  \textbf{Content counting}:
  \(N_c \times N_{\text{base}} = 12 = n_{\text{eff}} - 1\) (fermions)
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Uniqueness Theorem}: Only THREE solutions exist:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Solution & \{b₃, N\_c, N\_eff, N\_base\} & Fermions & Physics \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Empty & \{1, 1, 1, 0\} & 0 & None \\
Minimal & \{5, 1, 5, 4\} & 4 & No color confinement \\
\textbf{Standard} & \textbf{\{7, 3, 13, 4\}} & \textbf{12} & \textbf{Our
universe} \\
\end{longtable}

Within this constraint class, the Standard Model solution is singled out
among the listed alternatives for \(N_c > 1\). Any broader claim of
necessity should be read as conditional on the stated skeleton
constraints. See \textbf{?@sec-uniqueness}.

\subsection{The Lemniscate-Alpha Uniqueness
Resolution}\label{the-lemniscate-alpha-uniqueness-resolution}

The Lemniscate-Alpha curve is \textbf{unique within the stated
construction}. The argument follows a chain of determinations under the
book's explicit constraints:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Structure fixed}: 5 modes at frequencies \{1, 2, 4, 8, 16\}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Self-reference requires N² = highest frequency
  \item
    Only N = 4 works: 4² = 16 = 2⁴
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Coefficients encode framework integers}:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Mode 8: \(a_8 + |b_8| = 2/5 + 7/20 = 3/4 = N_c/N_{\text{base}}\)
  \item
    Mode 16: \(a_{16} = b_{16} = 1/16 = 1/N_{\text{base}}^2\)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{G* self-consistently determined}:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \(G^* = \frac{(1/\alpha) \times N_c}{(1/\alpha) + N_c}\)
    (harmonic-mean relation)
  \item
    \(G^* = 3 - \frac{17\alpha}{3}\) where 17 =
    \(n_{\text{eff}} + N_{\text{base}}\)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Framework integers unique} (by Fibonacci skeleton)
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Result (scoped)}: Given the stated constraints, the chain fixes
the construction. See \textbf{?@sec-curve-uniqueness}.

\section{The Honest Assessment}\label{the-honest-assessment}

FTD is a \textbf{structured framework} with clearly delineated scope:

\subsection{Core Paper Establishes}\label{core-paper-establishes}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{4 theorems} (T1-T6): rigorous derivations from axioms
\item
  \textbf{Fine structure constant α} to 1.26 ppm (base); \textbf{0.21
  ppt} (precision formula)
\item
  \textbf{Coefficient 16} from lattice degrees of freedom
\item
  \textbf{Selection Principles S1-S4}: argued but not uniquely proven
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Companion Work Extends}\label{companion-work-extends}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{31 additional derivations} using α + framework relations
\item
  Particle masses, flavor physics, cosmological constant
\item
  These are mathematically consistent but form a separate derivation
  chain
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Key Distinction}\label{key-distinction}

The core paper makes no claim about particle masses, CKM/PMNS matrices,
or cosmological parameters. Those are \textbf{companion work} using the
same framework integers but requiring additional theoretical machinery.

We claim to have found a consistent ontology from which physics-like
behavior emerges. We do NOT claim to have derived all of physics from
the core paper alone.

The value of FTD lies in: 1. The emergent features (these are genuine
discoveries) 2. The derived constants (these reduce free parameters) 3.
The explicit distinction between core and companion (this is
intellectual honesty)

What we impose, we label. What we derive, we scope. What we conjecture,
we test.

\section{Part I: Why These Integers?}\label{part-i-why-these-integers}

The framework uses four integers: \{b₃=7, N\_c=3, N\_eff=13,
N\_base=4\}. We will show these are \textbf{not arbitrary}: they form a
self-consistent solution within the stated constraint class.

The strongest uniqueness language in this chapter is always relative to
an explicit constraint class (e.g., the discrete-lattice +
Fibonacci-constraint setup used throughout).

\subsection{The Self-Reference Loop}\label{the-self-reference-loop}

The integers form a closed loop of mutual determination: N\_c = 3
determines N\_generations = ⌊N\_c⌋ = 3, which gives N\_flavors = 2 ×
N\_gen = 6, yielding b₃ = 11 - 2n\_f/3 = 7, from which N\_eff =
F\_\{b₃\} = F\_7 = 13, and finally α = f(b₃, N\_c, N\_eff) = 1/137.036.
The gauge group SU(N\_c) = SU(3) closes the loop back to N\_c.

Each integer determines the others. This is not fine-tuning: it is
\textbf{self-determination}.

\subsection{Step 1: N\_c Determines
Everything}\label{step-1-n_c-determines-everything}

\textbf{Claim}: Within the framework's constraint set, N\_c = 3 is the
value singled out by the stated consistency requirements.

\textbf{Proof}:

The SU(N\_c) gauge group requires:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Asymptotic freedom: b₃ \textgreater{} 0
\item
  Confinement: Flux tubes must form
\item
  Three generations of fermions
\end{enumerate}

The QCD beta function coefficient:

\[b_3 = 11 - \frac{2n_f}{3}\]

For asymptotic freedom: b₃ \textgreater{} 0 requires n\_f \textless{}
16.5

The number of flavors: n\_f = 2 × N\_generations = 2 × ⌊N\_c⌋

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llllr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
N\_c & N\_gen & n\_f & b₃ & Asymptotic Freedom? \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 & 2 & 4 & 8.33 & Yes, but no color confinement \\
3 & 3 & 6 & 7 & \textbf{Yes} \\
4 & 4 & 8 & 5.67 & Yes, but wrong physics \\
5 & 5 & 10 & 4.33 & Yes, but wrong physics \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Why N\_c = 2 fails}: SU(2) has no cubic Casimir, so no chiral
anomaly → no baryogenesis.

\textbf{Why N\_c ≥ 4 fails}: The Weinberg angle relation sin²θ\_W =
3/(b₃ + N\_c + N\_eff - 1) gives wrong values.

\textbf{Conclusion}: Within this constraint class, N\_c = 3 is selected.

\subsection{Step 2: b₃ = 7 Follows}\label{step-2-bux2083-7-follows}

Given N\_c = 3:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  N\_generations = 3
\item
  n\_f = 6
\item
  b₃ = 11 - 4 = 7
\end{itemize}

This is not a choice: it is determined by QCD with the matter content.

\subsection{Step 3: N\_eff = F\_\{b₃\} =
13}\label{step-3-n_eff-f_bux2083-13}

\textbf{Claim}: The effective dimension is the b₃-th Fibonacci number.

\textbf{Why Fibonacci?}

The Fibonacci sequence arises from the recurrence:

\[F_n = F_{n-1} + F_{n-2}\]

In FTD, this corresponds to \textbf{mode coupling} in the flux field.
Each harmonic mode couples to the two previous modes, creating Fibonacci
scaling.

The effective dimension counts independent flux configurations at scale
λ:

\[n_{\text{eff}}(\lambda) = F_{\lfloor \log_\phi(\lambda/\ell_P) \rfloor}\]

At the QCD scale (where b₃ governs), we have:

\[n_{\text{eff}} = F_{b_3} = F_7 = 13\]

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
n & F\_n & Physical interpretation \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
1 & 1 & Single voxel \\
2 & 1 & Pair \\
3 & 2 & Triangle (minimal closed) \\
4 & 3 & Tetrahedron \\
5 & 5 & Bound structure \\
6 & 8 & Complex \\
7 & 13 & \textbf{QCD scale ← N\_eff} \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Step 4: N\_base = 4 from
Spacetime}\label{step-4-n_base-4-from-spacetime}

\textbf{Claim}: N\_base = 4 is the number of independent wave modes in
3+1 dimensions.

In the flux field J ∈ ℝ³, waves have:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  2 transverse polarizations (physical photon modes)
\item
  1 longitudinal mode (constrained by Gauss law)
\item
  1 temporal mode (from ∂\_t J)
\end{itemize}

But the constraint removes 1, leaving:

\[N_{\text{base}} = 3 + 1 = 4\]

Alternatively: 4 = 2² represents the spinor dimension for fermions.

\subsection{The Master Quadratic
Connection}\label{the-master-quadratic-connection}

The integers connect to the fine structure constant through the master
quadratic (\textbf{?@sec-master-quadratic-derivation}):

\[x^2 - 16(G^*)^2 x + 16(G^*)^3 = 0\]

where the coefficient 16 = N\_base² = 4² emerges from the lattice
degrees of freedom, and the lemniscatic constant G* encodes the elliptic
integral structure.

The larger root x₊ = 137.0361\ldots{} = 1/α, while the integers appear
in:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  b₃ = 7: QCD beta function coefficient
\item
  N\_c = 3: Color charge number (from x₋ = 3.024\ldots)
\item
  N\_eff = 13 = F₇: Effective degrees of freedom at QCD scale
\item
  N\_base = 4: Spacetime wave modes (giving coefficient 16 = 4²)
\end{itemize}

The self-consistency is that b₃ = 11 - 2n\_f/3 = 7 requires n\_f = 6
(three generations of quarks), which requires N\_generations = 3 = ⌊x₋⌋,
closing the loop

\textbf{Uniqueness (scoped)}: Within the stated constraint class,
alternative integer choices typically violate one or more of:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Asymptotic freedom
\item
  Correct Weinberg angle
\item
  Correct fine structure constant
\item
  Fibonacci scaling
\end{itemize}

\section{Part II: The Strong CP Problem: Proposed Mechanism
{[}†{]}}\label{part-ii-the-strong-cp-problem-proposed-mechanism}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Companion Work}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

This section extends beyond what the core paper establishes. The
resolution of the Strong CP problem follows from discrete lattice
topology but is developed in companion papers.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{The Problem}\label{the-problem-1}

QCD allows a CP-violating term:

\[\mathcal{L}_\theta = \frac{\theta g^2}{32\pi^2} \epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma} F_{\mu\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}\]

Experimentally: \textbar θ\textbar{} \textless{} 10⁻¹⁰

Why so small?

\subsection{The FTD Solution: Topological
Quantization}\label{the-ftd-solution-topological-quantization}

In FTD, the θ term corresponds to the \textbf{winding number} of flux
configurations:

\[\theta = \frac{1}{8\pi^2} \int d^4x \, \vec{J} \cdot (\nabla \times \vec{J})\]

\textbf{Claim}: On a discrete lattice, θ is exactly quantized to integer
multiples of 2π.

\textbf{Proof}:

The integral \(\int \mathbf{J} \cdot (\nabla \times \mathbf{J})\) counts
the linking number of flux lines. On a discrete lattice:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Flux lines are discrete curves through voxels
\item
  Linking numbers are integers
\item
  Therefore: θ ∈ 2πℤ
\end{enumerate}

But physical observables depend only on exp(iθ):

\[e^{i\theta} = e^{i \cdot 2\pi n} = 1 \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{Z}\]

\textbf{Therefore}: θ has no physical effect in FTD!

\subsection{The Discrete Topology
Argument}\label{the-discrete-topology-argument}

In continuous spacetime, θ can take any real value, and θ ≈ 0 requires
fine-tuning. In the discrete lattice of FTD, θ ∈ 2πℤ is topologically
quantized, so exp(iθ) = 1 always, and no fine-tuning is needed.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Strong CP: Proposed Mechanism {[}†{]}}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Companion-work claim {[}†{]}: In the discrete-lattice formulation used
here, the topological structure can enforce θ = 0 (mod 2π) for the flux
configurations considered, eliminating the usual fine-tuning concern
\emph{within that formulation}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Axion-Like Excitations}\label{axion-like-excitations}

Although θ = 0 is enforced, FTD predicts \textbf{axion-like modes}:

The flux field has oscillatory modes around the ground state:

\[\delta J \sim \cos(m_a t - \vec{k} \cdot \vec{x})\]

The axion mass:

\[m_a = \frac{\sqrt{m_u m_d}}{m_u + m_d} \cdot \frac{f_\pi m_\pi}{f_a}\]

From Lemniscate-Alpha:

\[f_a = \frac{M_P}{\alpha^{12}} \approx 10^{12} \text{ GeV}\]

This gives:

\[m_a \approx 6 \times 10^{-6} \text{ eV}\]

\textbf{Prediction}: Axion-like particles with m\_a ≈ 6 μeV exist as
flux oscillations, detectable by experiments like ADMX.

FTD makes specific, falsifiable predictions that differ from the
Standard Model alone.

\subsection{Prediction 1: No WIMP
Detection}\label{prediction-1-no-wimp-detection}

\textbf{Standard expectation}: Dark matter consists of WIMPs with σ
\textasciitilde{} 10⁻⁴⁵ cm².

\textbf{FTD prediction}: Dark matter is coherent void fluctuations, not
particles.

\textbf{Testable consequence}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  All direct detection experiments (XENON, LZ, etc.) will find nothing
\item
  Indirect detection (annihilation signals) will find nothing
\item
  Continued non-detection would be consistent with this claim as limits
  improve
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Prediction 2: Planck-Scale Lorentz
Violation}\label{prediction-2-planck-scale-lorentz-violation}

\textbf{Standard expectation}: Lorentz invariance holds exactly at all
scales.

\textbf{FTD prediction}: The discrete lattice creates
\textbf{Planck-suppressed} Lorentz violation:

\[\delta c/c \sim (E/E_P)^n\]

where n = 1 or 2 depending on the direction.

\textbf{Testable consequence}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  High-energy gamma rays (GRB observations) may show energy-dependent
  dispersion
\item
  Current bounds: n=1 ruled out, n=2 still allowed
\item
  Fermi satellite and future CTA observations can probe this
\end{itemize}

Parameters}\label{prediction-3-specific-inflation-parameters}

\textbf{Testable consequence}:

\item
  Prediction is specific and falsifiable
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Prediction 5: Proton
Stability}\label{prediction-5-proton-stability}

In many GUT theories, protons decay via X-boson exchange:

\[p \to e^+ + \pi^0\]

\textbf{FTD prediction}: The GUT scale M\_GUT ≈ 10¹⁵ GeV gives:

\[\tau_p \approx \frac{M_{\text{GUT}}^4}{\alpha_{\text{GUT}}^2 m_p^5} \approx 10^{35} \text{ years}\]

\textbf{Current bound}: τ\_p \textgreater{} 2.4 × 10³⁴ years
(Super-Kamiokande)

\textbf{Testable consequence}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Hyper-Kamiokande will reach 10³⁵ years sensitivity
\item
  FTD predicts detection within this range
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Prediction 6: Gravitational Wave Background from Discrete
Spacetime}\label{prediction-6-gravitational-wave-background-from-discrete-spacetime}

The lattice structure creates a stochastic gravitational wave
background:

\[h_{\text{stochastic}} \sim \frac{\ell_P}{L} \sim 10^{-43}\]

at frequency f \textasciitilde{} c/ℓ\_P \textasciitilde{} 10⁴³ Hz.

This is far too high frequency for detection, but causes:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Decorrelation of LIGO signals at Planck time scales
\item
  Potentially observable as ``noise'' in future detectors
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Prediction 7: Magnetic Monopole
Absence}\label{prediction-7-magnetic-monopole-absence}

\textbf{Standard GUT expectation}: Magnetic monopoles produced at
T\_GUT.

\textbf{FTD prediction}: The discrete lattice structure forbids
monopoles because:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Monopoles require ∇·B ≠ 0
\item
  But ∇·B = ∇·(∇×J) = 0 identically on any lattice
\item
  Therefore: No monopoles exist
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Testable consequence}: Continued non-detection of monopoles
(already consistent).

\subsection{Prediction 8: The Electroweak Phase Transition is
First-Order}\label{prediction-8-the-electroweak-phase-transition-is-first-order}

\textbf{Standard Model}: EW transition is a crossover (smooth).

\textbf{FTD prediction}: EW transition is first-order (bubble
nucleation).

\textbf{Testable consequence}:

\begin{itemize}

  Gravitational waves from bubble collisions at f \textasciitilde{} mHz
\item
  LISA sensitivity: yes, marginally
\item
  Predicted amplitude: Ω\_GW \textasciitilde{} 10⁻¹² at 1 mHz
\end{itemize}

\section{Summary: The Complete
Framework}\label{summary-the-complete-framework}

\subsection{What the Core Paper
Establishes}\label{what-the-core-paper-establishes}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}

  \textbf{Integers are selected (within constraints)}: \{7, 3, 13, 4\}
  form a self-consistent solution satisfying the stated Fibonacci
  constraints
\item
  \textbf{Coefficient 16 derived}: From lattice DoF counting (primary
  derivation)

  precision} (precision formula)
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{What Companion Work Extends
{[}†{]}}\label{what-companion-work-extends}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}

  \textbf{Strong CP mechanism proposed}: θ = 0 from lattice topology, no
  fine-tuning (within the companion-work formulation)
\item

\item
  \textbf{Particle-mass program extended}: Using α plus framework
  relations (companion work)
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{The Self-Referential
Closure}\label{the-self-referential-closure}

FTD achieves something rare in physics: \textbf{self-determining
structure}. In a typical theory, axioms lead to predictions, and the
axioms are free parameters. In FTD, axioms and predictions constrain
each other: N\_c = 3 determines b₃ = 7, which determines N\_eff = 13,
which determines α = 1/137, and self-consistency closes the loop.

The framework is not just internally consistent: it is \textbf{highly
constrained} by requiring self-consistency (within the stated constraint
class).

\subsection{The Remaining Input}\label{the-remaining-input}

The one remaining input: \textbf{Why a discrete 3D lattice?}

Possible answers:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Any topology supporting SU(3) gauge theory requires 3 spatial
  dimensions
\item
  The lattice is a candidate regularization intended to preserve both
  gauge invariance and locality
\item
  This is the ``cosmological selection'' principle: other structures do
  not produce observers
\end{enumerate}

But even without answering this, FTD has reduced the number of
unexplained inputs from dozens (Standard Model parameters) to
essentially one (the existence of the lattice itself).
