\chapter{Information Quantification in
FTD}\label{sec-information-quantification}

\emph{``To know is to constrain; to experience is to accumulate
constraints''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Key Revelation}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Information is not substance: it is constraint on configuration space.
We can quantify it using entropy measures. An sLoop's priors are its
initial representation structure; its experiences are accumulated mutual
information with the environment.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{1. Information in Configuration
Space}\label{information-in-configuration-space}

\subsection{Definition 1.1 (State Information
Density)}\label{definition-1.1-state-information-density}

For a single voxel with ternary state \(s \in \{-1, 0, +1\}\), the
maximum information content is:

\[I_{\text{state}} = \log_2(3) \approx 1.585 \text{ bits per voxel}\]

For a region \(R \subseteq \mathcal{L}\) with \(|R|\) voxels:

\[I_{\text{max}}(R) = |R| \cdot \log_2(3) \text{ bits}\]

\subsection{Definition 1.2 (Actual State
Information)}\label{definition-1.2-actual-state-information}

Given a probability distribution \(P(s)\) over states in region \(R\):

\[I_{\text{actual}}(R) = -\sum_{c \in \mathcal{C}_R} P(c) \log_2 P(c)\]

The \textbf{information deficit} (how much the region is constrained):

\[\Delta I(R) = I_{\text{max}}(R) - I_{\text{actual}}(R) \geq 0\]

A fully random region has \(\Delta I = 0\). A fully determined region
has \(\Delta I = I_{\text{max}}\).

\subsection{Definition 1.3 (Flux
Information)}\label{definition-1.3-flux-information}

The continuous flux field \(\mathbf{J}(v) \in \mathbb{R}^3\) requires
discretization for finite information content. At resolution
\(\epsilon\):

\[I_{\text{flux}}(R, \epsilon) = |R| \cdot 3 \cdot \log_2\left(\frac{J_{\text{max}}}{\epsilon}\right)\]

where \(J_{\text{max}}\) is the maximum flux magnitude.

\textbf{Physical interpretation}: The Planck scale sets a natural
cutoff. Below Planck resolution, flux information is undefined.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{2. Information Measures}\label{information-measures}

\subsection{2.1 Shannon Entropy}\label{shannon-entropy-2}

For a configuration distribution \(P(c)\):

\[H[P] = -\sum_c P(c) \log_2 P(c)\]

\textbf{Properties:} - \(H \geq 0\) (non-negative) - \(H = 0\) iff
deterministic (one configuration has \(P = 1\)) - \(H = H_{\text{max}}\)
iff uniform distribution

\subsection{2.2 Relative Entropy (Kullback-Leibler
Divergence)}\label{relative-entropy-kullback-leibler-divergence}

The information gained by updating from prior \(Q\) to posterior \(P\):

\[D_{KL}(P \| Q) = \sum_c P(c) \log_2 \frac{P(c)}{Q(c)} \geq 0\]

\textbf{Interpretation}: How many bits of ``surprise'' when reality is
\(P\) but you expected \(Q\).

\subsection{2.3 Mutual Information}\label{mutual-information}

The shared information between two regions \(A\) and \(B\):

\[I(A; B) = H[A] + H[B] - H[A, B]\]

\textbf{Interpretation}: How much knowing \(A\) tells you about \(B\).

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Mutual Information is Symmetric}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

\(I(A; B) = I(B; A)\)

Correlation is bidirectional. This is crucial for understanding sLoop:
the observer-observed relationship is mutual.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{3. Information Geometry of
Space}\label{information-geometry-of-space}

\subsection{Definition 3.1 (Local Information
Density)}\label{definition-3.1-local-information-density}

At voxel \(v\), the information density is:

\[\rho_I(v) = -\sum_{s \in \{-1,0,+1\}} P(s|v) \log_2 P(s|v)\]

This creates a scalar field over the lattice: an \textbf{information
landscape}.

\subsection{Definition 3.2 (Information
Gradient)}\label{definition-3.2-information-gradient}

\[\nabla I(v) = \left(\frac{\partial \rho_I}{\partial x}, \frac{\partial \rho_I}{\partial y}, \frac{\partial \rho_I}{\partial z}\right)\]

\textbf{Physical meaning}: Information flows from high-entropy
(uncertain) to low-entropy (determined) regions during collapse.

\subsection{Definition 3.3 (Information
Current)}\label{definition-3.3-information-current}

The rate of information flow through a surface \(\Sigma\):

\[\Phi_I = \int_\Sigma \rho_I \mathbf{v} \cdot d\mathbf{A}\]

where \(\mathbf{v}\) is the flux velocity field.

\subsection{Theorem 3.1 (Information
Conservation)}\label{theorem-3.1-information-conservation}

In a closed system, total information is conserved:

\[\frac{d}{dt} \int_V \rho_I \, dV = -\oint_{\partial V} \rho_I \mathbf{v} \cdot d\mathbf{A}\]

Information cannot be created or destroyed: only transferred or
transformed.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{4. sLoop Information
Structure}\label{sloop-information-structure}

An sLoop \((\Omega, \phi, \sigma)\) has three distinct information
reservoirs:

\subsection{4.1 Substrate Information}\label{substrate-information}

The physical information content of the sLoop's matter:

\[I_\Omega = \sum_{v \in \Omega} \rho_I(v)\]

This is the ``hardware'': the bits stored in the physical configuration.

\subsection{4.2 Representation
Information}\label{representation-information}

The capacity of the representation map
\(\phi: \mathcal{C} \to \mathcal{M}\):

\[I_\phi = \log_2 |\mathcal{M}|\]

This is the ``software'': how many distinct representations are
possible.

\subsection{4.3 Self-Model Information}\label{self-model-information}

The information content of the self-embedding \(\sigma\):

\[I_\sigma = I(\sigma(\Omega); \Omega)\]

The mutual information between the self-model and the actual substrate.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{The Three Information Types}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Symbol & What It Measures \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Substrate & \(I_\Omega\) & Physical bits in matter \\
Representation & \(I_\phi\) & Capacity of mental model \\
Self-model & \(I_\sigma\) & Accuracy of self-knowledge \\
\end{longtable}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{5. Quantifying Priors}\label{quantifying-priors}

\subsection{Definition 5.1 (Prior
Distribution)}\label{definition-5.1-prior-distribution}

An sLoop's \textbf{prior} is the probability distribution over
configurations before observation:

\[\pi: \mathcal{C} \to [0, 1], \quad \sum_c \pi(c) = 1\]

\subsection{Definition 5.2 (Prior
Entropy)}\label{definition-5.2-prior-entropy}

The uncertainty before observation:

\[H_{\text{prior}} = H[\pi] = -\sum_c \pi(c) \log_2 \pi(c)\]

\textbf{Maximum ignorance}: Uniform prior, \(\pi(c) = 1/|\mathcal{C}|\),
gives \(H_{\text{prior}} = \log_2 |\mathcal{C}|\).

\textbf{Maximum knowledge}: Deterministic prior, \(\pi(c_0) = 1\), gives
\(H_{\text{prior}} = 0\).

\subsection{Definition 5.3 (Prior
Structure)}\label{definition-5.3-prior-structure}

The prior is not uniform: it has structure encoding beliefs about
reality:

\[\pi(c) = \frac{1}{Z} e^{-\beta E(c)}\]

where: - \(E(c)\) is the ``implausibility'' of configuration \(c\) -
\(\beta\) is a confidence parameter - \(Z\) is the normalization
(partition function)

\subsection{5.1 Innate Priors (Built-in
Structure)}\label{innate-priors-built-in-structure}

Some priors are ``hardwired'' into the sLoop's architecture:

\[\pi_{\text{innate}}(c) \propto \exp\left(-\sum_i w_i f_i(c)\right)\]

where \(f_i\) are feature detectors and \(w_i\) are innate weights.

\textbf{Examples of innate priors:} - Spatial continuity: nearby voxels
likely have similar states - Temporal persistence: configurations change
slowly - Conservation: total flux is approximately constant

\subsection{5.2 Learned Priors (Acquired
Structure)}\label{learned-priors-acquired-structure}

Experience updates the prior via Bayes' theorem:

\[\pi_{t+1}(c) = \frac{P(\text{obs}|c) \cdot \pi_t(c)}{P(\text{obs})}\]

The \textbf{learning rate} is the information gain per observation:

\[\Delta I_{\text{learn}} = D_{KL}(\pi_{t+1} \| \pi_t)\]

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{6. Quantifying Experience}\label{quantifying-experience}

\subsection{Definition 6.1
(Experience)}\label{definition-6.1-experience}

An \textbf{experience} is a sequence of observations
\((o_1, o_2, \ldots, o_T)\) that updates the sLoop's internal state.

\subsection{Definition 6.2 (Experiential
Information)}\label{definition-6.2-experiential-information}

The total information accumulated through experience:

\[I_{\text{exp}} = \sum_{t=1}^T D_{KL}(\pi_t \| \pi_{t-1})\]

This is the cumulative ``surprise'': how much the sLoop's beliefs have
changed.

\subsection{Definition 6.3 (Experience
Entropy)}\label{definition-6.3-experience-entropy}

The entropy of the experience sequence:

\[H_{\text{exp}} = -\sum_{\mathbf{o}} P(\mathbf{o}) \log_2 P(\mathbf{o})\]

High \(H_{\text{exp}}\) means varied, unpredictable experiences. Low
\(H_{\text{exp}}\) means repetitive, predictable experiences.

\subsection{6.1 The Experience Integral}\label{the-experience-integral}

Over continuous time, experience accumulates:

\[I_{\text{exp}}(T) = \int_0^T \dot{I}(t) \, dt\]

where \(\dot{I}(t)\) is the instantaneous information rate (bits per
tick).

\subsection{6.2 Experience Compression}\label{experience-compression}

Not all observations are retained. The \textbf{compression ratio} is:

\[\eta = \frac{I_{\text{stored}}}{I_{\text{received}}}\]

Human memory has \(\eta \ll 1\): most experience is discarded, only
patterns retained.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{7. The Prior-Experience
Decomposition}\label{the-prior-experience-decomposition}

\subsection{Theorem 7.1 (Total Knowledge
Decomposition)}\label{theorem-7.1-total-knowledge-decomposition}

An sLoop's total knowledge \(K\) decomposes as:

\[K = K_{\text{prior}} + K_{\text{exp}} - K_{\text{forgotten}}\]

where: - \(K_{\text{prior}}\) = innate + inherited knowledge -
\(K_{\text{exp}}\) = accumulated experiential information -
\(K_{\text{forgotten}}\) = information lost to decay/overwriting

\subsection{Definition 7.1 (Knowledge
State)}\label{definition-7.1-knowledge-state}

The knowledge state at time \(t\) is the posterior distribution:

\[\kappa_t(c) = P(c | o_1, \ldots, o_t, \pi_0)\]

This encodes everything the sLoop ``knows'' about configurations.

\subsection{Definition 7.2 (Knowledge
Entropy)}\label{definition-7.2-knowledge-entropy}

\[H_{\text{knowledge}}(t) = H[\kappa_t]\]

As knowledge increases, entropy decreases (uncertainty shrinks).

\subsection{The Fundamental
Constraint}\label{the-fundamental-constraint}

\[H_{\text{knowledge}}(t) \leq H_{\text{prior}} - I_{\text{exp}}(t) + H_{\text{noise}}(t)\]

You cannot know more than your priors plus experience, minus noise.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{8. Information Flow in sLoop
Dynamics}\label{information-flow-in-sloop-dynamics}

\subsection{8.1 The Perception Channel}\label{the-perception-channel}

External configuration \(c\) → Observation \(o\) → Updated belief
\(\kappa\)

The \textbf{channel capacity} limits perception:

\[I(c; o) \leq C_{\text{perception}}\]

where \(C_{\text{perception}}\) is determined by sensory bandwidth.

\subsection{8.2 The Action Channel}\label{the-action-channel}

Intention \(\iota\) → Action \(a\) → Environmental change \(\Delta c\)

The \textbf{action bandwidth} limits influence:

\[I(\iota; \Delta c) \leq C_{\text{action}}\]

\subsection{8.3 The Reflection Channel
(sLoop-specific)}\label{the-reflection-channel-sloop-specific}

Self-model \(\sigma\) → Self-observation \(o_\sigma\) → Updated
self-model \(\sigma'\)

The \textbf{reflection bandwidth}:

\[I(\sigma; o_\sigma) \leq C_{\text{reflection}}\]

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{The sLoop Bottleneck}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Consciousness is limited by reflection bandwidth. We cannot fully know
ourselves because \(I_\sigma < I_\Omega\): the self-model is always a
compression of the substrate.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{9. Quantitative Measures for
Consciousness}\label{quantitative-measures-for-consciousness}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Definition 9.1 (Integrated Information -
\(\Phi\))}{Definition 9.1 (Integrated Information - \textbackslash Phi)}}\label{definition-9.1-integrated-information---phi}

Following Tononi's IIT, we define:

\[\Phi(\Omega) = \min_{\text{partitions } P} I(\Omega) - \sum_{p \in P} I(p)\]

The information lost by any partition. High \(\Phi\) → integrated
consciousness.

\subsection{Definition 9.2 (sLoop Depth
Information)}\label{definition-9.2-sloop-depth-information}

The information content of nested self-reference:

\[I_d = \sum_{k=1}^{d} I(\sigma^k(\Omega); \sigma^{k-1}(\Omega))\]

where \(\sigma^k\) is \(k\)-fold self-modeling (thinking about thinking
about\ldots).

\subsection{Definition 9.3 (Experiential
Richness)}\label{definition-9.3-experiential-richness}

\[R = H_{\text{exp}} \times I_{\text{exp}} \times \Phi\]

The product of variety, quantity, and integration of experience.

\subsection{Definition 9.4 (Wisdom)}\label{definition-9.4-wisdom}

\[W = \frac{K_{\text{compressed}}}{I_{\text{exp}}}\]

How much reusable knowledge per bit of experience. High \(W\) →
efficient learning.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{11. Biological Sensory
Channels}\label{biological-sensory-channels}

The biological sLoop receives information through specialized
transduction pathways. Each sense converts physical configurations into
neural patterns.

\subsection{11.1 The Transduction
Hierarchy}\label{the-transduction-hierarchy}

The path from environment to memory proceeds through distinct stages:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Environment} (configuration c) →
\item
  \textbf{Physical Signal} → (transduction)
\item
  \textbf{Receptor Activation} → (encoding)
\item
  \textbf{Neural Spike Train} → (integration)
\item
  \textbf{Cortical Pattern} → (binding)
\item
  \textbf{Conscious Percept} → (storage)
\item
  \textbf{Memory Trace}
\end{enumerate}

At each stage, information is transformed and compressed.

\subsection{11.2 Sensory Channel
Capacities}\label{sensory-channel-capacities}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{The Consciousness Bottleneck}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Only 1 in 100,000 bits of sensory input reaches conscious awareness. The
vast majority is processed unconsciously, filtered, and discarded.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{11.3 Transduction as
Through-Pattern}\label{transduction-as-through-pattern}

Each receptor type implements a specific through-pattern:

\[\tau_{\text{sense}}: \mathcal{C}_{\text{physical}} \to \mathcal{C}_{\text{neural}}\]

\textbf{Vision} (photoreceptors):
\[\tau_{\text{vis}}(\gamma) = \sigma(\int \gamma(\lambda) S(\lambda) d\lambda)\]
where \(\gamma(\lambda)\) is photon flux, \(S(\lambda)\) is spectral
sensitivity, and \(\sigma\) is neural nonlinearity.

\textbf{Audition} (hair cells):
\[\tau_{\text{aud}}(p) = \sigma(\mathcal{F}[p(t)])\] where \(p(t)\) is
pressure wave and \(\mathcal{F}\) is cochlear Fourier transform.

\textbf{Touch} (mechanoreceptors):
\[\tau_{\text{touch}}(\mathbf{F}) = \sigma(\nabla \cdot \mathbf{F})\]
where \(\mathbf{F}\) is force field on skin surface.

\subsection{11.4 The Sensory Funnel}\label{the-sensory-funnel}

Information flow narrows as it approaches consciousness:

\textbf{The Sensory Funnel}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Layer & Stage & Capacity & Bottleneck \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
0 & Physical World & \textasciitilde10²² bits & receptor limits \\
1 & Receptor Activation & \textasciitilde10⁷ bits/sec & lateral
inhibition \\
2 & Primary Sensory Cortex & \textasciitilde10⁶ bits/sec & feature
extraction \\
3 & Association Cortex & \textasciitilde10⁵ bits/sec & attention
filter \\
4 & Working Memory & \textasciitilde10² bits/sec & consolidation \\
5 & Long-Term Memory & \textasciitilde10¹ bits/sec stored & --- \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{11.5 Sensory Information
Formulas}\label{sensory-information-formulas}

\textbf{Definition 11.1 (Sensory Mutual Information)}

The information a sense \(S\) provides about configuration \(c\):

\[I_S = I(o_S; c) = H[o_S] - H[o_S | c]\]

\textbf{Definition 11.2 (Sensory Efficiency)}

How much of the available information is captured:

\[\eta_S = \frac{I(o_S; c)}{H[c_{\text{accessible}}]}\]

\textbf{Definition 11.3 (Cross-Modal Integration)}

Information gained by combining senses:

\[I_{\text{multi}} = I(o_1, o_2, \ldots, o_n; c) - \sum_i I(o_i; c)\]

Positive \(I_{\text{multi}}\) indicates synergy (senses reinforce each
other). Negative indicates redundancy (senses overlap).

\subsection{11.6 Interoception: The Internal
Senses}\label{interoception-the-internal-senses}

Internal body states provide continuous background information:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
System & What It Senses & Information Rate \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Cardiovascular & Heart rate, blood pressure & \textasciitilde100
bits/sec \\
Respiratory & Breath rate, O₂/CO₂ levels & \textasciitilde50 bits/sec \\
Digestive & Hunger, satiety, nausea & \textasciitilde10 bits/sec \\
Thermal & Core temperature, skin temp & \textasciitilde20 bits/sec \\
Vestibular & Balance, acceleration & \textasciitilde100 bits/sec \\
Nociceptive & Pain, tissue damage & \textasciitilde10³ bits/sec
(variable) \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Total interoceptive input}: \textasciitilde10⁵ bits/sec
\textbf{Conscious access}: \textasciitilde0.1 bits/sec

Interoception is mostly unconscious: only extreme states (pain, hunger,
dizziness) break through to awareness.

\subsection{11.7 The Binding Problem}\label{the-binding-problem}

Sensory inputs arrive as separate streams. How does the sLoop unify
them?

\textbf{Definition 11.4 (Binding Information)}

The information required to specify which features belong together:

\[I_{\text{bind}} = H[\text{all features}] - H[\text{correct bindings}]\]

For \(n\) features with \(k\) objects:
\(I_{\text{bind}} \approx n \log_2 k\) bits.

\textbf{Proposed FTD Solution}: Binding occurs via \textbf{temporal
synchrony} in flux patterns. Features processed by the same sLoop region
at the same tick are bound together.

\[\text{Bound}(f_1, f_2) \iff \exists \Omega: f_1, f_2 \in \phi_\Omega(c) \text{ simultaneously}\]

\subsection{11.8 Attention as Information
Gating}\label{attention-as-information-gating}

Attention selects which sensory channels reach consciousness.

\textbf{Definition 11.5 (Attentional Gain)}

\[G_A(S) = \frac{I_S^{\text{attended}}}{I_S^{\text{unattended}}}\]

Typical values: \(G_A \approx 3\)-\(10\) for attended vs unattended
stimuli.

\textbf{Attentional Capacity}:

\[\sum_S G_A(S) \cdot I_S \leq C_{\text{conscious}} \approx 50 \text{ bits/sec}\]

Attending to one channel reduces capacity for others: attention is a
\textbf{zero-sum game}.

\subsection{11.9 Sensory Memory Stages}\label{sensory-memory-stages}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Stage & Duration & Capacity & Compression \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Iconic (visual) & \textasciitilde250 ms & \textasciitilde10⁶ bits &
1:1 \\
Echoic (auditory) & \textasciitilde2 sec & \textasciitilde10⁵ bits &
1:1 \\
Working memory & \textasciitilde20 sec & \textasciitilde100 bits &
10⁻⁴ \\
Short-term & \textasciitilde hours & \textasciitilde10³ bits & 10⁻³ \\
Long-term & \textasciitilde years & \textasciitilde10⁹ bits & 10⁻⁶ \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{The Great Forgetting}: From sensory input to long-term memory,
compression ratio is \textasciitilde10⁻⁶. Only patterns survive.

\subsection{11.10 Biological Prior
Structure}\label{biological-prior-structure}

The sLoop's priors are shaped by:

\textbf{Genetic priors} (innate): - Edge detection (V1 orientation
columns) - Face recognition (fusiform gyrus) - Language acquisition
(Broca's/Wernicke's areas) - Threat detection (amygdala)

\textbf{Developmental priors} (critical periods): - Visual acuity (first
6 months) - Phoneme discrimination (first year) - Language syntax (first
7 years) - Social cognition (adolescence)

\textbf{Learned priors} (experience): - Updated via Bayesian inference -
Consolidated during sleep - Subject to interference and decay

\[\pi_{\text{total}} = \pi_{\text{genetic}} \cdot \pi_{\text{developmental}} \cdot \pi_{\text{learned}}\]

\section{Transition}\label{transition-46}

Information quantification provides the metrics for consciousness. We
can now measure priors (what an sLoop expects), experience (what it has
observed), and knowledge (what it believes). Biological sensory channels
show the massive compression (\textasciitilde10⁻⁵) from raw input to
conscious awareness: the sLoop samples reality through a narrow straw.
This completes the formal apparatus for treating consciousness as a
physical phenomenon within FTD: a self-referential information structure
constrained by channel capacities, shaped by genetic and developmental
priors, and growing through Bayesian update from filtered sensory
experience.

\chapter{Number Theory Foundations}\label{sec-number-theory}

\emph{``The integers are not arbitrary: they are fixed points of
self-referential consistency.''}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Deep Mathematical Structure}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

The framework integers \{3, 4, 7, 13\} are not parameters: they are
\textbf{structural necessities} arising from self-referential, bounded,
consistent systems. These integers appear across disparate areas of pure
mathematics in a way that appears collectively significant, though
correlations between appearances reduce naive independence estimates.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{The Framework Integers}\label{the-framework-integers}

\subsection{Definition and Roles}\label{definition-and-roles}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Symbol & Value & Physical Role & Mathematical Role \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}

\section{The Tightened Derivation
Chain}\label{the-tightened-derivation-chain}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Step 1: \(N_{\text{eff}} = 13\) from
Dimensional
Crossover}{Step 1: N\_\{\textbackslash text\{eff\}\} = 13 from Dimensional Crossover}}\label{step-1-n_texteff-13-from-dimensional-crossover}

\textbf{Principle:} The effective degrees of freedom must encode both
the 2D fiber structure (Fibonacci/elliptic) and the 3D spatial structure
(Tribonacci/cubic).

\textbf{Mathematical Result:} \(F_7 = T_7 = 13\) is the \textbf{unique}
crossover point where both sequences meet (excluding trivial values).

\textbf{Verification:} - Fibonacci: 0, 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, \textbf{13},
21, 34\ldots{} - Tribonacci: 0, 0, 1, 1, 2, 4, 7, \textbf{13}, 24,
44\ldots{}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Theorem NTHR-4}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

\(F_7 = T_7 = 13\) is the unique non-trivial crossover point of the
Fibonacci and Tribonacci sequences. Therefore \(N_{\text{eff}} = 13\) is
\textbf{derived}, not selected.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Step 2: \(b_3 = 7\) from Consecutive
Tribonacci}{Step 2: b\_3 = 7 from Consecutive Tribonacci}}\label{step-2-b_3-7-from-consecutive-tribonacci}

\textbf{Principle:} The topological parameter \(b_3\) and effective
parameter \(N_{\text{eff}}\) must be consecutive Tribonacci numbers
(encoding 3D structure).

\textbf{Mathematical Result:} Since \(N_{\text{eff}} = T_7 = 13\), we
have \(b_3 = T_6 = 7\).

\textbf{Additional Confirmations:} - \(b_3 = 7\) is the 4th Heegner
number - \(B_6 = 1/42\) where \(42 = 2 \times 3 \times 7\) requires
\(b_3 = 7\) - \(\tau(3) = 252 = 4 \times 9 \times 7\) requires
\(b_3 = 7\)

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Step 3: \(N_{\text{base}} = 4\) from Lucas +
Lattice
Constraint}{Step 3: N\_\{\textbackslash text\{base\}\} = 4 from Lucas + Lattice Constraint}}\label{step-3-n_textbase-4-from-lucas-lattice-constraint}

\textbf{Principle:} \(N_{\text{base}}\) and \(b_3\) must be consecutive
Lucas numbers, AND \(N_{\text{base}}^2\) must equal the lattice DoF
coefficient (16).

\textbf{Mathematical Result:} \(L_3 = 4\), \(L_4 = 7\), and
\(4^2 = 16\).

\textbf{Uniqueness:} 4 is the \textbf{only} Lucas number (besides 1)
that is a perfect square.

\textbf{Physical Grounding:} - 4 = vertices of tetrahedron (minimal
3-simplex) - 16 = physical DoF on \(2 \times 2 \times 2\) lattice - 4 =
\(L_3\) where \(L_4 = b_3 = 7\)

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Step 4: \(j = 1728\) as Derived
Consequence}{Step 4: j = 1728 as Derived Consequence}}\label{step-4-j-1728-as-derived-consequence}

\textbf{Key Result:}

\[\boxed{j = (N_{\text{base}} \times N_c)^3 = (4 \times 3)^3 = 12^3 = 1728}\]

This means \textbf{\(j = 1728\) is now DERIVED} from the integer values,
not independently selected.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{\faExclamation}\hspace{0.5em}{Theorem NTHR-1}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

Given \(N_{\text{base}} = 4\) and \(N_c = 3\) (from the master
quadratic), the j-invariant is uniquely determined as
\(j = (N_{\text{base}} \times N_c)^3 = 1728\).

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Step 5: Self-Referential
Closure}\label{step-5-self-referential-closure}

A remarkable feature: \textbf{the crossover occurs at index \(b_3\)}.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(F_{\mathbf{7}} = T_{\mathbf{7}} = 13\)
\item
  The index 7 = \(b_3\) = \(N_{\text{base}} + N_c\)
\end{itemize}

This is self-referential: 1. \(b_3\) determines the index where
Fibonacci meets Tribonacci 2. The value at that index is
\(N_{\text{eff}}\) 3. \(b_3\) itself is \(T_6\) (one before the
crossover)

\textbf{The integers determine each other through interlocking
constraints.}

\section{Number Theory Connections}\label{number-theory-connections}

\subsection{Moonshine and Monster
Group}\label{moonshine-and-monster-group}

The j-invariant \(j = 1728 = (N_{\text{base}} \times N_c)^3 = 12^3\) is
the unique modular function for SL(2,ℤ). Its value for the lemniscate
curve (\(\tau = i\)) equals exactly \((4 \times 3)^3\).

Additional decompositions: - \(1728 = 4 \times 432\) (Vedic sacred
numbers) - \(1728 = 2^6 \times 3^3\) - \(1728 = 42 \times 41 + 6\)
(connecting to 42 nexus)

\subsection{Ramanujan Tau Function}\label{ramanujan-tau-function}

\[\tau(3) = 252 = N_{\text{base}} \times N_c^2 \times b_3 = 4 \times 9 \times 7\]

The Ramanujan tau function, coefficient of \(q^n\) in the modular
discriminant \(\Delta(q)\), has \(\tau(3) = 252\), which exactly equals
the product of framework integers.

Related: \(E_6\) coefficient \(504 = 2 \times \tau(3) = 2 \times 252\)

\subsection{Heegner Numbers and 42}\label{heegner-numbers-and-42}

\textbf{First four Heegner products:}

\[1 \times 2 \times 3 \times 7 = 42 = 2 \times N_c \times b_3\]

Heegner numbers are the unique \(d\) where \(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-d})\) has
class number 1. Their product equals the FTD bridge constant.

\textbf{The 42 Nexus} appears through multiple independent routes:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Source & Expression & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Heegner product & \(1 \times 2 \times 3 \times 7\) & 42 \\
FTD integers & \(2 \times N_c \times b_3\) & 42 \\
Catalan number & \(C_5\) & 42 \\
Bernoulli denominator & \(1/B_6\) & 42 \\
j-invariant residue & \(1728 = 42 \times 41 + 6\) & - \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Hardy-Ramanujan Taxicab
Number}\label{hardy-ramanujan-taxicab-number}

\[1729 = 7 \times 13 \times 19 = b_3 \times N_{\text{eff}} \times (\text{6th Heegner})\]

The famous taxicab number (smallest number expressible as sum of two
cubes in two ways) is the product of FTD integers times the 6th Heegner
number.

Note: \(1729 = 1728 + 1 = j + 1\)

\subsection{The Sequence Reference}\label{the-sequence-reference}

\textbf{Tribonacci:} 0, 0, 1, 1, 2, 4, \textbf{7}, \textbf{13}, 24, 44,
81\ldots{} - \(T_6 = 7 = b_3\) - \(T_7 = 13 = N_{\text{eff}}\) -
\(T_8 = 24 = \eta\) exponent

\textbf{Fibonacci:} 0, 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, \textbf{13}, 21, 34\ldots{} -
\(F_7 = 13 = N_{\text{eff}}\) (crossover with Tribonacci)

\textbf{Lucas:} 2, 1, 3, \textbf{4}, \textbf{7}, 11, 18, 29\ldots{} -
\(L_3 = 4 = N_{\text{base}}\) - \(L_4 = 7 = b_3\) - \(L_5 = 11\) (5th
Heegner)

\textbf{Heegner:} 1, 2, 3, \textbf{7}, 11, 19, 43, 67, 163 - Product of
first 4 = 42 - \(7 = b_3\) - 19 appears in
\(1729 = b_3 \times N_{\text{eff}} \times 19\)

\section{The Riemann Zeta Connection}\label{the-riemann-zeta-connection}

\subsection{The First Zero Formula}\label{the-first-zero-formula}

The first non-trivial zero of the Riemann zeta function at
\(s = 1/2 + it_1\) satisfies:

\[t_1 = \frac{N_c^2}{2}\pi - \frac{1}{N_c \cdot \alpha^{-1}}\]

\textbf{Numerical verification:}

Component & Value & Meaning \\

\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot

\((9/2)\pi\) & 14.1372 & Main term \\
\(1/(N_c \times \alpha^{-1})\) & 0.00243 & EM-color correction \\
\textbf{Predicted} & 14.1347344903 & - \\
\textbf{Actual} & 14.1347251417 & - \\
\textbf{Error} & \textbf{0.66 ppm} & - \\
\end{longtable}

This precision is comparable to the TRD base derivation of \(\alpha\)
(1.26 ppm); the precision formula achieves \textbf{0.21 ppt}.

\subsection{The Prime Counting
Connection}\label{the-prime-counting-connection}

The prime counting function at 42 equals \(N_{\text{eff}}\):

\[\pi(42) = 13 = N_{\text{eff}}\]

where 42 = \(2 \times N_c \times b_3\) = \(2 \times 3 \times 7\).

\textbf{The 42-Chain:}

Iterating the prime counting function:
\[42 \to 13 \to 6 \to 3 \to 2 \to 1\]

This chain passes through two TRD integers: - 13 = \(N_{\text{eff}}\) -
3 = \(N_c\)

\subsection{The Prime Wavelength}\label{the-prime-wavelength}

The first Riemann zero determines the dominant oscillation in the prime
distribution:

\[\lambda_1 = \frac{2\pi}{t_1} \approx \frac{4}{N_c^2} = \frac{4}{9}\]

\textbf{Numerical verification:} - \(\lambda_1 = 2\pi/t_1\) =
0.4445212230 - \(4/9\) = 0.4444444444 - \textbf{Error: 0.017\%}

\textbf{Physical Interpretation:} The ``wavelength'' of prime
oscillations is approximately \(4/N_c^2\), suggesting that prime
distribution encodes color structure.

\subsection{Base Integers from Riemann
Zeros}\label{base-integers-from-riemann-zeros}

When Riemann zeros are expressed as \(t_n \approx (a_n/2)\pi\), the base
integers include TRD-related values:

modes) - 16 = \(N_{\text{base}}^2 = k_{\text{phys}}\) (lattice DoF)

\textbf{All three are fundamental TRD constants.}

\section{Mathematical Anomalies
Explained}\label{mathematical-anomalies-explained}

\subsection{Why 24 Appears Everywhere}\label{why-24-appears-everywhere}

\textbf{The Anomaly:} The number 24 appears ubiquitously: - Modular
discriminant: \(\Delta = \eta(\tau)^{24}\) - Leech lattice:
24-dimensional - String theory: Critical dimension \(D = 26 = 24 + 2\) -
Sporadic groups: 24 divides many orders - Ramanujan tau:
\(\tau(2) = -24\)

\textbf{FTD Explanation:}

\[24 = N_{\text{base}} + b_3 + N_{\text{eff}} = 4 + 7 + 13\]

Also: \(24 = T_8\) (next Tribonacci) = \(N_{\text{base}}! = 4!\)

The ubiquity of 24 reflects it being the \textbf{total framework
content}: base geometry + topological structure + effective modes.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(e^\pi - \pi \approx 20\)}{e\^{}\textbackslash pi - \textbackslash pi \textbackslash approx 20}}\label{epi---pi-approx-20}

\textbf{The Anomaly:} \(e^\pi - \pi = 19.999099...\) is mysteriously
close to exactly 20.

\textbf{FTD Explanation:}

\[e^\pi - \pi \approx b_3 + N_{\text{eff}} = 7 + 13 = 20\]

Relative error: 0.005\%

\emph{Interpretation:} \(e^\pi\) encodes total transcendental content;
\(\pi\) encodes circular/periodic content; their difference equals the
framework's internal structure (topological + effective modes).

\subsection{The j-Function Constant
744}\label{the-j-function-constant-744}

\textbf{The Anomaly:} The j-function expansion
\(j(\tau) = 1/q + 744 + 196884q + ...\) has the ``mysterious'' constant
744.

\textbf{FTD Explanation:}

\[744 = 24 \times 31 = (N_{\text{base}} + b_3 + N_{\text{eff}}) \times (24 + b_3)\]

Additional structure: -
\(j = 1728 = 42 \times 41 + 6 = 42 \times 41 + 2N_c\) -
\(j - 744 = 984 = 24 \times 41 = 24 \times (42 - 1)\)

\subsection{Bernoulli Number
Denominators}\label{bernoulli-number-denominators}

\textbf{FTD Discovery:}

\[B_6 \text{ denominator} = 42 = 2 \times 3 \times 7 = 2 \times N_c \times b_3\]

\[B_{12} \text{ denominator} = 2730 = 2 \times 3 \times 5 \times 7 \times 13 = 2 \times N_c \times 5 \times b_3 \times N_{\text{eff}}\]

The \(B_{12}\) denominator explicitly encodes both \(b_3 = 7\) AND
\(N_{\text{eff}} = 13\)!

\subsection{Why Exactly 9 Heegner
Numbers?}\label{why-exactly-9-heegner-numbers}

\textbf{The Anomaly:} There are exactly 9 values of \(d\) where
\(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-d})\) has class number 1: \{1, 2, 3, 7, 11, 19, 43,
67, 163\}

\textbf{FTD Observation:}

\[9 = N_c^2 = 3^2\]

The count equals the number of color-anticolor pairs in QCD.

Additional structure: - The 9 Heegner numbers group into three sets of 3
- \(N_c = 3\) is the 3rd Heegner number - \(b_3 = 7\) is the 4th Heegner
number - First 4 product:
\(1 \times 2 \times 3 \times 7 = 42 = 2 \times N_c \times b_3\)

\section{Concepts}\label{concepts-47}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{fibonacci-tribonacci-crossover}: Unique point
  \(F_7 = T_7 = 13\) where both sequences meet
\item
  \textbf{heegner-numbers}: Values \(d\) where \(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-d})\)
  has class number 1
\item
  \textbf{42-nexus}: Multiple routes to 42: Heegner, Catalan, Bernoulli,
  FTD integers
\item
  \textbf{self-referential-closure}: Integers determine each other
  through interlocking constraints
\item
  \textbf{prime-wavelength}: Dominant oscillation
  \(\lambda_1 \approx 4/N_c^2\) in prime distribution
\item
  \textbf{42-chain}: Prime counting iteration
  \(42 \to 13 \to 6 \to 3 \to 2 \to 1\)
\item
  \textbf{riemann-first-zero}: \(t_1 \approx (N_c^2/2)\pi\) to 0.66 ppm
\end{itemize}

\section{Transition}\label{transition-47}

The number-theoretic foundations establish that FTD's framework integers
are not arbitrary: they are fixed points of mathematical
self-consistency. These same integers appear across modular forms,
elliptic curves, and prime distribution, suggesting that physics and
mathematics share a common structural foundation.

The next appendix provides the complete experimental predictions catalog
with falsification criteria.

\section{The First Test: Cloud-9}\label{sec-cloud9}

In January 2025, astronomers announced the discovery of Cloud-9, a
``ghost galaxy'' in the M94 field. This discovery represents the
\textbf{first observation consistent with predictions} derived from the
FTD framework.

\subsection{What This Means}\label{what-this-means}

Cloud-9 is what FTD calls \textbf{the void made visible}: a region where
flux exists but has never crossed the manifestation threshold to become
stars.

In FTD terms: - \textbf{State s = 0 everywhere} (no manifested voxels) -
\textbf{Flux \(J \neq 0\)} (dark matter halo present) - \textbf{Below
\(\kappa_B\) threshold} (no genesis has occurred)

This is direct evidence that: 1. Unmanifested structures can exist
stably 2. They have the geometry FTD predicts (spherical, non-rotating)
3. There is a threshold for manifestation

\section{Other Confirmed Predictions}\label{sec-other-confirmed}

\subsection{No Fourth Generation}\label{no-fourth-generation}

\textbf{Status:} {[}CONSISTENT{]}

The G* framework predicts:
\[N_{\text{gen}} = \lfloor x_- \rfloor = \lfloor 3.024 \rfloor = 3\]

Current LHC bounds exclude fourth-generation quarks up to
\textasciitilde1 TeV. This is \textbf{consistent} with FTD but does not
uniquely support it (many theories predict 3 generations).

\subsection{Tensor-Only Gravitational
Waves}\label{tensor-only-gravitational-waves}

\textbf{Status:} {[}CONFIRMED{]}

FTD predicts gravitational waves have exactly 2 tensor polarizations (+
and ×), derived from the two transverse flux components J\_x and J\_y.

LIGO/Virgo observations show no evidence for scalar or vector modes,
\textbf{confirming} the tensor-only prediction.

\subsection{The Migdal Effect}\label{the-migdal-effect}

\textbf{Status:} {[}CONSISTENT{]}

In January 2026, Nature published the first direct observation of the
\textbf{Migdal effect}: electron ejection from atoms experiencing sudden
acceleration during neutral particle collisions (Yi et al. 2026).

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Observation & Value & Significance \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot

Mechanism & Neutron-nucleus collision & Neutral projectile \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{FTD Interpretation:}

The Migdal effect has deep significance for the manifestation dynamics
framework:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Flux Perturbation Mechanism}: The ``instantaneous
  acceleration'' of the nucleus creates a sharp gradient \(\nabla J\) in
  the flux field. This perturbation propagates to the electron shell
  faster than the electrons can adiabatically adjust.
\item
  \textbf{Threshold Crossing at Distance}: The electron ejection occurs
  not at the collision site but in the electron shell, demonstrating
  that flux perturbations can trigger manifestation transitions (here:
  bound → free) at locations distant from the primary interaction.
\item
  \textbf{Dark Matter Detection Enhancement}: This validates the
  mechanism by which sub-threshold dark matter interactions could
  produce \emph{detectable} signatures. In FTD terms: dark matter
  (\(|J| < \kappa_B\)) collides with nucleus, creates flux gradient,
  gradient propagates to electron shell, electron crosses threshold.
\item
  \textbf{sLoop Coupling}: The detection apparatus (6 events from
  \(10^6\)) is itself a manifested structure coupling to the flux field,
  precisely the observer-substrate relationship FTD predicts for
  measurement.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Epistemic Note}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

\subsection{Fine Structure Constant}\label{fine-structure-constant-1}

\textbf{Status:} {[}CONSISTENT TO 0.21 PPT{]}

The master quadratic with precision formula yields:
\[\frac{1}{\alpha} = x_+ - \frac{9}{47}|\varepsilon| + \frac{5}{64}|\varepsilon|^2 = 137.035999...\]

where \(\varepsilon = e^\pi - \pi - 20 \approx -9 \times 10^{-4}\).

CODATA 2022 (Tiesinga et al. 2025): \(1/\alpha = 137.035999177(21)\)

Agreement to \textbf{0.21 ppt} (parts per trillion): the most precise
theoretical derivation of α ever achieved. This is 6,000× better than
the base 1.26 ppm derivation and depends on the selection principles
(S1, S2, S3), which are argued rather than proven.

\textbf{Why Baryons Matter:}

CP violation had been observed in mesons (K, B, D) but never in baryons
(the three-quark structures that constitute visible matter: protons,
neutrons). This observation confirms that CP asymmetry operates in the
same structures FTD models as ``triads.''

\textbf{FTD Interpretation:}

FTD derives CP violation from the framework integers:

\[\delta_{\text{CP}} = \arctan\left(\frac{7}{3}\right) \approx 66.8°\]

The observation of CP violation in baryons with
\(A_{\text{CP}} \approx 2.45\%\) is \textbf{consistent} with FTD's
prediction that:

  CP violation arises from the discrete structure of triads (3-quark
  bound states)
\item
  The CKM phase is determined by integer ratios (7/3)
\item
  Baryogenesis requires CP violation in baryonic matter, now confirmed
  to exist
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Electron Electric Dipole Moment: CP Violation
Constraints}\label{sec-eedm}

\textbf{Status:} {[}CONSISTENT{]}

In July 2023, the JILA laboratory at the University of Colorado
announced the most precise measurement ever of the electron's electric
dipole moment (eEDM) (Roussy et al. 2023):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3939}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2121}}

\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Improvement
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Upper limit & \(|d_e| < 4.1 \times 10^{-30}\) e cm & 2.4x better than
previous \\
Method & Trapped \(\text{HfF}^+\) molecular ions & 300 hours
measurement \\
Constraint & New physics above \textasciitilde{}\(10^{13}\) GeV &
Supersymmetry strongly constrained \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Why the eEDM Matters:}

A non-zero eEDM would indicate CP violation beyond the Standard Model.
The measurement probes physics at energy scales far beyond any collider:

\begin{verbatim}
eEDM SENSITIVITY VS. ACCELERATORS:
  LHC probes:          ~10^4 GeV
  eEDM (2025) probes:  ~10^13 GeV

  eEDM is ~10^9x more sensitive to BSM physics
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{FTD Interpretation:}

FTD derives CP violation from the discrete integer structure:

\[\delta_{\text{CP}} = \arctan\left(\frac{7}{3}\right) \approx 66.8°\]

This phase manifests in: 1. \textbf{CKM matrix}: quark flavor mixing
(2.1\% accuracy) 2. \textbf{PMNS matrix}: neutrino mixing 3.
\textbf{Baryogenesis}: matter-antimatter asymmetry

However, the electron is a \textbf{fundamental manifestation} in FTD,
not a composite structure. CP-violating phases arise in: - Transitions
between manifestation states (weak decay) - Composite structures where
phase accumulates (quarks, baryons) - Flavor mixing matrices
(multi-generation structure)

For a pointlike electron: - No internal structure to carry phase - CP
violation enters only via loop corrections - Predicted \(d_e\) is
extremely small

violation in triads) - \textbf{Detection physics validated} (Migdal

satisfied} (JILA eEDM: no BSM CP violation in fundamental particles)

That seven discoveries across cosmology, particle physics, quantum
foundations, condensed matter, and precision tests are all consistent
with FTD is collectively striking. However, these are not fully
independent (they involve the same framework), so naive probability
estimates overstate the significance.

This does not ``prove'' FTD, but it transforms the framework from
speculative to \textbf{empirically grounded}.

\section{Active Investigations: Unresolved
Puzzles}\label{sec-active-investigations}

Two long-standing precision physics puzzles remain unresolved. FTD
offers potential interpretive frameworks for both, pending experimental
resolution.

\subsection{The Neutron Lifetime Puzzle}\label{sec-neutron-lifetime}

\textbf{Status:} {[}OPEN - UNDER INVESTIGATION{]}

Free neutrons decay via the weak force with a lifetime of approximately
880 seconds. However, two measurement methods yield persistently
discrepant results (Particle Data Group 2024b):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2750}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2750}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Method
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Lifetime
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Precision
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Technique
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Bottle & 877.8 ± 0.3 s (Gonzalez et al. 2021) & High & Trap neutrons,
count survivors \\
Beam & 888.1 ± 2.0 s (Yue et al. 2013) & Lower & Count decay protons in
flight \\
\textbf{Discrepancy} & \textbf{\textasciitilde10 seconds} &
\textbf{\textasciitilde{}\(5\sigma\)} & Unexplained \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Current Status (2025):}

In September 2025, representatives from all operating neutron lifetime
experiments convened at PSI to address this puzzle. New results from
LANL, NIST, J-PARC, and PSI are expected in coming years. J-PARC's
electron-detection method (Hirota et al. 2020) (distinct from
proton-detection beam experiments) shows consistency with bottle results
but \(2.3\sigma\) tension with proton-based beam measurements.

Proposed explanations include: - Exotic decay channels (mirror
neutrons), largely excluded - Excited neutron states with different
lifetimes - Multiple elastic collision effects in bottles - Systematic
measurement artifacts

\textbf{FTD Interpretation (Speculative):}

FTD predicts \(\tau_n \approx 880\) s (Ch. 12 in v2; formerly Chapter
2.7), falling between the two measured values. The discrepancy may arise
from \textbf{measurement-context-dependent sLoop coupling}:

\begin{verbatim}
BOTTLE METHOD:
  Neutron ← strong sLoop coupling → Container walls
  Local flux concentration enhanced
  Effective decay rate: slightly higher

BEAM METHOD:
  Neutron ← weak sLoop coupling → Vacuum
  Lower environmental flux density
  Effective decay rate: slightly lower
\end{verbatim}

In FTD, the weak decay rate depends on flux field dynamics at the triad
(neutron = udd configuration). If the \textbf{effective phase space
factor f} varies with environmental flux density, different measurement
contexts would yield different lifetimes, not due to exotic physics, but
due to context-dependent substrate coupling.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Epistemic Status}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]

This interpretation is \textbf{speculative}. FTD does not yet provide a
quantitative calculation of the expected discrepancy. The puzzle may be
resolved by improved experimental systematics rather than new physics.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{The Proton Radius Puzzle}\label{sec-proton-radius}

\textbf{Status:} {[}OPEN - UNDER INVESTIGATION{]}

The proton's charge radius measured differently depending on the probe
particle (Pohl et al. 2010; Beyer et al. 2017):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Method & Radius & Probe \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Muonic hydrogen & \textasciitilde0.84 fm (Pohl et al. 2010) & Muon (m =
105.7 MeV) \\
Electronic hydrogen & \textasciitilde0.88 fm & Electron (m = 0.511
MeV) \\
\textbf{Discrepancy} & \textbf{\textasciitilde4\%} & Historically
\(> 7\sigma\) \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Current Status (2025):}

The MUSE (MUon proton Scattering Experiment) at PSI (Gilman et al. 2017;
Strauch 2025) is designed to resolve this puzzle by measuring electron
and muon scattering \textbf{simultaneously} under identical kinematic
conditions. The experiment uses:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Mixed lepton beams (electrons, muons, pions)
\item
  GEM detectors and wire chambers for tracking
\item
  Scintillators for particle identification
\item
  Both polarities (particles and antiparticles)
\end{itemize}

With 2025 beam time, MUSE approaches its statistics goals for definitive
measurements.

\textbf{MUSE Analysis Timeline:}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Phase & Milestone & Status & Target \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
1.1 & Scintillator/calorimeter response vs MC & Blinded & 2026 \\
1.2 & Track reconstruction (GEM-STT, vertices) & Blinded & 2026 \\
1.3 & Physics model, geometry, beam verification & Blinded & 2026 \\
2 & Left/right, up/down independence verification & Blinded & 2027 \\
3 & Analysis procedure independence & Blinded & 2027 \\
4 & Pion scattering cross sections & π-data unblinded & 2027+ \\
5 & Muon decay distributions & μ-data unblinded & 2027+ \\
\end{longtable}

The blinded analysis phases (2026-2027) ensure systematic uncertainties
are fully characterized before unblinding physics results.

Recent results (2019-2022) favor the smaller muonic value (Xiong et al.
2019), but the puzzle is not universally considered resolved. Possible
explanations include: - QED calculation corrections - Two-photon
exchange effects - Proton structure beyond simple form factors - New
physics coupling differently to electrons and muons

\textbf{FTD Interpretation (Speculative):}

In FTD, the proton is a \textbf{triad} (uud) with characteristic flux
distribution. The measured ``radius'' is not a fixed property but the
\textbf{scale at which probes couple to the flux density}:

\begin{verbatim}
PROTON FLUX DISTRIBUTION:
     ρ(r) = ρ_core × exp(-r/r_core) + ρ_halo × exp(-r/r_halo)
                     ↑                        ↑
              Concentrated core         Diffuse outer region
\end{verbatim}

Different probes have different de Broglie wavelengths:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2258}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1935}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1935}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3871}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Probe
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Mass
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
λ\_dB
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Couples to
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Electron & 0.511 MeV & \textasciitilde{}\(10^{-12}\) m & Averages over
larger region (\(r_{\text{halo}}\)) \\
Muon & 105.7 MeV & \textasciitilde{}\(10^{-14}\) m & Probes smaller core
(\(r_{\text{core}}\)) \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{FTD Prediction:} The proton ``radius'' is \textbf{intrinsically
probe-dependent}. Both measurements are correct for their respective
probe wavelengths. The discrepancy is not a puzzle to be resolved but a
\textbf{feature of composite structure}.

If confirmed, this would mean: - No single ``proton radius'' exists -
Different probes see different effective sizes - The flux distribution
has multi-scale structure

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, coltitle=black, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, bottomtitle=1mm, opacityback=0, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Epistemic Status}, leftrule=.75mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, opacitybacktitle=0.6, colback=white, arc=.35mm, breakable, toptitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, titlerule=0mm, rightrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm]
